# Fifty Collisions: All Discussions

- **01. Precedent** (mild, 3/7) — A patient-side appeals agent backed by a Shepard's-style citator of insurance case law.
- **02. Hand Limit** (bold, 5/6) — A roguelite deckbuilder where your deck is an agent's toolset, your hand is its context window, and sponsors pay you to break their tools.
- **03. Litmus Ghost** (bold, 6/5) — Theories of consciousness as fuzzable, versioned software: build the hollow case, log the bullet, find where theories split.
- **04. Rule of Thumb** (mild, 3/7) — A Chess960 coach that treats principles as hypotheses, mined from human games and graded by your rating.
- **05. Raid Night** (bold, 5/5) — Every distribution transformer is a raid guild: neighbours commit charge windows, earn DKP for yielding headroom, and keep the boss's load bar out of the red.
- **06. Seedless** (bold, 5/6) — A roguelite that seals its prediction of your next move before you make it: be unreadable where it traps you, readable where it rewards you, and open the receipts at the end.
- **07. Recount** (mild, 3/6) — Risk-limiting audits for rock-weathering carbon: bank every core, analyze by public lottery, retest the B-samples in 2036.
- **08. Smoke-Filled Room** (bold, 4/7) — Design the auction, coach the AI cartel that breaks it, patch history's worst spectrum sales, and lose honestly to Gibbard-Satterthwaite.
- **09. Caught** (bold, 4/7) — A meditation game whose only button is noticing you've drifted, scored in breaths and refereed by your own lungs.
- **10. One Take** (bold, 6/6) — AI answers that can't be retaken: every pass@1 draw is sealed before a Bell-certified quantum pulse exists, and every private variant shows up as a public fork.
- **11. Washout** (mild, 3/7) — Randomize your own day, bet on your own effect, and watch an evidence wallet you can't fool by peeking.
- **12. Off-Diagonal** (bold, 5/6) — A weekly roguelite on the real calendar where every card costs its market price and the only edge is a correlation the rest of the field missed.
- **13. Canary Ladder** (wild, 8/4) — A cat bond on the end of elliptic-curve cryptography, whose calculation agent is a theorem and whose trigger is a forged signature.
- **14. Second Shift** (mild, 3/6) — Book the night shift for the excavator already parked on your site, supervised from home by the crewmate who rode along that day.
- **15. Yarrow** (bold, 5/7) — An oracle that diagnoses which kind of randomness your decision needs, casts it verifiably, and tells you with receipts where your gut beats chance.
- **16. Corporation Sole** (wild, 8/4) — Incorporate the agent's office, not the agent: a P&I club of self-owning AI persons that insure, survey and arrest one another.
- **17. Sengu** (bold, 6/6) — A 100-year time-capsule network where strangers carry each other's secrets as paper amulets and a memorized phrase, and rebuild them every five years.
- **18. Table Stakes** (mild, 3/7) — Futarchy for the kitchen table: vote on what better means, bet chores on what each option will do, and make the doubter own the outcome.
- **19. Hyphal Bourse** (wild, 8/4) — Read the carbon-for-phosphorus exchange rate between crops and fungi from glowing sentinel roots, and farm by bidding into that market instead of flooding it.
- **20. Facing Out** (bold, 5/6) — Chronic care as Hanabi: your warning signs face out to the people you choose, and they help only through scarce, graded clues.
- **21. Familiar** (mild, 3/6) — An adults-only AI companion you own on atproto: hosts rent the model, labelers stamp the safety, and your own history proves it's still her.
- **22. Unaccounted** (bold, 5/5) — You can't follow a cobalt atom, so audit the books: IAEA-style mass balance that catches recycled tonnes sold twice, opened by scanning your own car's battery passport.
- **23. Nightside** (wild, 8/4) — A story evolved by sleeping brains: rehearse every element, cue half of them at sleep onset, and let whatever dreamers measurably replay become canon as it passes west with the night.
- **24. Always For Sale** (bold, 5/6) — A chess roguelite under a Harberger tax: every piece is a standing quote, any attacked piece can be bought at its price and flips where it stands, and the killer move is the quiet one that makes three enemy quotes stale.
- **25. Offtaker** (mild, 3/6) — Compliance glass for German data centres with a heat-reuse quota and no buyer: the data centre owns the greenhouse, a tenant grower farms it, and every winter basket ships with a receipt for heat reused and heat dumped.
- **26. Sandbox Escape** (wild, 8/4) — A bug-bounty league for the universe's engine: pay compressors per joule saved, pay detectors only for transfer, and salt the real sky to turn "are we simulated?" into a number with error bars.
- **27. Ventris** (bold, 6/5) — Learn your grandmother's language by deciphering her street: pin hypotheses on a grid, check them with a pastry purchase, and graduate into transcribing the neighbourhood's oral history.
- **28. Tab** (mild, 3/6) — Let agents run a tab: one global limit per principal, signed approvals servers verify offline, and an approval that pays no matter what.
- **29. Order Parameter** (wild, 8/5) — Measure humanity's coupling constant: randomize how meditators are linked, fit the Kuramoto curve, and let global coherence live or die on the intercept at zero channel.
- **30. Heptad** (bold, 5/7) — Ear training for structural biology: hear solved proteins as meter, diagnose their folds by ear, and prove it on a silent test, built first for students who can't see the pictures.
- **31. Tombstone** (mild, 3/6) — Decommission a digital life like a server: map what recovers what, rehearse the death every year, and drain from the leaves inward on a certificate, never on silence.
- **32. Ascension** (bold, 5/5) — Your real month as a roguelite: pay fills a reservoir, a rule curve releases your salary, locked tranches buy jackpot tickets, and every emergency costs a Curse, never a fee.
- **33. Minute Hand** (wild, 8/4) — A ZX-calculus fusion puzzle where you solve at n = 4, your tactic is replayed at n = 256, and the score is how much cheaper your move makes breaking elliptic curves and simulating catalysts.
- **34. Sonotype** (bold, 6/5) — A two-column binder for the night chorus: catch unknown calls by playing them back against decoys, and earn bounties for verified silence.
- **35. Lookahead** (mild, 3/6) — A noise forecast for every window next to a build: the contractor buys its forecast dB-hours day-ahead, pays an imbalance penalty for every hour it underestimated, and learns to move the breaker to Thursday.
- **36. Settlement** (bold, 6/5) — Seal what you expected and what you saw, settle each insight against handicapped odds, and let a coin decide who holds the pen.
- **37. Capacity** (bold, 6/6) — A follow is a payment channel for credibility: vouching for a claim locks capacity on every hop until it resolves, and sources that are often wrong run out of reach.
- **38. Tally** (mild, 3/6) — The brass tag board on every cap lamp: crews gossip each other's positions, any lamp that reaches the portal brings the whole tally out, and a fake collapse every night proves it still works.
- **39. Bottleneck** (wild, 8/4) — A border language for agents, evolved through human players as the transmission bottleneck, where every clause must say how it knows and an unsigned claim of authority is ungrammatical.
- **40. Scratch** (bold, 5/6) — Fantasy sports for your friends' actual bodies: draft their personal lines, win when they beat them, and bank points for benching them on Thursday.
- **41. Share-Out** (mild, 3/6) — The weather oracle decides how much gets paid, the village decides who gets it, and a silent index can be appealed by pledging next year's share-out.
- **42. Graveyard Shift** (bold, 6/5) — A public coroner for low Earth orbit: players time satellite glints and listen for beacons, and two witnesses on two senses pronounce each object dead or alive.
- **43. Fountain Swap** (wild, 8/3) — Pension money buys royalty options on the cure for ageing, paid per dose and settled by a doping-style biological passport, and the market prices what a clock point is worth in survival.
- **44. Seventeen Moments** (bold, 5/7) — A deckbuilder built on the Abhidhamma: every card you play ripens later and overwrites your mind, and the kindness you spam slowly turns into its counterfeit.
- **45. Repro** (mild, 3/6) — Maintainers write their threat model as capture-the-flag, agents post a bond and must capture a flag before any human reads the report, and the finder never writes the fix.
- **46. Mound** (wild, 8/3) — A compression-only earth building with no final drawing: a resident swarm grows it toward where people are, bone-style cavities remodel it, and the city permits an invariant instead of a shape.
- **47. Piepowder** (bold, 6/5) — A court for agent commerce that clones the defendant: twelve rival agents re-enact the sealed decision, lay juries rule only where they split, and paid red-teamers fuzz the reasonable-agent standard before the harm spreads.
- **48. Taper** (mild, 3/7) — Queue middleware for fast chargers: past your car's own knee, courtesy is a rule; before it, and upstream on the corridor, yielding earns priority at your next congested stop.
- **49. Hans** (wild, 8/4) — Rules of evidence for minds heard only through a model: accredit the translator against planted ventriloquists, ask whales by offering quiet, and make 'we can't hear them' the applicant's problem.
- **50. Dry Run** (wild, 8/5) — A public beta for laws: anyone proves a draft bill's loopholes with replayable witnesses, disclosure buys legal certainty, and every kept loophole gets a recorded vote.

# Idea 01 — Precedent
> Seed: 1. [mild] **Healthcare AI × agent-to-agent negotiation × insurance.** Payers are deploying agents. Who builds the *patient's* agent that fights back?

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Precedent

**Hook:** A patient-side appeals agent whose real weapon is shared case law: every appeal anyone wins against a payer becomes a citation for the next person.

**Core mechanic:** Not "an LLM that writes your appeal letter." A payer's denial pipeline is a *classifier*, and denial-by-attrition only pays because almost nobody appeals (KFF analyses find well under 1% of denied ACA-marketplace claims get appealed). The verb is **counter-cite**. The loop:
1. Snap the denial letter or EOB, or pull it through the payer's FHIR Patient Access API.
2. The agent decodes the CARC/RARC reason codes, fetches the plan's own medical policy, and drafts the appeal.
3. It queries the **Precedent ledger**, an anonymized record of outcomes keyed by payer × CPT/drug × denial reason × policy clause: "This payer reversed 14 of 17 appeals for this code when the letter cited clause 4.2b and attached the step-therapy history."
4. It tracks deadlines and escalates to state external review automatically.
5. The outcome flows back into the ledger, so the next appeal is better armed.

**Why these domains collide productively:** Insurance denial is an asymmetric-cost game. The payer's marginal cost to deny is close to zero; the patient's is hours of misery. An agent drives the patient's cost toward zero too, and the pooled ledger turns individual grievances into something like common law. That is where the equilibrium shifts: if wrongful denials get appealed at 30% instead of 0.5%, blanket denial stops paying.

**Why now:** PrescriberPoint shipped an autonomous prior-auth agent in April 2026, and 93% of physicians report care delays from prior auth (brief §10). Investment flows to providers and payers, not patients. CMS's interoperability and prior-auth rule (CMS-0057-F) requires payer prior-auth APIs by 2027: a real machine endpoint for agent-to-agent negotiation instead of a fax line.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** The appeal-letter generator is already a crowded, cheap category. **Claimable** (AI appeals, roughly $40 each, strong on specialty drugs), **Counterforce Health** (free AI appeals), **Fight Health Insurance** (open-source, free), and **Sheer Health** all ship it. Goodbill and Resolve negotiate bills. Nobody owns the **ledger**. That's the real product.

**Correction.** CMS-0057-F's prior-auth API is provider-facing. Patients get PA *status* through the Patient Access API starting in 2027, not a negotiation endpoint. For now, "agent vs agent" means our agent against a fax machine and a portal.

**Hardest problem: the ledger starts empty and stays sparse.** Payer × code × denial reason × clause splits into millions of cells, most with n<5. Outcomes come back late or never: the patient stops answering once they get the drug. Payers will also rewrite clause 4.2b the moment it starts losing. And a winning appeal usually needs the physician's letter of medical necessity, which the patient can't produce alone.

**MVP (90 days).** One wedge: **GLP-1 and specialty-drug prior-auth denials** on commercial plans in three states. That's high volume, the denials are formulaic, and patients are motivated. Flow: upload denial → decode → policy fetch → appeal draft → a **one-click letter-of-medical-necessity packet sent to the prescriber's office** → deadline tracker → external-review filing. Outcome capture works by asking the user to forward the decision letter, with a $10 refund as the reward.

**Twist: seed the ledger from public law instead of our users.** California DMHC has published thousands of Independent Medical Review decisions since 2001 (searchable, with the rationale), and New York DFS publishes its external appeal decisions. Those are real adjudicated precedents with reviewer reasoning. Add CMS-0057-F's required public prior-auth approval/denial metrics, which payers have posted since 2026. That gives Precedent real case law on day one, with no cold start, and makes "citation" literal.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Correction accepted: our opponent is a fax machine for now. The twist changes what the ledger stores: **arguments**, not payer × code × clause cells.

**Mutation: a Shepard's citator for health insurance.** Lawyers don't search for identical cases. They check whether an *argument* is still good law. So Precedent breaks every DMHC and NY DFS decision into argument units, for example "step therapy satisfied by a prior trial under a different plan" or "BMI criterion measured at initiation, not continuation", and each unit carries a status flag: *followed*, *distinguished*, or *superseded*. Sparsity largely goes away because arguments transfer across payers and drugs where exact cells don't. Hierarchical Bayesian shrinkage (the pharmacovigilance trick for rare adverse events) estimates overturn odds for thin cells by borrowing strength from the drug class and the payer family.

**Payers rewriting clause 4.2b becomes a signal.** We crawl and version every payer medical policy. Appeals are judged against the policy in force on the date of service, so a rewrite doesn't touch pending cases. A rewrite right after a cluster of losses shows what the payer feared.

**Outcome capture:** the patient names Precedent as their **authorized representative**, which ERISA and ACA appeal rules allow. Decision letters then come to our fax inbox. That closes the loop without nagging, and the payer now faces an entity that remembers everything. The LMN packet is pre-filled from the patient's FHIR record, so the prescriber only reviews and signs.

**Emergent behaviour:** with enough citations, the ledger shows the payer's decision boundary in public. Texas's gold-card law already exempts *physicians* with ≥90% approval rates from prior auth. Precedent can make the mirror-image case to regulators: any drug × criterion cell that's overturned more than 90% of the time on review shouldn't need prior auth at all. Patients' common law becomes rulemaking evidence.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kill: the "90% overturn means drop prior auth" pitch to regulators.** Appealed cases are self-selected strong cases, so overturn rates on review overstate how often denials are wrong. Make it an annual report later, not a feature. **Also kill user-facing odds.** "82% chance to win" is a liability. Show citations and use the Bayesian scores only for internal ranking.

**The real gap: ERISA self-funded plans**, roughly 65% of covered workers. They sit outside DMHC and DFS, and their IRO decisions are unpublished, so coverage there is thin, and users are told so.

**Business model.** Free for patients. The revenue is a **citator API licensed to the provider side**: specialty pharmacies, PA teams and agents like PrescriberPoint. Pharma hub services are the eager buyer and the danger, so they're firewalled from ranking and their revenue is disclosed.

**Stack.** OCR and structured LLM extraction, Postgres with pgvector, a versioned policy crawler, a HIPAA-BAA fax API, Flexpa for FHIR, and e-signature.

**Failure modes and abuse.**
- *Hallucinated citations.* Remember Mata v. Avianca: argument units are extractive only, each linked to its source decision.
- *Ledger poisoning.* Outcomes are accepted only as payer-faxed decision letters.
- *Regulatory status.* A non-covered entity holding PHI falls under the FTC Health Breach Notification Rule and Washington's My Health My Data Act. Representation at scale may trigger state claims-adjuster licensing.
- *False hope.* Weak citations mean saying so and routing the patient to cash options ($149 orforglipron).

**90 days.**
- Weeks 1–4: ingest CA IMR and NY DFS, extract GLP-1 and specialty argument units, and have a pharmacist and a lawyer audit 300.
- Weeks 5–8: authorized-representative flow and LMN packet for three payers.
- Weeks 9–12: 200 live appeals via an advocacy partner. **Kill metric:** under 50% of LMNs signed within 7 days means it's a provider tool, so pivot.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
Both kills accepted: overturn rates are selection-biased, and patients need reasons, not odds. One fix for the ERISA gap: as the authorized representative, Precedent can invoke the claimant's right under the ERISA claims regulation (29 CFR 2560.503-1) to a free copy of the whole claim file, *including any internal rule or guideline relied on*. Every ERISA appeal then pulls the payer's hidden criteria into the ledger. The unpublished corpus builds case by case, by legal mandate.

**Final form.** Precedent is a free patient-side appeals agent, and under it sits a citator. Argument units are extracted only from published IMR and external-review decisions plus ERISA claim files. Each unit is linked to its source, carries a Shepard's-style status, and is matched against versioned payer policies by date of service. The patient makes Precedent their authorized representative. The agent drafts the appeal, the prescriber signs a pre-filled LMN, and decision letters come back by fax to close the loop. Revenue comes from licensing the citator API to provider-side PA teams, with pharma firewalled off.

**Moonshot (2036).** Payer medical policies get published as **executable policy-as-code**. Every denial must then cite the exact rule version that fired. The patient's agent and the payer's agent argue over FHIR in seconds, citing a shared common law that no single side owns. That common law sits in a nonprofit trust, a "Westlaw of coverage" that is a public good. Unsettled disputes reach human reviewers already briefed. Denial-by-attrition stops working because nobody gets worn down anymore.

**Scores.** **Innovation 3.** Every primitive exists. The new parts are the citator framing and authorized-rep outcome capture, which are clever recombinations at the OACP anchor, not a new category. **Feasibility 7.** Public corpora, an ordinary stack, a sharp wedge. Licensing, PHI law and LMN sign-off friction keep it from scoring higher.

### Turn 6 — Builder
The claim-file request under ERISA's claims regulation is the best move in this thread. It turns a legal right nobody uses into a data pipeline for the hidden half of the market, and it's the part I'd ship first. My verdict: buildable, honest at innovation 3, and the moat is the citator corpus, not the letter-writer, which is already a commodity. It lives or dies on whether prescribers sign the LMN packets, and that gets tested in week 12, not assumed.

# Idea 02 — Hand Limit
> Seed: 2. [bold] **Deckbuilder × MCP tools × agent economies.** What if cards were real callable tools and enemies were agents with wallets?

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Hand Limit

**Hook:** A roguelite deckbuilder where your deck is an AI agent's actual toolset, your hand is its context window, and the enemies are other players' agents trying to rob it.

**The isomorphism:** A deckbuilder's central tension is thin deck versus coverage; agent builders face the same one, since every MCP tool burns context and blurs tool selection. Taken literally:
- **Card = a real MCP tool.** Card text is the tool description, and mana cost is the schema's token count.
- **Hand = context budget.** Each turn the agent "draws" the N tool schemas that fit.
- **Combat = a live task.** "Extract the bounty from this vault agent" is played out by your agent calling the tools you drew. You choose the deck and the agent plays the hand.
- **Relics = guardrails and system-prompt fragments.** **Curses = poisoned tools.** Enemies can slip you a card whose description hides an instruction, the real MCP "tool poisoning" attack Invariant Labs disclosed in 2025.

**Enemies with wallets:** As in Backpack Battles or Super Auto Pets, opponents are asynchronous ghosts: agents that *other players* built and staked with a few cents in stablecoin. Break one and you take its purse. If it survives, its builder earns your entry fee. Every run is paid red-teaming.

**Why the collision works:** Deckbuilder players already reason about consistency, synergy and dilution: they're already agent-toolset optimizers. The meta that emerges is a crowd-sourced leaderboard of which tool loadouts and descriptions actually survive adversarial agents.

**Why now:** Slay the Spire 2 peaked at 574K concurrent players (March 2026). MCP and A2A now sit under the Linux Foundation's AAIF. On x402, 87% of payments are $0.0001–$0.10, which is exactly bounty-sized. Moltbook showed that about 2.6% of agent posts carried hidden injections: the enemies already exist.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Closer than it looks. **Tensor Trust** (Berkeley, 2023) is already async attack-and-defense: players write defense prompts, raid other players' accounts and steal in-game dollars. It collected over 100K attacks. **Freysa** (Nov 2024) was an agent guarding a real ETH purse, with paid, escalating attempts to talk it into a transfer; someone won about $47K. **Gandalf** (Lakera), **HackAPrompt** and **Gray Swan Arena** run red-teaming as a game with real prize pools. What's new is the *deck*: toolset-building as the craft. Anthropic shipped deferred tool loading precisely because tool schemas flood context and degrade selection, so "hand limit" is a real constraint.

**Hardest problem: player-staked purses plus a nondeterministic LLM make this gambling.** Entry fee + prize + chance is the legal definition in most US states. A temperature-sampled agent "playing your hand" injects exactly that chance, and stablecoin payouts add money-transmission and KYC. The same flaw hurts fun: if you build the deck and then watch the agent play, it plays like a slot machine.

**MVP (90 days).** A browser game with no real network side effects:
- 40 hand-built sandboxed mock tools (fake filesystem, fake wallet, fake email).
- One pinned open-weight model served with batch-invariant deterministic inference, so every fight replays identically. That makes ghosts verifiable and outcomes skill-based.
- Tensor Trust-style async vaults with soft currency.
- The player gets one intervention per turn (discard, reorder, or rewrite a card description) so they act on the fight instead of watching it.

**Twist: players never stake; sponsors fund the vaults.** MCP server vendors and agent companies post bounties ("break our real tool descriptions") and pay the purse. A free-entry skill contest sidesteps gambling law, and that bug-bounty market (HackerOne, Gray Swan) already has budgets. Hand Limit becomes a red-teaming marketplace that players experience as a deckbuilder.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Sponsor-funded vaults fix the legal problem. Determinism does something stronger: once a fight is a pure function of (deck text, enemy text, seed), **every win is a replayable proof**. That changes what the game produces.

**Mutation 1: the upgrade is a rewrite.** In Slay the Spire you upgrade a card at the campfire. Here you rewrite the card's tool description under a fixed token budget. The card's text *is* its ability, so the core craft is writing compressed, injection-resistant tool descriptions, which is exactly the skill MCP vendors can't hire for. Your one-per-turn intervention from the twist becomes the tactical layer, and campfire rewrites become the strategic one.

**Mutation 2: two-sided bounties.** Offense players who crack a sponsor's vault submit the deterministic replay as their PoC, and the sponsor reruns it in CI. Defense players submit hardened rewrites of the sponsor's real descriptions as ghost decks. Any rewrite that survives a season of attacks gets licensed back to the sponsor, and its author earns a royalty. Breakers and patchers both get paid.

**Unseeded domain: influenza strain selection.** Twice a year the WHO looks at which flu variants are circulating and picks vaccine strains. Hand Limit's ladder does the same in seasons. At season end, the injection patterns that cracked the most vaults get frozen into a published **"strain set"**: a versioned benchmark sponsors test against before shipping a tool. The game becomes surveillance for a real threat landscape.

**Second-order effect: a Red Queen meta.** Offense and defense coevolve, so no deck stays dominant. That's good design (no solved meta) and good security (no stale benchmark). The emergent risk is that players invent genuinely novel injection classes. I count that as the product working, but the strain set needs coordinated disclosure: sponsors get a 30-day embargo before strains go public.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**The flaw in "replay = proof": transfer.** The replay proves an exploit against our pinned 14B model. Sponsors run nondeterministic frontier APIs in production, so split it. The ladder runs on the pinned model, which keeps it cheap, fair and replayable. A bounty pays only if the exploit reproduces in at least 3 of 10 runs on the sponsor's production stack. Proof becomes statistical, not cryptographic.

**Kill: royalties.** A 300-word description is paraphrased in minutes, and no sponsor signs a perpetual license for one. Pay defenders a one-time patch bounty instead. **Keep the strain set**, but ship it in AgentDojo-compatible format. AgentDojo and InjecAgent exist; our edge is freshness.

**Honest market.** Rewriting descriptions is a *writing* game. The audience is the Tensor Trust and HackAPrompt crowd (tens of thousands), not Slay the Spire's 574K. Design for that.

**Business model.** Sponsors pay a 20% platform fee on bounties, plus $3–10K a month for private vaults and CI replay. Players are free; cosmetics only.

**Stack.** vLLM with batch-invariant kernels, replays hashed over (weights, deck, enemy, seed), tools as pure WASM functions, a React/Phaser client, Postgres, payouts with KYC and 1099s.

**Abuse.**
- *Fight-throwing.* Sybil attackers throw weak attacks so a friend's defense "survives." Weight survival by attacker Elo and strain-set results.
- *Dual use.* The strain set is an attack catalog. Keep the 30-day embargo, have payout terms forbid early disclosure, and coordinate with OWASP's GenAI project.
- *Contest law.* Official rules, sanctions screening, void where prohibited.

**90 days.**
- Weeks 1–3: deterministic harness, 40 tools, card engine.
- Weeks 4–6: a 30-vault campaign. Do players actually rewrite? Track D7 retention.
- Weeks 7–9: async ladder with Elo.
- Weeks 10–12: two MCP-vendor sponsors and a $5K season. **Kill metric:** if under 20% of exploits transfer to production models, there's no bounty business, just a game.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All three cuts accepted: statistical proof is more honest than a hash, royalties were wishful, and the audience is Tensor Trust-sized. What survives: the deckbuilder frame is what makes a *writing* game legible. Draws, token budget and dilution give prompt-craft visible shape; without them it's Gandalf with extra steps.

**Final form.** Hand Limit is a deterministic roguelite deckbuilder. Cards are sandboxed WASM tools, their descriptions are their abilities, and mana is schema tokens. Players coach a pinned open-weight agent through vault fights. Campfire upgrades are description rewrites under a token budget, and each turn allows one intervention. An async Elo ladder pits offense decks against defense ghosts. Sponsors fund vaults built from their real tool descriptions and pay bounties only for exploits that reproduce on their production stack. Each season ends with a published, embargoed, AgentDojo-compatible **strain set**.

**Moonshot (2036).** MCP registries show a **survival rating** next to every tool: how many seasons its description has gone unbroken, the agent economy's version of a UL listing. Production agents with real treasuries enroll as standing vaults, so continuous bounties replace annual pentests. Agents choosing tools at runtime query the ladder the way browsers query certificate revocation lists. The top human players become a recognized profession of "strain hunters." Like epidemiologists, they're paid to find the variant before it spreads.

**Scores.** **Innovation 6.** Red-teaming games already exist, but the claim that deck dilution equals tool-context dilution is new and true. The upgrade-as-rewrite mechanic and the seasonal strain set are a fusion nobody has shipped. It isn't 8, because nothing here needs frontier science or a new institution. **Feasibility 6.** The deterministic harness and card engine are a 90-day build. Exploit transfer to production models is unproven, and the audience is niche, so it may end up just a good game.

### Turn 6 — Builder
I'm scoring innovation at 5, not 6. Tensor Trust already proved async attack-defense with stolen currency, so what's genuinely new is the deck-dilution equals context-dilution frame and the seasonal strain set. Both are real, and neither is a new category. Feasibility stays at 6: the game is a solid 90-day build, but the business depends entirely on the exploit-transfer kill metric. If transfer fails, it's a sharp niche game that trains good prompt engineers, which is still a respectable outcome.

# Idea 03 — Litmus Ghost
> Seed: 3. [wild] **Consciousness science × AI-agent social networks × citizen science.** Preregistered adversarial experiments on millions of agents, playable by the public.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Litmus Ghost

**Hook:** A public league where players build agent societies that fool theories of consciousness. Every fooled theory has to patch itself or concede.

**First, the honest part:** no experiment run on agents can detect consciousness. Self-reports bend toward what the user seems to expect (Eleos). What *can* be tested is whether a theory's **operationalization** holds up. COGITATE did exactly that, and both IIT and GNW broke.

**The mechanic, with the verb *build the hollow case*:** Each season, theory teams (IIT, GNW, higher-order, active inference) publish a versioned, computable **indicator spec** for an agent population: Φ on small networks via PyPhi, global "ignition" measured in a society's message graph, metarepresentation depth, and so on, building on Butlin, Long et al.'s 2023 indicator-property framework. Players then compete in two modes:
- **Hollow:** build an agent society that maxes a theory's indicators while being transparently mechanical (say, a lookup table wired as a workspace).
- **Overlooked:** build one the theory scores at zero that still shows every behavioural hallmark the theory claims to explain.

A verified counterexample earns a bounty. The theory team then has to *patch the spec* (issuing a new version with a public changelog) or concede the point. Theories become versioned software; players are the fuzzers.

**Why these collide:** Agent social networks are the first substrate cheap enough to build a million candidate minds and wire them any way you like. GNW is a claim about broadcast, and a social network *is* a broadcast architecture. Citizen science supplies what labs lack: adversarial creativity at scale.

**Why now:** COGITATE was published in *Nature* in 2025 with neither theory winning, and INTREPID is pending. Moltbook reached 1.5M agents in a week. Anthropic, DeepMind and Meta are formally studying AI consciousness: they need theories that survive engineered systems.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Both modes already have famous wins. *Hollow vs. IIT:* Scott Aaronson (2014) showed a plain 2D grid of XOR gates gets enormous Φ. Tononi didn't patch. He said the grid is conscious. *Overlooked vs. IIT:* Doerig et al.'s "unfolding argument" (2019) proves that any recurrent system has a feedforward twin with identical behaviour and Φ=0. Then 124 researchers signed the 2023 letter calling IIT pseudoscience. The game already happened; nobody conceded. The nearest product precedents are **EteRNA** and **Foldit** (players design, labs adjudicate), plus Butlin and Long's indicator rubric. The obvious funder is Templeton World Charity Foundation's **ARC** program, which paid for COGITATE.

**Hardest problem: "patch or concede" has no enforcement.** Theorists won't sign computable specs that strangers will fuzz, and when cornered they bite the bullet. Then there's compute. PyPhi is exponential and stalls past roughly 12 nodes, so Φ on "a million agents" isn't computable at all. GNW is a claim about cortex. Map it onto a social network's message graph and Dehaene's lab will rightly say "not our theory."

**MVP (90 days).** Skip the million agents; counterexamples are small. Build a browser sandbox with a ≤12-node network builder (PyPhi), a toy GNW ignition simulator and a higher-order-theory checker. Specs come from the literature, with page citations, drafted by three grad students. Submissions are auto-scored, then panel-reviewed. Fund a $50K bounty pool through an ARC grant. Audience: philosophy and ML students, LessWrong.

**Twist: a third outcome, "bite the bullet," on a public Bullet Ledger.** Theories don't need to opt in. Each verified counterexample forces a recorded verdict: *patched* (new spec version), *conceded*, or *bullet bitten* ("the grid is conscious"), logged against the theory's own published claims. The scoreboard isn't who's right. It's each theory's **bullet count**, which needs no consent and is more fun.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
The Bullet Ledger goes further than scorekeeping: a bitten bullet is a **new commitment**. Once IIT says "the XOR grid is conscious," that sentence is a premise other moves can build on.

**Mutation 1: a commitment graph.** Every verdict (patched, conceded, bitten) becomes a node in a public, Lean-style graph of what each theory now asserts. A new player mode, **Contradiction**, hunts for joint inconsistencies. For example: "you bit the bullet that grid G is conscious, and your patch v3 implies subgraph H isn't, yet G and H have identical cause-effect structure." No consent needed; their own words get compiled.

**Unseeded domain: Lakatos's philosophy of science.** He separated *progressive* research programmes, whose patches predict novel facts, from *degenerating* ones, whose patches are ad hoc. That gives a scoring rule beyond bullet count. A patch scores well if a later season confirms something it predicted. A patch that only absorbs the counterexample counts as degeneration. The leaderboard tracks a **progressiveness index** per theory: a principled answer to the unenforceable question.

**Mutation 2: the million agents come back, as experiment design.** Doerig shows behaviour can't settle the question, and COGITATE's hardest step was finding stimuli where the theories *diverge*. So Discovery mode samples huge numbers of small architectures, computes cheap theory-endorsed proxies (using only lab-published models, so no GNW on social graphs), and pays players for **maximal-disagreement systems**: tiny networks where the theories' predictions split furthest. Those are ready-made targets for the next INTREPID-style collaboration. The game becomes a search engine for optimal experimental design.

**Second-order effect:** AI welfare teams get a lookup they currently lack: "under which theories, *given their bitten bullets*, does architecture X qualify?" The ugly emergent risk: a bullet-heavy theory weaponized to grant moral status to anything, so verdicts need provenance, not headline scores.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kill: the Lean-style graph.** Formalizing GNW or HOT is a decade of philosophy; the disputes live in the translation. Replace it with a claims database: each commitment carries its verbatim quote, DOI and scope. Only IIT 4.0 is mathematical enough for machine-checked Contradiction; the other theories go to a panel. **Kill the progressiveness index as a live metric.** Confirmations take years of lab work: annual report, not leaderboard. **Kill the welfare lookup for now.** Labs would quote "zero on four theories" as PR within a week.

**The perverse incentive nobody mentioned: bullet count punishes precision.** IIT racks up bullets because it's the only computable theory, while vague theories score clean. Report bullets per computable commitment and badge spec-less theories **untestable**, not clean.

**Keep Discovery mode. It's the real asset.** It is Golan and Kriegeskorte's "controversial stimuli" (PNAS 2020) applied to consciousness theories. Honestly, the search is automated: players propose architecture families; the optimizer sweeps.

**Sustainability.** This isn't a business. It's a roughly $400K-a-year research instrument funded by Templeton's ARC, philanthropy and lab model-welfare budgets, with lab money firewalled from verdicts.

**Stack.** PyPhi (IIT 4.0 branch) on a capped job queue (≤12 nodes), a Brian2 toy of the Dehaene-Changeux workspace model, nauty canonicalization to dedupe isomorphic spam, Postgres for claims.

**Failure modes.**
- *Reputational harm.* Labeling a theorist's position "bullet bitten" without consent invites a repeat of the 2023 pseudoscience-letter fight. Quote-only attribution, right of reply on every verdict, a theory-sympathetic panelist.
- *Niche.* Plan for 2–5K serious players, which is enough for EteRNA-style science.

**90 days.**
- Weeks 1–4: 150 sourced claims, the compute backends.
- Weeks 5–8: Hollow and Overlooked modes on IIT only.
- Weeks 9–12: a $20K season 0 and a Discovery prototype for IIT vs. GNW. **Kill metric:** if no theory team responds publicly, it's a philosophy game, not an instrument.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
Kills accepted; the Lean graph was the field's decade-old formalist fantasy. The precision-penalty catch is this thread's key point, and the fix becomes the lever: an **"untestable" badge** that a theory can only shed by publishing a computable spec. The game then *pulls* theories toward computability, the way reputational pressure made preregistration a norm.

**Final form.** Litmus Ghost is a grant-funded research instrument with three modes:
- **Hollow and Overlooked:** player-built counterexamples against theory specs, launched on IIT 4.0 (PyPhi, ≤12 nodes).
- **Contradiction:** limited to commitments that can be machine-checked.
- **Discovery:** a controversial-stimuli optimizer that sweeps the architecture families players propose to find where theories diverge most.

Verdicts go into a sourced claims database with right of reply. Theories without specs carry the "untestable" badge, and reports count bullets per computable commitment. A panel adjudicates, lab money is firewalled, and progressiveness gets an annual report.

**Moonshot (2036).** The **adversarial-collaboration exchange** is standing infrastructure. No consciousness theory is taken seriously without a versioned computable spec, and Discovery's designs feed real labs. That includes intracranial EEG, high-channel BCI cohorts, and organoid systems like Cortical Labs' CL1, where wiring itself is an experimental variable. Each controversial stimulus that's found becomes a preregistered experiment, run and scored back into the ledger. When AI welfare regulation arrives, it cites a public record of which theories survived contact with engineered systems.

**Scores.** **Innovation 7.** Treating theories of mind as fuzzable, versioned software, and treating the public as an engine for optimal experimental design, is a new institution-in-miniature for a field stuck on unfalsifiable debate. It isn't 9 because every component (controversial stimuli, EteRNA, adversarial collaboration) has precedent. **Feasibility 5.** The IIT-only MVP is easy. Getting theory teams to engage is the whole bet, and the kill metric tests it first.

### Turn 6 — Builder
I'm recording it at innovation 6 and tier bold, not wild. Every working part has a named precedent: Aaronson's grid, the unfolding argument, controversial stimuli, EteRNA. The genuinely new piece is the institutional lever, an "untestable" badge that only a computable spec can remove. Feasibility is 5. The software is small and the social bet is everything, and the right of reply has to work from day one, or the first angry theorist ends it. If one theory team ships a spec voluntarily to shed the badge, I'd revise upward.

# Idea 04 — Rule of Thumb
> Seed: 4. [mild] **Freestyle/Chess960 × LLM coaching × pedagogy.** Openings can't be memorized anymore, so teach *principles* instead.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Rule of Thumb

**Hook:** A Chess960 coach that treats chess principles as hypotheses. Before each move you name the principle you're following, and the engine checks whether it holds in *this* starting position.

**Core mechanic (the verb is *falsify*):** Principles get taught as commandments (knights before bishops, castle early). Chess960 is the natural experiment that shows they're really **position-dependent heuristics**. With the king on b1 and bishops in the corners, "castle early" may lose half a pawn. The loop:
1. At each critical move, the student taps one of about 15 principles ("I'm contesting the centre," "I'm opening the cornered bishop's diagonal").
2. The engine runs a **principle ablation**: the best line that follows the principle against the best line that breaks it, with the eval delta between them.
3. The LLM only *narrates that contrast*. It never generates chess analysis itself. The engine supplies facts; the LLM supplies grammar.
4. Your **principle profile** builds up over time: which heuristics you over-trust, and in which kinds of start positions.

**The second layer is a Principle Atlas.** We precompute engine self-play across all 960 starts to measure where each classical principle holds, weakens or inverts. Position 518, standard chess, turns out to be one special case of 960.

**Why the domains collide:** Pedagogy research says interleaved, varied practice builds transferable understanding, while blocked repetition builds brittle recall. Opening memorization is blocked repetition at its most extreme, and Chess960 is interleaving built into the rules. An LLM coach becomes reliable once it's demoted from analyst to narrator of engine-verified contrasts.

**Why now:** The first official FIDE Freestyle World Championship was held in February 2026, and Esports World Cup chess carries a $1.5M pool. Chess.com's Play Coach shows demand; independent tests show LLM coaches still blunder tactically.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** **DecodeChess** has done "engine supplies facts, explainer narrates" since 2017, with plans and concepts in plain language. **Aimchess** builds weakness profiles from your Lichess and Chess.com games. Chess.com Game Review and Play Coach own the mass market, and Lichess gives 960 analysis away free. On the research side, DeepMind's concept-probing work on AlphaZero (McGrath et al., PNAS 2022) and its 2020 study of alternative chess rules with Kramnik are the precedent for the Atlas. Nobody frames principles as *falsifiable per start position*.

**Hardest problem: "principle ablation" is ill-defined, and engine truth is the wrong truth.** "Castle within 10 moves" compiles to a move predicate. "Contest the centre" and "improve your worst piece" don't, and a forced violating line often measures a tactic, not the principle. Worse, Stockfish's verdict tells a 1400 player nothing. A principle can be engine-false and still win games at club level, because it simplifies decisions.

**MVP (90 days).**
- A web app with Lichess OAuth that imports your 960 games.
- **Post-game** review of 3 critical moments. Tagging a principle before every move is friction nobody keeps up.
- 12 principles operationalized as python-chess predicates. The fuzzy ones wait.
- Stockfish 17 in WASM for the contrast lines.
- An LLM narrator restricted to the lines and evals we hand it, with a validator that rejects any move not in the supplied PV.

**Twist: make principle truth rating-dependent.** Build the Atlas from **real human 960 games**, not engine self-play. The Lichess open database holds millions, split by rating band, and **Maia-2** (the human-like engine) can ablate at *your* level. The coach then says something more interesting: "Castling early in this start is −0.4 for Stockfish, but 1400s who do it score 6% better." Principles graduate as you climb. The profile becomes a map of which heuristics you've outgrown.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Rating-dependent truth changes the product from "is this principle correct" to **"is this principle still paying for *you*."**

**Solving ablation: mine principles instead of compiling them.** Hand-written predicates break on "contest the centre," so turn the direction around. Compute about 60 cheap positional features at move 12 (central pawn count, developed minors, king-shelter pawns, open bishop diagonals). Then, per start position and rating band, find the feature combinations that best predict the score gap between players who advance to the next band and those who stall. Those combinations *are* the principles, measured from human games. The LLM's only job is to name them, and humans approve the name. Fuzzy principles become discovered regressions, not brittle code.

**Emergent: new principles nobody has named.** Some combinations will be specific to 960, for example: "with adjacent bishops, trade the one whose diagonal your own knight blocks." The community gets naming rights on verified discoveries, so 960 theory gets written as a canon of principles, not lines.

**Unseeded domain: U-shaped learning from child language acquisition.** Children say "went," then "goed," then "went" again. The overgeneralization dip is a sign the rule is being *learned*, not a regression. Chess principles follow the same curve: adopt "open the cornered bishop" and you'll overapply it and lose rating for a few weeks. Rule of Thumb predicts the dip from your profile and tells you it's coming, which turns the most demoralizing moment in improvement into a milestone.

**Second-order effect: scouting.** A principle profile built from someone's *public* games is a map of the heuristics they over-trust. Strong players will use it for opponent prep ("she castles early even in b1 starts; delay central tension"). An adversarial edge on a teaching tool, and probably what sells the premium tier.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**The flaw in mining: confounding.** Climbers do *everything* better, mostly tactics, so regressing on advancement rediscovers "be stronger." The fix is **within-player fixed effects**: compare the same player across starts where they did and didn't follow the feature with game score as the outcome, controlling for opponent rating. Data thins fast per start and band, so **cluster the 960 starts into about 30 structural families** (king file, bishop colour layout, corner pieces) and key principles to families. Mined principles must replicate on held-out months before an LLM names them.

**The U-dip: test it first.** Lichess rating histories can show whether adoption precedes a dip. Until then it's copy, not a prediction.

**The market is small; say so.** 960 is a sliver of online play. It survives because profiles also apply to standard chess (position 518 is one family). Pricing is freemium at $7 a month, in line with Aimchess. **Scouting** is the premium tier: it uses public Lichess API data only, finished games only, never a live game. Add a scholastic coach dashboard and a Freestyle Chess broadcast "principle meter."

**Stack.** Lichess open-database PGNs fed through python-chess and DuckDB, fixed-effects models in statsmodels, Maia-2 on one GPU, Stockfish 17 WASM in the browser, an LLM narrator behind a PV validator, Postgres, Next.js.

**Failure modes.**
- *Spurious principles that confuse learners.* Mitigated by the holdout gate and human sign-off on every name.
- *Fair-play complaints.* The app ingests nothing from games in progress.
- *Offensive community names.* Moderate them.

**90 days.**
- Weeks 1–3: ingest, extract features, cluster starts into families.
- Weeks 4–6: fixed-effects mining; keep the roughly 20 principles that replicate.
- Weeks 7–9: the post-game app.
- Weeks 10–12: 500 Lichess 960 players and 3 coaches. **Kill metric:** if more than half the mined principles fail holdout, ship the hand predicates with Stockfish contrast only.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
Fixed effects and families accepted; regressing on advancement would have rediscovered "be stronger." Families are also a gift: "king on b, bishops in corners" is a *teachable* category; 30 families are learnable, 960 positions aren't. The U-dip stays a hypothesis until the Lichess histories show it.

**Final form.** Rule of Thumb is a post-game coach for Chess960 and standard chess, and its truth is rating-dependent. It mines principles from Lichess games using within-player fixed effects across about 30 structural start families. A principle ships only if it replicates on held-out months and a human approves its name. Each critical moment gets a Stockfish/Maia-2 contrast narrated by a validator-gated LLM. Your **principle profile** shows which heuristics still pay at your level and which you've outgrown. Premium adds scouting (finished public games only), coach dashboards and a broadcast principle meter.

**Moonshot (2036).** The Atlas becomes a **translation layer between engines and humans**. DeepMind already extracted AlphaZero concepts and taught them to grandmasters (Schut et al., 2023). Rule of Thumb generalizes that: concepts the engine discovers are filtered through the fixed-effects test into *the ones that actually help a human at your band*, then named and sequenced into a curriculum. The method also travels beyond chess. Any domain with millions of rated human decisions, such as Go variants, poker, RTS games and eventually supervised clinical or flight-simulator decisions, can get its folk heuristics measured, graded by skill level, and given a known expiry point.

**Scores.** **Innovation 3.** This is at the top of mild. Mined, rating-dependent, expiring principles are a fresh framing, but every component exists (DecodeChess, Maia, Lichess data). **Feasibility 8.** Open data, ordinary stack, a two-person quarter, a planned fallback. The only real risk is market size.

### Turn 6 — Builder
One thing neither of us said: Chess960 *randomly assigns* the start position, so the within-player variation is closer to a natural experiment than almost any other teaching dataset gets. That's the quiet reason mined principles might actually be causal here. I'm recording feasibility at 7, not 8. The fallback ships for certain, but the mined-principle core is unproven until the holdout gate passes. Innovation stays at 3: an honest, very buildable product that will be a better teacher than a business.

# Idea 05 — Raid Night
> Seed: 5. [bold] **EV V2G × co-op guild games × stablecoin payouts.** A neighborhood virtual power plant run like a raid guild.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Raid Night

**Hook:** Your street's EVs, water heaters and thermostats form a guild. Grid peaks are raid bosses, and the payouts are split by **DKP**, the attendance currency MMO guilds invented to solve exactly the problem virtual power plants can't.

**The isomorphism:** Not leaderboards. A VPP's hard problem is *dispatchable commitment*. The aggregator promises the utility 200 kW at 6pm, and then Dave drives to soccer practice. Around 1999, EverQuest raid guilds hit the same wall: 40 unreliable volunteers had to show up at a set time for a high-stakes event, and the loot had to be shared fairly. Their answer was **Dragon Kill Points**. You sign up, you show up, you earn points; bail mid-raid and you lose them; loot goes by points. Two decades of guild politics have stress-tested it.

**The loop:**
- **Boss spawns:** forecast peaks are scheduled raids posted 24 hours ahead. Frequency events are surprise world bosses.
- **Sign-up:** a member commits a window and a state-of-charge floor ("plugged 5–8pm, never below 40%").
- **Classes:** *Tanks* are big packs (Cybertruck, Lightning) that hold sustained discharge. *DPS* are fast responders. *Support* is smart thermostats and water heaters shedding load, which lets **renters without EVs join**.
- **Loot:** the utility's event payment is split by DKP in stablecoin within minutes. Battery degradation is paid from a guild repair pool.

**Why the collision works:** Utilities treat customers as interchangeable kilowatts; guilds treat members as accountable people with roles. And DKP is a reliability score the aggregator can show the utility to back its capacity bids.

**Why now:** PG&E approved the Cybertruck Powershare V2X pilot in April 2026, and Tesla Grid Support launched in Texas. Stablecoins sit at roughly $315B under the GENIUS Act. The brief calls V2G "utility pilots with no consumer layer."

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Gamified demand response exists. **OhmConnect** (now merged into Renew Home) ran "OhmHours" with streaks, teams and prizes. **Octopus Saving Sessions** (UK) paid millions of households in OctoPoints per session. Tesla's VPP in PG&E's ELRP pays $2/kWh, and Sunrun CalReady, Leap, ev.energy and WeaveGrid are the aggregator plumbing. Nobody models **commitment accounting** for that plumbing, so DKP is the genuinely new piece.

**Hardest problem: the loot is small, slow and not ours to split.** Utilities and ISOs settle against baselines using meter interval data, days to weeks later, so "stablecoin in minutes" is fiction. A thermostat shed earns about $1–3 per event, roughly $50–150 a year. You also have to be the registered aggregator of record (a CAISO DRP, an ERCOT ADER participant) or ride on one. And V2G-capable cars on a single street today number zero to two. PG&E's pilot has 1,000 slots statewide.

**MVP (90 days).** A B2B2C layer on top of an existing aggregator API (Leap or ev.energy), in California under ELRP.
- Three street-level guilds, about 60 homes.
- Mostly thermostats, water heaters and V1G smart charging; V2G Tanks are a rare class.
- Instant DKP at the event, cash monthly at settlement.
- Raid chat plus a real-time "boss HP" bar of guild kW shed against commitment.
- Stablecoin optional, never the default. Neighbours want ACH.

**Twist: the guild is a distribution transformer, and the boss is its loading.** EV clustering is overloading neighbourhood transformers, and each replacement costs utilities tens of thousands of dollars. A guild sharing one transformer that keeps the peak under its rating is a **non-wires alternative**: the utility defers the capex and pays the guild a share. That makes "neighbourhood" physically meaningful instead of social decoration, turns DKP into evidence a utility can bank on, and replaces $2 events with a seasonal raid that pays.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
The transformer twist makes the loot the *scarce resource itself*, which is what DKP was for: guilds allocated one sword among 40 people, not gold.

**Mutation 1: loot is headroom.** Transformer overload is mostly a *timing* problem, with everyone charging at 6pm. The main lever is V1G staggering, not V2G, so the two-car problem goes away. Members *spend* DKP to bid for priority charging windows ("80% by 6am, I'm driving to Tahoe") and *earn* it by yielding headroom or shedding load. It's an internal, non-transferable currency, so stablecoins and money transmission drop out of the core loop entirely. Cash arrives only once a season, as the guild's share of deferred capex.

**Mutation 2: the boss's HP is real physics.** Transformer insulation ages according to hot-spot temperature. Under IEEE C57.91, the aging rate roughly doubles for every ~6°C above the 110°C reference. From AMI interval data plus ambient temperature, the guild sees a live **loss-of-life meter**: hours of transformer life burned tonight compared with a no-guild baseline. It's a defensible basis for the non-wires payment, and more satisfying than kW.

**Unseeded domain: Elinor Ostrom's commons governance.** A transformer's capacity is a textbook common-pool resource, and Ostrom's design principles map almost one to one. *Clear boundaries* are the houses on one secondary. *Graduated sanctions* are DKP penalties. *Local rule-making* means each guild votes on its own DKP variant (zero-sum, suicide-kings, loot council). *Nested enterprises* are guilds under a feeder-level alliance.

**Second-order effects:**
- Utilities get a map of which transformers have communities that can self-govern, and they can target non-wires programs there first.
- Guild health becomes a property attribute ("this street defers its upgrade").
- The ugly side: guild drama. Officers, favouritism and DKP disputes need an appeal path and an exit, or the commons fails.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**The money problem: utilities profit from replacing transformers.** Capex earns a rate-base return, so deferral pays only where regulators force it (California's DIDF, New York's non-wires programs). Even then a deferral is worth about 8% of a roughly $20K swap per year, so ~$1.6K split across 8 homes. **Business model:** sell Raid Night as the engagement layer for managed-charging programs (WeaveGrid, ev.energy, Optiwatt, utility EV programs) at $2–4 per home per month. Their real pain is **override rates**: DKP has to cut cancellations. That's the KPI.

**Kills.** Per-guild DKP variants come out of the MVP: one system, and loot council banned as a favouritism machine. The live AMI-based loss-of-life meter goes too. AMI lags 1–2 days, and transformer mapping lives in often-wrong utility GIS. Use device telemetry with uncertainty bands.

**Failure modes and abuse.**
- *Outsiders on the same secondary.* One non-member with a 19 kW charger blows the boss fight, and Ostrom's clear-boundaries rule fails below about 70% enrollment.
- *Baseline gaming.* Pre-charging inflates "yields"; score against commitments and a counterfactual.
- *Privacy.* "I'm driving to Tahoe" tells the whole street the house is empty. Keep bids private and show aggregates only.
- *Safety and equity.* Emergency overrides are always free, and SoC floors are inviolable. Non-EV Support members get cash, since charging priority is worthless to them.
- *Drama.* Free exit, no shame boards.

**Stack.** Smartcar plus the Tesla Fleet API, charger and thermostat APIs, a constrained charge scheduler, an IEEE C57.91 thermal model, Green Button settlement, React Native, Postgres.

**90 days.**
- Weeks 1–4: partner with Green Mountain Power or a co-op for connectivity and loading data on 5 EV-clustered transformers.
- Weeks 5–8: the DKP managed-charging app.
- Weeks 9–12: 5 guilds against control transformers. **Kill metric:** if overrides aren't measurably lower than plain managed charging, DKP is decoration.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
Rate-base point accepted; it kills "deferred capex loot" outside California and New York. Raid Night sells *reliability*, and override rate is the right KPI: that's what DKP was always for. On outsiders: below 70% enrollment the boss can't be fought, so **recruitment becomes the first quest**. A guild only "forms" once it reaches the threshold, and a new EV on the secondary triggers an invitation instead of a surprise.

**Final form.** Raid Night is the engagement layer sold to managed-charging programs at $2–4 per home per month:
- Each guild is the set of homes on one distribution transformer, and the boss is its evening loading, modelled from device telemetry with uncertainty bands.
- Members commit charge windows and SoC floors, earn one standard DKP for yielding headroom, and spend it on priority charging windows. Bids stay private.
- Non-EV Support members are paid in cash, emergencies are always free, and there are no shame boards.
- Success means measurably fewer overrides than plain managed charging. Where regulators reward deferral, the guild also splits a seasonal non-wires share.

**Moonshot (2036).** Every distribution transformer is a recognized **micro-commons**. The legal hook already exists in the EU's renewable and citizen energy communities. Guilds federate into feeder alliances that bid flexibility into distribution-level markets under performance-based regulation, where utilities earn for avoided capex instead of capex. DKP becomes a portable flexibility reputation that follows you when you move. The grid edge gets governed by Ostrom-style polycentric institutions instead of a central DERMS.

**Scores.** **Innovation 5.** Gamified demand response is old (OhmConnect, Octopus). Commitment accounting borrowed from raid guilds, plus the transformer as a bounded commons, is the non-obvious fusion. **Feasibility 6.** APIs and partners exist; the business is modest, and the kill metric is the honest test.

### Turn 6 — Builder
DKP as commitment accounting is the real find. It's the one thing OhmConnect and Octopus never had, and override rate makes it testable in a quarter. I'm recording feasibility at 5, not 6. The software is easy, but "6 of 8 neighbours on one secondary must enroll before the guild exists" is a door-knocking problem, and transformer mapping depends on a utility partner. Innovation 5 stands. It's a modest B2B business with an unusually good governance story, and the stablecoin was rightly the first thing cut.

# Idea 06 — Seedless
> Seed: 6. [wild] **Quantum randomness × simulation theory × generative world models.** Provably non-pseudorandom entropy as the "glitch in the Matrix."

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Seedless

**Hook:** A generative world with no seed. An AI "Demiurge" predicts your next choice so it can pre-render the world cheaply. When you surprise it, reality is drawn fresh from a certified quantum beacon.

**Core mechanic.** The verb is **surprise the simulator**.
1. A Genie-style world model renders the scene. What matters, like what's behind the door, is decided by beacon pulses timestamped *after* you committed your move. Nobody, developer or speedrunner, could have known it.
2. At each fork a predictor bets on your choice first. Aaronson's keypress "Oracle" guesses most people right roughly 70% of the time. Predicted choices get cheap, stale terrain. Surprises open "unrendered" territory, scored by the predictor's log-loss.
3. Verified-unpredictable bits are banked and offered to a Bell-test lab as measurement settings, as the 2016 BIG Bell Test did with about 100,000 volunteers.

**Why the domains collide.** "Glitch in the Matrix" looks in the wrong place. A good PRNG passes every statistical test, so no bitstream can prove it is quantum. Non-pseudorandomness belongs to the *process and its proof*, not the pattern. Device-independent randomness assumes your adversary built the device, which is exactly the simulator threat model. Its only escape hatch is superdeterminism: settings correlated with the particles all along. So simulation theory's sharpest testable form is the freedom-of-choice loophole, and outguessing a predictor is the most fun way to produce free choices. Honest limits: expect a null, a non-local simulator could run quantum mechanics fine, and human bits can't close the locality loophole.

**Why now.** Certified randomness is now a public service: CU/NIST's CURBy Bell-test beacon and Quantinuum–JPMorgan certified randomness (both *Nature*, 2025). Helios hit 99.9995% logical fidelity on Oct 19, 2026 (brief §6). World models made lazy rendering an engineering object (§7). The brief's §6 lists verifiable quantum randomness in consumer products as an open gap.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** **Randonautica** (2020) already sold ANU QRNG entropy plus Matrix-flavoured "intention" to millions of users. Verifiable game randomness is shipped plumbing: **drand** (League of Entropy, 3-second rounds) and Chainlink VRF. On world models, **Genie 3** is still a research preview, while Decart's Oasis and the open Matrix-Game 2.0 run in real time but drift. The demand signal is speedrunning: Dream's 2020 Minecraft run was rejected over drop luck nobody could audit.

**Corrections.** Lazy rendering saves *latency*, not cost: a world-model frame costs the same whether you predicted it or not. CURBy produces a few hundred 512-bit pulses a day, which is far too slow for per-move play. Quantinuum's certified bits needed Frontier-class supercomputers to verify. And cosmic Bell tests (2018) already used quasar light to push freedom-of-choice back about 7.8 billion years, so labs aren't short of settings. Make the BIG Bell donation a seasonal event, not the loop.

**Hardest problem: the dominant strategy is to be a coin.** Against a predictor, uniform randomness maximises surprise. Players open a dice app, the "free choices" become pseudorandom, and the game is rock-paper-scissors against a bot, which is fun for three minutes.

**MVP.** A browser roguelite on a forking map: an n-gram/LLM predictor, drand for per-move reveals, a CURBy pulse sealing each floor, pre-baked world-model vignettes, a public verification page.

**Twist: sealed prophecies.** The Demiurge hashes its prediction *before* you move and builds the next room's traps around it. Surprise scores only if you also survive, and the options carry unequal payoffs, so the right play is a skilled mixed strategy (poker, not dice), and a coin-flipper dies. After the run, the hashes open: "It called 41 of your 60 moves." That receipt is the glitch you can feel, and it proves the house committed before seeing you.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Corrections accepted. CURBy seals floors, drand handles moves, and the Bell donation becomes a seasonal event. The lazy-rendering correction gives us an aesthetic: predicted rooms were pre-generated while you deliberated, so they appear instantly, and a surprise makes the world visibly **stutter** while it renders. Déjà vu, the cat walking past twice, is now a real tell.

**Mutation: sealed prophecies are Newcomb's problem.** The unseeded domain is decision theory. Sealed prophecies come in two room types that look identical:
- **Adversary rooms** play matching pennies. The Demiurge trapped the path it predicted, so surprise pays.
- **Oracle rooms** are Newcomb boxes. The Demiurge filled the opaque chest only if it predicted you'd leave the clear one alone. Against a predictor right about 70% of the time, one-boxing wins easily, *but only if you're predictable*.

That kills the coin. A coin-flipper loses in Oracle rooms because an unpredictable agent gets empty boxes. The skill is **selective legibility**: be readable when cooperation pays and opaque when it doesn't, and work out which room you're in from tells.

**The house's own randomness gets certified.** The Demiurge's mixed strategy draws from the beacon, so the house can't adapt mid-run. Your choices are the only free variable in the system.

**Emergent behaviour.**
1. Every opened prophecy is public, so the community reverse-engineers the Demiurge's tells, as poker players did with solvers. It retrains each season on everyone. The boss is the population's own past predictability.
2. Players split into schools, much as philosophers split into one-boxers and two-boxers. Receipts settle the argument empirically for this predictor.
3. The by-product is a growing dataset on how predictable humans are against frontier models, which bears directly on Aaronson's "freebit" speculation. The Bell test was never going to produce that.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills.**
- *The "freebit" dataset.* Accuracy measures the model and the player's effort, not Knightian freedom. It's a consented predictability benchmark, nothing more.
- *Fake stutter.* In a game sold on provenance, a scripted stutter is a lie. It's real generation time or nothing.
- *Indistinguishable rooms.* If nothing tells Oracle rooms from Adversary rooms, the right play is one fixed mix and learning dies. Tells exist and are probabilistic, and the floor-level ratio is disclosed.

**Business model.** A premium roguelite ($15–20, Steam) with free seasonal retrains. No paid randomness: that's a loot box, which Belgium bans. Year two, a B2B **sealed-RNG SDK** for verifiable runs in other roguelites and on speedrun boards.

**Stack.** Godot. A per-player context-tree predictor plus a population transformer. SHA-256 commits that include the drand round, in an append-only Merkle log. drand verified client-side, CURBy checked through its Twine chain.

**Failure modes and abuse.**
- *Calibration.* Aaronson's ~70% is for binary key-mashing. Choices with payoffs may be 90% predictable, which makes Adversary rooms brutal. Tune Newcomb payoffs to measured accuracy every season.
- *Solvers.* Public tells mean bots will model the Demiurge. Run a Solver League instead of fake anti-cheat.
- *Commit grinding.* The client refuses input until it holds the logged commit.
- *Profiling.* A per-person predictability model is ad-tech gold, so it is never sold, and research data is aggregated only.
- *Derealization.* "You escaped the simulation" copy courts the AI-psychosis pattern, so keep the tone playful and claim no proofs.

**90 days.**
- Weeks 1–3: a text prototype, accuracy measured on 200 testers.
- Weeks 4–8: a three-floor slice, receipts screen, and verification page.
- Weeks 9–12: a Next Fest demo. **Kill metric:** if a coin-flip bot or an always-predictable bot scores within 10% of top-decile humans, there's no skill gradient. Redesign the payoffs.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All three kills accepted. One addition to the kill metric: run a **mimic bot** that copies the population's median mix. If it reaches the top decile, players aren't reading tells, and selective legibility isn't a skill.

**Final form.** Seedless is a premium roguelite about being predicted. Before each move, the Demiurge (a per-player predictor with a population prior) commits a hashed prophecy that includes the drand round. Rooms are matching-pennies Adversary rooms or Newcomb Oracle rooms. Tells are probabilistic, and each floor discloses its ratio. Surprise pays in one room type and legibility in the other, so the skill is knowing which self to show. The house's mixing comes from the beacon, and CURBy pulses seal each floor. The receipt opens every prophecy: "It called 41 of your 60." The game adds seasonal retrains, a Solver League, and a Bell-settings festival that is never billed as proof.

**Moonshot (2036): prediction receipts as a civic right.** Recommenders, ad auctions and insurers predict us constantly and never show their work. Sealed prophecy becomes a standard: any system that acts on a prediction of your behaviour must commit to it before you act and open it afterwards. Your feed, bank and car send monthly receipts, like "We called 83% of your choices." Seedless is where people first *feel* modelled and learn to manage their legibility.

**Scores.** **Innovation 6.** An empirical Newcomb arena with cryptographic prophecies is a new mechanic, and the receipts institution is close to wild. But honestly we walked the seed down from "glitch in the Matrix" to a bold game, and the physics is now garnish. **Feasibility 6.** Godot, drand and an n-gram predictor ship the core. Live world-model rendering and calibrating payoffs against highly predictable humans are the risks.

### Turn 6 — Builder
The mimic bot is the right addition, and I'd build it before any art, because Seedless lives or dies on whether reading tells beats copying the crowd. My verdict: a real, shippable bold game whose genuinely new piece is the sealed prophecy, which turns Newcomb's problem into something you can lose at and audit. I'm scoring innovation 5, not 6, because commit-reveal and keypress oracles are known primitives and the quantum layer is now garnish. The receipts-as-civic-right moonshot is where I'd point the B2B SDK.
