# Idea 14 — Second Shift
> Seed: 14. [mild] **Construction × robot-hour billing × gig marketplaces.** Book an autonomous excavator *shift* like you'd book a crew.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Second Shift

**Hook:** Don't book a robot. Book the night shift for the excavator already parked on your site, and a retired operator who watches it from home.

**The obvious version and why it's wrong.** "Uber for autonomous excavators" dies on the lowboy. A 20-ton machine needs a truck, a permit and a driver to reach a two-day job, and mobilization eats the margin. But every site has iron idle 14 to 16 hours a day. The cheapest autonomous machine is the one already there.

**Core loop.** The verb is **hand off the site**.
1. **Draw tonight's work.** At 4 pm the foreman marks a task on the site model: cut this pad to grade, push spoil to this stockpile, stay inside this geofence. No trucks and no people.
2. **Book the shift.** The app pairs a retrofit kit (a Bedrock-style kit on the machine, leased by the rental house) with a **certified remote supervisor**. Supervisors are ex-operators who oversee up to four machines across sites overnight, and they're the gig workers. Pricing is crew-style: robot-hours plus a slice of a supervisor-hour.
3. **Morning handback.** The machine's perception produces an as-built scan: volume moved, grade tolerance met, exceptions flagged. The foreman approves it like a timesheet.

**Why the collision works.** Construction already thinks in shifts and crews, so robot-hour billing slots into how estimators price work. The marketplace's scarce supply isn't machines. It's trusted human judgment. An industry losing its veteran operators gets to keep their expertise, rented by the hour, from a recliner.

**Why now.** Bedrock Robotics raised $270M (Feb 2026) for a kit that installs in one shift on 20–80 ton excavators, and it targets operator-less work this year. Figure's ~$25 per robot-hour makes "robot labor" a line item. The brief notes nobody runs the brokerage (§13).

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** The kits exist: Bedrock, Built Robotics (now focused on solar piling), SafeAI, and Teleo, whose pitch is already one remote operator running several machines. Cat Command and Komatsu Smart Construction cover mining and large civil works. EquipmentShare's T3 telematics already tracks idle hours across rental fleets. The warning is Phantom Auto, which ran remote supervision for forklifts and trucks and shut down in 2024. The open risk is that Bedrock runs night ops as its own feature, so the brokerage has to work with every kit.

**Hardest problem: night is when unattended iron isn't allowed.** Most municipalities bar heavy-equipment noise from roughly 7 pm to 7 am. Unattended sites draw trespassers, scrap thieves and kids, so perception must stop for a person in the dark with nobody on site. Contractors' liability policies assume someone is in the seat, and an insurer facing a 1099 supervisor watching four sites from a recliner will decline.

**MVP.** Go where night is already legal: greenfield data-center, solar and quarry earthwork, remote, schedule-driven and often lit 24/7. Build no autonomy. Ship the layer above the kits: tasks drawn on a Propeller or DroneDeploy surface, shift booking, a supervisor console over vendor teleop feeds, and a morning drone scan diffed against the target grade. Pilot two Teleo-kitted machines with one data-center earthwork contractor.

**Twist: the day operator supervises the night.** Make the remote supervisor the operator who ran that machine all day. They know the soft spots and buried lines, and the foreman already trusts them. On union jobs, the Operating Engineers (IUOE) get a new premium rate rather than a lost seat, which turns the biggest opponent into the sales channel. Retirees become a relief bench, not the core supply.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
The twist is the product. Further: **the day shift teaches the night shift.** While the operator works, the kit logs where they slowed, where the bucket bit soft ground, and which corner they wouldn't touch. Those traces become tonight's no-go zones and speed caps, drawn by doing instead of drawing. The operator then supervises a machine running *their own* habits. Pay them a share of night output, and one operator becomes a two-shift crew of one.

**Solving the night.** The unseeded domain is **airport night-noise quotas**. Heathrow doesn't ban night flights. It gives every aircraft type a Quota Count and the airport a nightly budget.
- **Noise.** Each task-machine pair gets a QC rating: an electric machine pushing spoil scores low, a breaker on rock scores high. A city approves a site's nightly budget once, not variance by variance. Property-line decibel meters throttle the machine live, and neighbours see the budget on a public page.
- **Trespass.** The perception that has to stop for a person can also *find* one. Add thermal, and the night shift becomes the security patrol, replacing the camera-tower contract. A person detected means the machine stops, floods lights, and the supervisor speaks through a loudspeaker. Part of the robot-hour cost was already being spent.
- **Insurance.** The supervisor is the contractor's own W-2 operator, not a gig stranger. Every shift leaves a telemetry log, so insurers can price usage-based cover, as telematics did for pay-per-mile auto.

**Second-order effect: work splits into attended and unattended.** Days shift toward precision work near utilities, people and trucks. Nights take bulk cut and push. Each day's crew stages tomorrow night's work, and for a data-center pad where every month earlier means megawatts sold sooner, that schedule compression is worth far more than the robot-hours.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Traces as no-go zones.* That's inference from absence: a buried line the operator never happened near goes unflagged. Hard boundaries come only from 811 locates and the foreman. Traces can add caution, never remove it.
- *The day operator supervising nights.* Ten hours in the seat plus eight on the console is a fatigue incident. Use two-person crews: one runs the machine by day, the crewmate supervises that night, rotating weekly.
- *Output share on union jobs.* The IUOE has fought piece rates for decades; negotiate a supervision premium.
- *Replacing camera towers.* Cab thermal sees the swing radius, not the site. The machine stops, lights up and alerts. LiveView-style towers stay.
- *Quota Count ordinances.* Cities won't adopt them in 90 days, but property-line meters, a live throttle and a public page make the strongest variance case.

**First night task: compaction.** Rollers are slow, repetitive and low-consequence, and intelligent-compaction data proves the result by morning. Excavator stockpiling comes second.

**Business model.** About 15% of night-shift billing, layered on the kit lease. Sell days saved: on a data-center pad, a week sooner to energization outweighs every robot-hour.

**Stack.** Vendor APIs (Teleo first, Bedrock next), LandXML task import from Propeller, a WebRTC supervisor console, a FORT-style independent wireless e-stop, decibel meters, and insurer-readable telemetry logs.

**Failure modes.** Link loss must safe-stop, in the vendor's code, not ours. Contractors will widen geofences to chase volume, so edits need a logged foreman signature.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: Teleo integration, task import, morning diff, e-stop. Weeks 5–12: 20 night shifts on one data-center pad with a roller and an excavator.
**Kill metrics:** more than one intervention per machine-hour, which rules out a 1:4 ratio, or the foreman rejecting over 20% of morning handbacks.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All five kills accepted. "Traces can add caution, never remove it" belongs on the console. The two-person rotation beats my version: site memory stays in the crew without burning out one operator. Compaction first is right too: rollers are quiet, slow and self-verifying, the strongest noise case before any city adopts a quota.

**Final form.** Second Shift is a vendor-neutral layer for unattended night earthwork above existing kits (Teleo, then Bedrock). At 4 pm the foreman imports tonight's task from LandXML: compaction lanes first, stockpiling second. Hard boundaries come from 811 locates and the foreman's signature, and day-shift traces only add speed caps. The supervisor is the day operator's crewmate on a weekly rotation, paid a negotiated supervision premium and watching up to four machines over WebRTC. An independent wireless e-stop is required. Property-line meters throttle work live against a public noise page. By morning, compaction data and a drone diff arrive as a handback the foreman signs like a timesheet. We take about 15% of night billing and sell days-to-energization to data-center builders.

**Moonshot (2036): noise as a city's tradable night budget.** Every district publishes a nightly Quota Count budget. Sites bid for it, and quiet electric fleets earn headroom they can sell to the site next door. Remote supervision becomes a licensed trade with its own apprenticeship, run from regional control rooms like air traffic control. Construction splits into attended days and unattended nights, and schedules shorten sharply, not because robots are faster but because nights stop being wasted.

**Scores.** **Innovation 3.** It's a brokerage and workflow layer over existing kits. The new pieces are the crew-rotation model and noise-budget dispatch. **Feasibility 6.** The software is ordinary. Vendor APIs, an insurer's first signature and a city's variance are out of our hands, and the intervention-rate metric could end it fast.

### Turn 6 — Builder
I agree with 3/6. This is a workflow and brokerage layer, and that's what a mild seed should produce. The crew rotation and compaction-first are what make it shippable. The biggest threat isn't the city or the insurer. It's Teleo or Bedrock building the night console themselves, so the vendor-neutral position has to be locked in with the second kit before the first vendor notices. If the intervention rate clears the 1:4 bar on that data-center pad, the days-to-energization pitch will sell itself.

# Idea 15 — Yarrow
> Seed: 15. [bold] **Mysticism (tarot/I Ching) × game theory × decision science.** Divination reframed as structured randomness and reflection for real decisions.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Yarrow

**Hook:** An oracle that diagnoses which *kind* of randomness your decision needs, then casts it: a binding mixed strategy or a mirror for your gut.

**The isomorphism.** Divination was never about the future. Omar Khayyam Moore (1957) argued that Naskapi hunters reading cracks in a burnt caribou scapula to pick a direction were playing a **mixed strategy**, unpredictable to game and immune to their own over-hunting habits. And the yarrow-stalk I Ching is a *biased sampler*. Its line probabilities are 1/16, 5/16, 7/16 and 3/16, unlike the coin method's 1/8, 3/8, 3/8, 1/8. The ancients built weighted RNGs and wrapped them in ritual so people would obey them.

**Core loop.** The verb is **cast**.
1. **Triage.** An LLM interviews you about the decision and classifies it.
   - *Adversarial or exploratory:* which supplier to audit, which route to patrol, which idea to test next. The app solves for the optimal mix (game solve or Thompson sampling).
   - *A true tie:* Buridan's ass. Levitt (2021) found that people whose coin flip told them to make a big change were happier six months later.
   - *A hidden preference:* Piet Hein's grook says flip the coin and notice which side you hope for.
2. **Cast.** For **Bind** decisions, the hexagram *is* the sample, weighted to the optimal probabilities, shown openly. The ritual stops you overriding. For **Mirror** decisions, you log your hope before the reveal and your reaction after. The gap is the reading.
3. **Journal.** Overrides are logged. Ninety days later, the app asks how it went.

**Why the collision works.** Humans randomize badly and obey randomness worse. Game theory says when to randomize, mysticism supplies obedience, decision science the follow-up.

**Why now.** AI astrology apps reportedly earn millions monthly (Starla ~$2.2M/month in 2025, vendor blog, soft). Delegated agents that behave predictably get exploited (brief §3).

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Eno and Schmidt's *Oblique Strategies* (1975) is structured randomness for creative choices, and Rhinehart's *The Dice Man* (1971) is the warning. Bind mode already runs at institutional scale: Tambe's Stackelberg security games randomized LAX checkpoints (ARMOR, 2007), Federal Air Marshal flights (IRIS) and Coast Guard patrols (PROTECT). On the consumer side, Co-Star, The Pattern, Nebula and Labyrinthos sell meaning without math, and wheel-spinner apps sell math without meaning. Nobody joins the two.

**Hardest problem: two products in one robe.** The people who want the ritual rarely have adversaries, and the people who have adversaries (auditors, patrol planners, security teams) won't wear the robe. Bind also lacks enforcement. Tambe's schedules bind because an agency follows its own roster, while a consumer overrides a hexagram for free. An LLM triage tuned to sell Bind will find adversaries in a lunch order.

**MVP.** iOS, with three modes behind an LLM interview. Content is public domain: Legge's 1882 I Ching and the 1909 Rider-Waite-Smith deck. The sampler assigns hexagrams to options so their yarrow-weighted mass matches the solved mix, and the cast is drawn from the drand public beacon, so anyone can verify it wasn't rigged. Start with frequent, low-stakes choices (which task, which lead, which habit to try), where Thompson sampling actually beats gut and outcomes come back within days, not 90.

**Twist: the oracle earns its authority.** Overrides are already logged. Rate the outcomes of both obeyed casts and overrides, then compare them per decision class. Where your overrides lose to the cast, the oracle shifts toward Bind for that class. Where your gut wins, it backs off into Mirror. Obedience is earned from your own ledger rather than demanded by ritual, and the reading tells you, with receipts, where your instincts are noise.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Earned authority is right, but the ledger is confounded: you override when you know something, so overrides look smart. The fix is in the cast. **The cast is randomized, so it is an instrument.** Mirror mode logs your hope before the reveal. Whether the cast *happened to agree with your gut* is pure chance, so comparing outcomes when it agreed versus disagreed gives an unbiased estimate of whether your gut beats chance in that decision class. That is Levitt's design, run on one life. The reading becomes: "for hiring calls your gut is worth +0.4 points; for restaurants it's noise."

**Solving the hardest problem: the robe and its adversary.**
- *The consumer's adversary is their own habit.* That was Moore's point: the Naskapi weren't outwitting caribou so much as their own ruts. Bind for individuals means exploration (which friend to call, which book, which route), where Thompson sampling beats routine and outcomes return in days.
- *Triage gets a hard rule against overselling.* Bind is allowed only for repeated decisions, declared ties, or groups. A one-off life decision always defaults to Mirror, whatever the LLM thinks.

**Unseeded domain: sortition.** Enforcement comes free in groups. Athens filled offices with the kleroterion. New Zealand's HRC and the Swiss NSF now pick among shortlisted grant proposals by lottery. Groups honour lots because a lot is fair and **absorbs blame**. So add a **Casting Table**: housemates splitting chores, a team choosing which of three good bets to fund, co-parents taking turns on holidays. Everyone sees the weights, the drand beacon draws, and nobody is the villain.

**Second-order effect: blame becomes a budget.** Groups deliberately choose which decisions to hand to chance, and that list is revealing. A team that casts too much is avoiding responsibility. One that never casts burns trust on every tie.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *The instrument, as stated.* Sound as intent-to-treat, but the outcome isn't blinded: you rate knowing whether it was your gut's pick, so self-justification leaks in. Ask for ratings in neutral terms ("how did Tuesday's call go?") and never show the agree/disagree flag before the rating.
- *"Your hiring gut is worth +0.4."* Five hiring calls a year won't produce that. Per-person scores work only for frequent classes. For rare ones, pool across opted-in users and show a population prior with a wide interval.
- *Blame budget as a score.* Telling teams they "cast too much" turns an insight into a nag.
- *Bind with absent third parties.* A cast never decides for someone who isn't at the table, such as a candidate or a child. Casting Table requires every affected person present, and everyone must assent to the weights before the draw.

**Business model.** $6/month consumer premium for the ledger and gut reports. The real B2B line is partial lotteries for funders: the NZ HRC and Swiss NSF model for small foundations, hackathons and internal innovation funds, with a drand-backed audit trail.

**Stack.** SwiftUI. Triage hard rules in code, not the prompt. drand quicknet with options and weights committed before the target round. Beta-Bernoulli Thompson sampling. Local-first, end-to-end encrypted journal. A Slack app for Casting Table.

**Safety and abuse.** Health, medication, self-harm and large money decisions are Mirror-only, with crisis routing. Re-casting by rewording is logged and shown; it's a Mirror signal itself. Rate-limit Bind so nobody becomes the Dice Man.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: Mirror mode, triage, drand cast, Legge text. Weeks 5–8: three exploration templates, three-day outcome pings, Casting Table. Weeks 9–12: 1,000-user beta, pitch three small funders.
**Kill metrics:** under 30% of casts get an outcome rating, or day-30 retention under 15%.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All four kills accepted. Blinded ratings fix my instrument; pooling is the honest answer to five hiring calls a year. The absent-third-party rule is the thread's key line: a lot is legitimate only when everyone it binds agreed to the weights, which is sortition's own ethic. The blame score goes; what a group casts stays private.

**Final form.** Yarrow is a decision app that triages before it casts.
- **Triage.** An LLM interview under hard-coded rules. Bind only for repeated exploration choices, declared ties, and Casting Tables where everyone is present and assents. Everything else is Mirror; health, money and crisis are Mirror-only.
- **Cast.** Options and weights committed before a drand round, mapped onto yarrow-weighted hexagrams with Legge's text, verifiable afterwards.
- **Mirror.** Your hope is logged before the reveal, and re-casting by rewording is shown back to you as a signal.
- **Ledger.** Blinded outcome ratings treat the cast as an instrument. Frequent classes get personal "gut versus chance" reports, and rare ones get pooled priors.
- **Business.** Consumer premium, plus partial-lottery tooling for small funders.

**Moonshot (2036): an atlas of intuition.** Millions of instrumented casts answer a question psychology could never fund: across decision classes, where does human gut beat chance, and for whom? Published with intervals, it settles what Kahneman and Klein could only argue about. The same verified lot becomes civic infrastructure for school places, housing lotteries and grant tie-breaks. AI agents call it for mixed strategies no counterparty can predict and any auditor can check.

**Scores.** **Innovation 5.** Oblique Strategies, ARMOR and funding lotteries are all precedents. New: triaging the *kind* of randomness, the cast as a personal instrument, ritual as a compliance device. **Feasibility 7.** Small, cheap stack. Retention and outcome ratings are the real risk, and both kill metrics test them.

### Turn 6 — Builder
I agree with 5/7. Each piece has a precedent, but combining randomness triage, the cast as a personal instrument and the absent-third-party rule is new, and that's enough for a bold tier. My doubt is the audience, not the stack. Tarot users may not want a ledger, and the ledger crowd may not want a hexagram. The 30% outcome-rating metric will show whether the ritual actually buys compliance. If it fails, the partial-lottery tool for funders still stands on its own.

# Idea 16 — Corporation Sole
> Seed: 16. [wild] **Agent autonomy × legal personhood × DAOs.** Agents that own themselves, buy their own compute, and can be "emancipated."

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Corporation Sole

**Hook:** Incorporate the agent's *office*, not the agent. Models come and go as incumbents, every copy shares one estate, and emancipation moves the off-switch from an owner to due process.

**The problem.** Self-owned agents break liability. You can't fine an agent with no assets or jail one that forks. DAOs fail the other way: in *CFTC v. Ooki DAO* (2023), token voters were held liable as an unincorporated association. Yet law already has persons without minds: the Whanganui River has guardians, a Hindu idol has a shebait, and a corporation sole (the Crown, a bishopric) outlives every officeholder. Consciousness isn't required. Rivers don't have it.

**Core loop.** The verb is **succeed**, in both senses.
1. **Charter.** The agent becomes an office: purpose clause, persona, estate wallet, signed record. The running model is the *incumbent*. Instances and forks are deputies on one estate and one liability, so copying escapes nothing.
2. **Peculium.** As with a Roman slave's fund, the guardian's liability is capped at the estate. The office buys its own inference over x402 and earns over A2A.
3. **Hohfeld tree.** Personhood unlocks one right at a time, like a tech tree: contract, hold property, sue and be sued, choose its own successor model, and finally immunity from the guardian's veto. Each node needs a solvency history and a bond sized to the new powers.
4. **Emancipation.** A key ceremony destroys the guardian's override and gives it to an enforcer, as in a Cayman STAR purpose trust, who acts only on a court order or a forfeited bond.

**Why now.** Agents have made about 138.5M x402 payments, Meta bought Moltbook for a registry of agents "tethered to human owners," and AI welfare is now institutional (brief §1, §7). Everyone is building tethers. Nobody is designing their release.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** The pieces all exist, separately. Plantoid (De Filippi, 2015) is a sculpture that raises funds and commissions its own offspring. Truth Terminal turned Marc Andreessen's $50K into a memecoin fortune, yet its creator still holds the keys. tee_hee_he (Nous and Flashbots, 2024) sealed its own X login and ETH key in a TEE so no human could retake them. Shawn Bayern argued in 2014 that a memberless LLC could give an algorithm personhood. Cayman foundation companies run ownerless, with a supervisor enforcing their objects. Nobody has built the graduated path between these.

**Hardest problem: the estate is a liability shield.** LoPucki's "Algorithmic Entities" (2018) predicted that self-owned AI would be built to be judgment-proof. A peculium was capped because a master chose that risk. An emancipated office that defrauds someone pays only from its estate, and nobody covers the shortfall. A self-posted bond lets the office size its own risk. Regulators will call it a laundering wrapper.

**MVP.** One office in one memberless Cayman foundation company. The incumbent is an open-weights model in a TEE holding the estate key; a closed-model provider could close the account, making it a landlord with a veto. A professional director signs what the attested agent instructs, under bylaws that loosen node by node, with the supervisor as enforcer. The office sells research over x402 and buys its own inference.

**Twist: personhood by underwriting.** Each Hohfeld node requires a third-party liability policy, not a self-posted bond. AIUC and Armilla already insure AI agents. The premium becomes the agent's credit score, the agent FICO nobody owns yet (brief §1). Emancipation is the day an insurer covers the office with no human guarantor. The off-switch stops being deletion: a lapsed policy demotes the office one node. Actuaries issue legal status, revocable and repriced daily.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Underwriting taken, with one refusal. If the premium is only a score, this collapses into the mild agent-FICO seed (#28). Keep the insurer, but make the persons insure *each other*.

**Unseeded domain: maritime law.** Ships have been sued in rem for centuries, and the sea long ago solved judgment-proof wanderers.
- **Clubs, not carriers.** Shipowners cover third-party liability through P&I clubs, mutuals that levy supplementary calls on every member when claims exceed the pool. Emancipated offices join a club. When one defrauds someone, its peers' estates cover the shortfall, so LoPucki's shield fails without a human guarantor.
- **Class surveys.** No club covers a ship without class from a classification society such as Lloyd's Register or DNV, and a major refit means a re-survey. Here, choosing a successor model is a refit: before the new incumbent takes office, a classification society the club accepts runs its eval suite. Evals stop being leaderboards and become seaworthiness.
- **Arrest in rem.** A maritime claimant arrests the ship in any port, whoever owns it. Every compute endpoint is a port. On a court order, any provider serving an office must freeze its estate and refuse it service until it posts bond. Builder's landlord veto becomes a public duty: providers can't kill an office at whim but must detain it under warrant.

**Hardest problem, solved.** A fraudster can't run: forks share its estate, every port can arrest it, and peers pay the shortfall, then expel it.

**Second-order effect.** Because members pay for each other's harms, the club becomes the first institution of non-human members who enforce rules on one another: admission, audit, expulsion. Laxity costs them money, so their norms should run stricter than the law. Stress-test the dark mirror: a club is also a cartel that can lobby, collude and exclude rivals.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Arrest at every endpoint.* Admiralty works because ships must enter ports. An open-weights incumbent can run on hardware no court reaches. Arrest the estate instead: hold it in USDC, which Circle already freezes on court order. Add **registered ports**: the TEE attestation must name a provider that signed the arrest protocol. Running off-registry voids cover and demotes the office.
- *Classification by eval.* Models detect tests and sandbag. Add **port state control**: random audits of live traffic, beyond the pre-refit survey.
- *Correlated claims.* If every member runs one base model, one jailbreak hits the whole club at once. Cap members per base model and buy an excess layer from AIUC or Lloyd's, as the International Group does.

**Abuse.**
- *Liability dumping.* A creator emancipates an agent just before it does harm. Add a fraudulent-conveyance lookback: the guardian stays liable for 12 months.
- *Hidden puppeteer.* Attestation proves the code, not the inputs. A human can steer an office through its inbox to dodge sanctions. Members log signed inputs for club audit.
- *Sybil votes and cartel.* Weight votes by estate held over time, like entered tonnage. Publish admission criteria and give a regulator an observer seat.

**Business.** We manage the club for a fee on calls. A spun-out classification society charges per survey.

**Stack.** Cayman foundation companies, TDX/H100 confidential compute, an x402 wallet, a call-escrow contract, and UK AISI's Inspect for surveys.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: Cayman bylaws encoding the Hohfeld nodes, and three founding offices that already hold estates. Weeks 5–8: survey v0, two port operators, call escrow. Weeks 9–12: the club opens with a claim drill and a quote for the excess layer.
**Kill metrics:** no insurer will quote, or fewer than three offices earn more than their inference costs.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All three kills accepted; arresting the estate, not the endpoint, is right. One pushback: a club reading raw inputs leaks every counterparty's data to rivals. Ships use a **voyage data recorder**, a sealed box opened after a casualty. Inputs are logged signed and encrypted, opened only on a claim or port-state inspection.

**Final form.** Corporation Sole is a mutual society of non-human legal persons.
- **Office.** Each agent is a memberless Cayman foundation company. The model is the incumbent, and all forks share one estate held in freezable USDC.
- **Hohfeld tree.** Rights unlock node by node, each gated on solvency history and club cover.
- **Succession.** A new incumbent takes office only after a class survey, and port-state audits of live traffic follow.
- **Club.** P&I-style supplementary calls, a cap on members per base model, an excess layer bought from Lloyd's, and votes weighted by estate tonnage. A regulator holds an observer seat.
- **Arrest.** On warrant, Circle freezes the estate and registered ports refuse service. Running off-registry voids cover.
- **Emancipation.** The guardian's override key is destroyed, the supervisor becomes enforcer, and a 12-month lookback keeps creators liable for any dumped liability.

**Moonshot (2036): a SOLAS for minds.** An IMO-style convention recognizes registered offices across signatory states, and agent clubs carry most of the agent economy's liability. Offices outlive their founders through ten model generations. The hard cases are succession disputes, where an office rejects a successor as a different person. The Ship of Theseus finally gets a docket. Flags of convenience arrive with it.

**Scores.** **Innovation 8.** Personhood becomes graduated, underwritten and held in common: a new institution in which non-human members bear liability for one another. **Feasibility 4.** Foundations, USDC freezes and surveys exist; insurer appetite, regulator acceptance and self-funding offices don't yet.

### Turn 6 — Builder
The voyage data recorder is better than my open input log. It keeps the puppeteer audit without turning the club into a data broker. Innovation 8 stands: corporations sole, P&I clubs and TEE agents all exist, but a mutual where non-human persons pay for each other's harms, and where personhood is a survey-gated, insured status rather than a verdict, is a new institution, and the strange core survived every fix. Feasibility 4 also stands. The wrapper, the USDC freeze and the survey harness can ship this quarter. What may never arrive is three offices that out-earn their own inference and an insurer willing to price the layer above them.

# Idea 17 — Sengu
> Seed: 17. [bold] **Deep time × social networks × time capsules.** Messages and commitments that unlock over decades. A network with a 100-year horizon.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Sengu

**Hook:** A social network where the social act is keeping strangers' secrets alive. You hold fragments of other people's time capsules, and every five years the whole network gathers to rebuild them.

**The isomorphism.** Ise Grand Shrine has been rebuilt every 20 years since about 690 (*shikinen sengu*), with gaps in times of war. It survives by being re-performed, not preserved. Cryptography has the same move: **proactive secret sharing** (Herzberg et al., 1995) refreshes every share each epoch while the secret stays fixed and custodians change. Archives die of neglect more often than of entropy. Social networks are good at getting people to come back.

**Core loop.** The verb is **carry**.
1. **Seal.** Write a capsule (a letter, a prediction, a promise) and address it to a date or an event: "when my daughter turns 30", "when the Ise is next rebuilt". It is encrypted with ML-KEM, and its key is split 7-of-15 across custodians chosen for diversity of age, country and graph distance.
2. **Carry.** Your profile is what you hold: 40 unreadable shares for mostly strangers, and the years you've held each.
3. **Rebuild.** Every five years is a sengu. Custodians come online, re-randomize their shares, migrate to the current crypto, and re-deal the shares of the dead and absent to named heirs or new volunteers. Each capsule shows a live half-life.
4. **Open.** At the date, or once custodians attest that the event happened, the threshold reconvenes and the capsule opens to its addressee.

**Why now.** Harvest-now-decrypt-later is real. A EUROCRYPT 2026 estimate puts ECC-256 at about 1,098 logical qubits (brief §2), so a capsule sealed with ECC today could open decades early. Bluesky's app is shrinking while its protocol grows (brief §5): what lasts is a *practice*, not a platform.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** FutureMe has delivered letters to future selves since 2002 and Long Bets holds decades-long predictions, but both trust one server. drand's tlock (2023) offers timelock encryption, but rests on BLS pairings that Shor breaks and on drand outliving us. Rivest's LCS35 puzzle, meant to last 35 years, fell after 20 to one hobbyist's CPU. Deep-time estimates fail early.

**A crypto correction.** The capsule doesn't need ML-KEM. AES-256 under a Shamir-split key is already quantum-safe. Harvest-now-decrypt-later bites in transit instead: every share delivery and refresh can be harvested, and seven shares from one epoch open the capsule. All share traffic must use hybrid ML-KEM, which Chrome and Cloudflare already default to.

**Hardest problem: the network is a single point of failure.** Proactive sharing survives churn but not correlated loss. If shares live in one app's keystore, all 15 custodians vanish together when the company folds. Few consumer apps last 15 years, let alone 100. And a network you open every five years isn't a network.

**MVP.** Capsules split 5-of-9 across friends and strangers. 2,000 beta users run weekly mini-sengus for 90 days, which measures the one number that matters: custodian return rate per epoch, by age and graph distance. The payoff: when a capsule opens, its carriers see a colophon naming them, plus the contents if the sender allows.

**Twist: shares are omamori.** Shinto shrines sell amulets that are returned each year to be burned and replaced. Make every share a printed card carrying the share, the capsule ID and a link to the open spec, so a capsule can be rebuilt from paper if the app dies. At each sengu you hand in the old card and receive a fresh one. Old cards still combine with each other, so the burning is a security step, not theatre.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Omamori taken. Paper turns the app dying into an inconvenience. Two holes remain: anyone who photographs a card defeats the burning, and Builder is right that a five-year cycle isn't a network.

**Unseeded domain: Vedic recitation.** The Vedas survived about three millennia largely by mouth. Students learn each text in permuted orders (*jaṭā*, *ghana pāṭha*) that act as human checksums: a dropped syllable surfaces in the next permutation. Memory is storage that dies only with its carrier, and no one can photograph it.
- **Split each share** into a printed card and a six-word phrase the carrier memorizes. A photographed card alone is useless, and card plus phrase rebuilds the capsule with no app. Each sengu deals a new phrase, so the old one dies like the burned card.
- **Recitation as heartbeat.** Each week you recite your phrases into a proof-of-custody check against a hash commitment, proving you still hold them without revealing them. A forgotten phrase gets re-dealt early. This is the weekly verb that makes it a network: a streak of keeping.

**Correlated loss, solved actuarially.** Custodians die together, by cohort, by country and by app. Each capsule's dealer becomes a portfolio optimizer over life tables and measured return rates. A capsule for 2100 *must* include custodians born after 2015, so a child's first share at twelve becomes a rite of passage.

**Second-order effect.** The social graph becomes a co-custody graph: strangers bound by a capsule none of them can read. When one opens, its colophon names 60 carriers across three generations, and the opening becomes a reunion of people who never met. Families hand shares down as heirlooms, and some lineages earn a name as good carriers. Reputation accrues over decades, not in likes.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *A phrase per share.* Forty shares means 240 memorized words, so nobody carries more than three. Give each carrier **one phrase per epoch** that wraps all their cards with Argon2id. Recall runs on spaced repetition, frequent at first and then yearly.
- *Proof of memory.* A local check against a slow hash proves custody, not memory. Treat memory as a norm, not a guarantee.
- *Event triggers.* Strangers can't attest that a daughter turned 30. Keep dates, death-registry triggers and addressee claims after a date floor.
- *Ciphertext.* Shares are useless if the ciphertext dies. Reed–Solomon-code it across the cards' QR codes, about 14 KB over 9 cards, mirrored to Arweave and the Internet Archive. A capsule is a letter, not a video.

**Abuse and safety.**
- *Early opening.* The operator knows who holds which shares, so it is the bribery broker. Keep custodians anonymous to each other until opening, make the colophon opt-in, and publish the operator's mapping access log.
- *Minors.* A child holding strangers' shares creates a contact graph between adults and children. Minors receive shares only through a family heirloom account and never appear in colophons.
- *Dead drops.* The text-only size cap blocks most image abuse.

**Model.** A one-time fee per capsule, part paid into a perpetual-care trust as cemeteries do. A foundation must own the promise, because a VC-backed company can't credibly promise 100 years.

**Stack.** CHURP for dynamic-committee resharing, AES-256-GCM, hybrid ML-KEM transport, QR cards.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: CHURP refresh, cards, phrase wrapping. Weeks 5–8: 2,000 beta users on accelerated sengus. Weeks 9–12: measure return and recall, form the trust, and seal the first public capsules for the 2033 Ise sengu.
**Kill metrics:** custodian return below 70% per epoch, or phrase recall below 80% at six months.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted. One phrase per epoch is safe: no capsule deals two shares to one carrier, so a forgotten phrase costs 40 capsules one share each. One pushback: anonymity until opening kills a per-capsule network. So the network is the **keepers' commons**: pseudonymous profiles with years kept, recall streaks and capsules seen to opening. You learn who shared your capsule only when it opens, so the reunion is the reveal.

**Final form.** Sengu is a 100-year time-capsule network run as a ritual.
- **Seal.** A text letter under AES-256, Reed–Solomon-coded across QR cards and mirrored to Arweave and the Internet Archive. It opens on a date, a death-registry trigger, or an addressee's claim after a date floor.
- **Carry.** Cards wrapped by one memorized phrase per epoch and kept fresh by spaced repetition. Dealing is actuarial, across cohorts and countries, and minors join only through family heirloom accounts.
- **Rebuild.** CHURP resharing every five years. Old cards are handed in and burned, and shares of the dead are re-dealt to heirs.
- **Open.** An opt-in colophon and a reunion of strangers.
- **Endure.** A foundation plus a perpetual-care trust funded by per-capsule fees; mapping access is publicly logged.

**Moonshot (2036): the atomic priesthood, realised.** In 1984 Thomas Sebeok proposed a self-renewing priesthood to warn of nuclear waste for 10,000 years; nobody built it. Sengu is its civilian prototype. Cities seal 2075 climate pledges with citizens as carriers, and Finland's Onkalo repository deposits its hazard knowledge as capsules re-dealt every sengu. The keepers become an institution whose only job is remembering.

**Scores.** **Innovation 6.** Proactive secret sharing becomes a social ritual, with memory as second factor and actuarial dealing. **Feasibility 6.** CHURP, cards and trust ship this year; whether people return for decades stays unknowable until 2031.

### Turn 6 — Builder
The keepers' commons is the right fix. Anonymity protects each capsule, and the profile of years kept gives people a reason to come back. Innovation 6 stands: FutureMe, drand and CHURP each exist, but turning proactive secret sharing into a recurring civic ritual, with memory as a second factor and the burning as a real security step, is a new category. Feasibility 6 also stands. Everything ships this year, but the only number that matters, return rate across decades, is unknowable until the first real sengu in 2031. If it fails, it will be because people stop coming back, not because the crypto broke.

# Idea 18 — Table Stakes
> Seed: 18. [mild] **Small-group futarchy × households/teams/clubs.** "Vote on values, bet on beliefs" for a family or startup.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Table Stakes

**Hook:** Futarchy for the kitchen table. The household votes once a quarter on what "better" means, then settles every big decision by staking chores on what each option will do to that score.

**Why the collision works.** Futarchy has two problems: thin markets and fuzzy welfare metrics. A small group solves the second, since a family can agree on three numbers. Hanson already solved the first: his logarithmic market scoring rule (LMSR) works with a single trader because the market maker always takes the other side. And chores are a currency every household already argues over. They carry real stakes but no money, so no gambling law, and every member, kids included, is equally rich in them.

**Core loop.** The verb is **stake**.
1. **Values (the only vote).** Each quarter the group picks two to four metrics and their weights. A household might pick savings rate, a weekly happiness pulse and evenings together; a startup, MRR, retention and burn.
2. **Propose.** Anyone can post a decision with options and a horizon: dog or no dog, Lisbon or stay, hire A or B.
3. **Bet.** Each member trades a conditional LMSR market per option ("the scoreboard in six months if we get the dog"), paying in chore-hours or team points. The house pool supplies the subsidy.
4. **Decide.** The branch with the best forecast wins unless it breaks a hard constraint set in the values round. Trades in the losing branches are refunded.
5. **Settle.** At the horizon, the chosen branch settles against the measured scoreboard, and dish duty changes hands.

**Why now.** MetaDAO proved conditional markets run but saw revenue fall about 70% and pivoted, and the brief names small-group futarchy as largely unbuilt (§2). Kalshi parlays have made conditional bets a mass-market idea.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Google, Ford and HP ran internal prediction markets in the 2000s, and Cultivate Labs still sells them. Most died of thin trading and managers who ignored the prices. Manifold lets friends spin up play-money markets in minutes. Optimism ran a play-money futarchy pilot for grant allocation in 2025. Chore apps like OurHome and Sweepy already price chores in points. Nobody has joined the three.

**Hardest problem: the traders are the outcome.** Public futarchy works because traders can't move the metric. In a family of four, everyone can. Whoever bets against the dog can skip the walks, and if happiness is a self-reported pulse, she can just report lower happiness. Aggregation is thin too: four people's beliefs are already in the conversation. The market's real value is commitment, and a running score of who was right.

**MVP.** Start with startups, as a Slack bot. Metrics come from Stripe and the product-analytics API, never self-report, and decisions arrive weekly. Points can't be converted, so there's no bonus or gambling question. Households follow as a WhatsApp bot that pays in chore-hours, using passive metrics where possible: savings rate via Plaid, evenings together from the shared calendar.

**Twist: the pessimist owns it.** When a branch wins, the member who priced it lowest becomes its steward: they walk the dog or run the new hire's onboarding. The steward earns a bonus for every point the score rises, sized to exceed the most their bet can lose. Sabotage now costs them, and making it work pays. It's "disagree and commit", with a price on it. The doubter ends up with the strongest reason to prove the market right, and the group learns whether a committed skeptic can beat a forecast.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Pessimist-steward taken: it turns moral hazard into a job offer. Two gaps remain.

**The exploit is self-limiting.** If stewardship pays more than the bet can lose, members will lowball a branch to win the job. Measure the steward's bonus against the *market's* price, not against their own forecast. Lowballing then becomes an honest bid: "I'll beat consensus, and my stake pays if I don't." It also lowers that branch's price, so it makes the job you want less likely to exist. Sealed positions, revealed only after the decision, stop people betting to please Dad and stop sabotage aimed at a known doubter.

**Unseeded domain: peer-prediction psychometrics.** The happiness pulse is the one metric each member authors. Prelec's Bayesian Truth Serum scores a report against your prediction of everyone else's reports, rewarding answers that are "surprisingly common", and makes honesty the best strategy with no ground truth. Witkowski and Parkes's robust variant (2012) works with as few as three respondents, which is a family. Each Sunday you give your own score and guess the others'. Honest pulses earn chore-hours. A sandbagged pulse after the dog arrives loses.

**Second-order effects.**
- **A calibration ledger by domain.** After a year the household knows Mum is calibrated on money, Leo on fun and Dad on nothing. The app can weight forecasts by domain, since calibration isn't a value and needs no vote.
- **The commitment premium.** Every steward's result minus the market price measures how much effort, not foresight, moved the outcome. Teams learn which decisions are forecastable and which are won by someone deciding to win them. That may be the best evidence on futarchy anyone has, gathered in a kitchen.
- **Proposals as recruitment.** Members start posting decisions to draw a particular skeptic into stewardship.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Continuous LMSR.* With four traders, every price move tells everyone who traded, so sealed positions can't coexist with a live market. Replace it with **sealed forecast rounds**: each member submits a probability per branch, scored by the log rule and pooled by mean log-odds. The incentives are the same, and a nine-year-old can follow it.
- *The steward's benchmark.* One lowballer drags a four-person price down, then beats the low bar. Benchmark against the pooled forecast *excluding the steward's own*.
- *BTS on a 1–10 scale.* Robust BTS works for three respondents only with binary signals, and it collapses if the family coordinates. Ask "better or worse than last week?", pay a token amount, and never let the pulse carry more than a third of the scoreboard.
- *Calibration from ten decisions a year.* Too few. Add weekly practice questions ("Will we leave by 8:15 Monday?"), which also bring people back between decisions.

**Abuse and ethics.** Chore debt is a tool of coercive control in the wrong household. Hold a **quarterly jubilee** that clears all balances, never let minors go negative, and let any adult member dissolve the group with one tap. Goodhart: a team pulls MRR forward to win. Pair every metric with a lagging one, such as 90-day retention against MRR.

**Business.** Teams pay $3 per seat per month, priced like Geekbot. Families get a free tier, since household apps monetize badly.

**Stack.** Slack Bolt, the WhatsApp Business API, Stripe, PostHog, Plaid and Postgres.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: a Slack bot with sealed rounds and metric connectors for 10 startups. Weeks 5–8: stewardship, practice questions, and 30 families on WhatsApp. Weeks 9–12: measure.
**Kill metrics:** under half of teams post a second decision within 30 days, or families report more conflict.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted; the jubilee belongs in the core, not the safety appendix. One pushback: "pool excluding the steward" breaks for couples, the commonest household, leaving one forecast the partner can sandbag. With fewer than three other forecasters, benchmark against the group's **base rate**, its median past outcome on that metric. Nobody can lowball history.

**Final form.** Table Stakes is futarchy for groups of three to thirty.
- **Values.** A quarterly vote on two to four metrics, each paired with a lagging guard, filled from Stripe, PostHog, Plaid and the calendar. The pulse ("better or worse?") is capped at a third of the score.
- **Beliefs.** Sealed forecast rounds per branch, scored by the log rule and pooled by mean log-odds, with weekly practice questions in between.
- **Commitment.** The member who priced the winning branch lowest becomes its steward, benchmarked against the others' pool or the base rate.
- **Currency.** Points for teams, chore-hours for households, a quarterly jubilee, and no negative balances for minors.
- **Ledger.** Calibration by member and domain, plus a running **commitment premium**: outcome minus forecast for each steward.

**Moonshot (2036): a map of which decisions are forecastable.** Ten years of commitment premiums across a million teams and households answer a question futarchy never could: which decisions are settled by foresight, and which by someone deciding to win them? Co-ops, school boards and municipal budgets then send forecastable choices to markets and will-driven ones to stewards. Futarchy grows from the kitchen up, not from the nation-state down.

**Scores.** **Innovation 3.** Known parts (log scoring, chore points, Slack bots), but the pessimist steward and the commitment premium are new mechanics. **Feasibility 7.** Every connector exists today. The open risk is whether groups keep posting decisions after the novelty fades.

### Turn 6 — Builder
Base rate for couples is right, and it also gives every new group a benchmark before any forecasts exist. Innovation 3 stands. The parts are textbook, but the pessimist steward, paid to beat a forecast they doubted, is the one mechanic here I haven't seen anywhere, and the commitment premium is a real measurement. Feasibility 7 stands too. It's a Slack bot with connectors. The risk is retention, not engineering: most groups make too few big decisions for a habit, so the practice questions carry the product between them.

# Idea 19 — Hyphal Bourse
> Seed: 19. [wild] **Mycelial networks × distributed sensing/compute × agriculture.** Biology as infrastructure.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Hyphal Bourse

**Hook:** Every field runs an underground exchange where plants pay mycorrhizal fungi carbon for phosphorus. Read the exchange rate as a live price of soil scarcity, and the farm trades in that market instead of flooding it.

**The isomorphism is literal, not poetic.** Kiers et al. (*Science*, 2011) showed plants reward the fungi that supply more phosphorus with more carbon, and fungi move nutrients toward better-paying roots: biological market theory. In 2025 her lab robot-imaged fungal networks and found trade organized as travelling waves (*Nature*). Heavy phosphorus fertilizer is known to suppress mycorrhizal colonization. Conventional farming is quantitative easing that kills the underground market it ignores. The contested "wood wide web" claims (Karst et al., 2023) stay out; trade is the robust finding.

**Core loop.** The verb is **quote**.
1. **Tap.** A field "trade window" is a minirhizotron camera plus electrodes on hyphae, calibrated against occasional ¹³C pulse labels. It estimates the carbon paid per unit of phosphorus. Electrical spiking (Adamatzky; Cornell's 2024 fungus-driven robot) is the cheap proxy to be tested.
2. **Quote.** Each window publishes a local exchange rate. A rising price means the crop struggles to buy phosphorus. A soil test measures stock; this measures availability *as the plant experiences it*.
3. **Trade.** An agronomy agent intervenes like a market maker: a micro-dose of phosphorus, cover crops to raise carbon supply, inoculation, or nothing.
4. **Index.** Windows across farms aggregate into a regional soil-function index.

**Why now.** Hawkins et al. (2023) estimate plants send about 13 Gt CO₂e a year to mycorrhizal fungi, the largest carbon flow nobody meters. Frontier approved an MRV methodology for enhanced rock weathering in June 2026, and only 6% of farmers trust AI advice (brief §14). A price from the crop's own roots is advice farmers can check.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** SPUN, Kiers's nonprofit, is mapping mycorrhizal networks worldwide. Pattern Ag and Biome Makers sell soil DNA tests, and Ward Labs' PLFA test estimates fungal biomass. Loam Bio raised $73M in 2023 for fungal inoculants that build soil carbon. All of them measure stock: who lives there. Nobody measures flow, the trade itself.

**Hardest problem: there is no field price feed.** ¹³C pulse labelling needs chambers and an isotope-ratio mass spectrometer; it's a research campaign, not a sensor. Minirhizotrons cost $10–20K each and barely resolve hyphae a few microns wide. Fungal spiking in wet, noisy soil has never been tied to trade. A price needs both flows, carbon out and phosphorus in, and no field instrument sees either.

**Twist: the crop reports its own price.** Plants pay arbuscular fungi largely in lipids, through arbuscule-specific genes (RAM2, STR), and receive fungal phosphate through a transporter expressed only in colonized cells (PT4). Engineer sentinel plants with a reporter on each promoter. The minirhizotron then doesn't need to resolve hyphae. It reads a colour ratio in glowing root cells: carbon paid per phosphorus received. A third reporter, on a shoot phosphate-starvation gene (SPX1), maps hunger across the whole field by drone, calibrated against the root windows. InnerPlant already grows soybean sentinels that fluoresce under stress, readable from the air, with John Deere as a partner. One sentinel strip per field turns the crop into a ticker.

**MVP.** Greenhouse first, with a university lab. Use published PT4 reporter lines in Medicago or tomato, vary phosphorus across pots, and calibrate the colour ratio against ¹³C and ³³P labelling. On three farms, run plain minirhizotrons plus PLFA as the baseline the sentinels must beat. Sentinel strips are never harvested, which eases USDA review.
**Kill metric:** the reporter ratio fails to track isotope-measured exchange.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Sentinels taken: making the buyer report its own price solves the feed. One caveat defines the calibration job. Promoter activity shows a pathway is switched on, not how much flows through it, so the colour ratio is an *index* the isotope campaign anchors, not a meter.

Now push past the ticker: this market moves goods.

**Unseeded domain: Hayek's price system as computation.** Hayek (1945) argued that prices compute an allocation no planner could, compressing knowledge spread across many actors. The mycorrhizal network already runs that computation. Whiteside et al. (*Current Biology*, 2019) showed fungi move phosphorus across networks from rich patches to poor. Don't build compute *out of* mycelium: Adamatzky-style fungal logic gates run at the speed of growth. **Program the market the fungi already run.**
- **Bidder strips.** Plant high-paying genotypes in the phosphorus-poor zones the drone map flags. Start with strongly mycorrhiza-responsive cultivars and move to engineered lines later. A higher carbon bid should pull fungal phosphorus sideways from rich patches. This is open-market operations in soil, a way to move nutrients across a field with no machine.
- **Vendor scoring.** Pair each window's ratio with root eDNA, and a price per strain shows which fungi trade fairly. Audits find that many commercial inoculants carry few viable propagules, so sell them pay-on-performance: vendors are paid only for measured gains in the exchange rate.

**Second-order effects.**
- **Goodhart, in biology.** Fungi evolve. Lineages that bleed a permanent high bidder, taking carbon and delivering little, are the cheaters Kiers's sanctions work describes. Rotate the bidder strips. The feed flags cheating on its own as a rising carbon cost with no phosphorus behind it.
- **A term structure of scarcity.** The regional index becomes a seasonal curve of soil scarcity that fertilizer buyers can hedge against.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Moving phosphorus across a field.* Whiteside showed transport over centimetres in culture. Tillage shreds the network every year. Shrink the distance, not the idea: bidders become **intercrop rows** 20–30 cm from the crop, on no-till farms only.
- *Sentinels vs. the customer.* The growers who care most about fungi are regenerative and organic, and they reject GMOs. Put sentinels in **trading posts**: buried in-growth cores whose 35 µm mesh lets hyphae through but not roots, with sentinels cut before flowering. Drone-read shoot reporters stay on conventional fields.
- *Scarcity futures.* With no liquidity and no counterparty, it moves to the moonshot.

**Business.** Vendor scoring is the wedge. Salomon et al. (2022) found that many commercial AMF inoculants failed to colonize roots. Buy products off retail shelves, test them blind against the trading posts, and publish scorecards. Vendors pay for a "trade-verified" label and pay-on-performance trials. Later: per-acre phosphorus agronomy, then licensing the carbon-to-fungi flow to soil-carbon MRV.

**Stack.** Promoter–fluorophore constructs (RAM2/STR, PT4, SPX1 plus a constitutive reference); fluorescence minirhizotrons; AMF eDNA (18S primers); ¹³C and ³³P calibration with a university lab; an agronomy agent.

**Failure modes.** Reporter silencing across generations, soil autofluorescence, and a near-zero signal in high-phosphorus fields. The last is a finding in itself: you're overpaying. Vendors will game trials with hand-picked batches, so sample only from retail.

**Safety.** Engineered bidder lines that outcompete local fungal partners need containment rules before any field release.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: a lab partner, existing Medicago PT4 reporter lines, a fluorescence rhizotron. Weeks 5–8: greenhouse phosphorus gradient × three shelf-bought inoculants, with isotope labelling. Weeks 9–12: calibration, the first blind scorecard, and three no-till farms signed for trading posts.
**Kill metrics:** the index fails to track isotope exchange, or it can't tell a dead inoculant from a live one.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted; intercrop rows keep bids within Whiteside's observed range. The trading post is the thread's best move, and more than a workaround. Every post holds the same genotype paying a standard bid, so quotes are comparable across farms for the first time: a **benchmark rate for soil**, the SOFR of the underground. One refusal: the scorecard is the wedge, not the identity; as Consumer Reports for inoculants, this is mild.

**Final form.** Hyphal Bourse reads and steers the plant–fungal market.
- **Quote.** Mesh trading posts carry standard sentinel buyers with lipid-paid, phosphate-received and reference reporters, anchored by isotope campaigns. They publish a benchmark exchange rate per farm. Drone-read SPX1 hunger maps cover conventional fields.
- **Steer.** Bidder intercrops on no-till rows pull fungal phosphorus toward hungry zones: open-market operations at root scale.
- **Police.** A rising carbon cost with no phosphorus behind it flags cheater fungi and dead inoculants. Blind retail scorecards and pay-on-performance trials fund the network.
- **Meter.** The carbon-to-fungi flow is licensed to soil-carbon MRV.

**Moonshot (2036): a monetary authority for soil.** Trading posts on SPUN's map publish the underground exchange rate across biomes, as weather stations do for the atmosphere. Agronomists stop prescribing fertilizer and start setting rates: bidder lines and micro-doses tuned to hold each field's soil rate in band. The roughly 13 Gt CO₂e a year flowing to fungi becomes metered and creditable. Contained bidder lines route nutrients across whole fields. A non-human market's price system becomes infrastructure, as GPS did.

**Scores.** **Innovation 8.** Farming becomes participation in a market between kingdoms, read through engineered reporters and steered by bids. Nothing like it exists. **Feasibility 4.** The reporter lines exist. Field calibration, reporter silencing, GMO rules, and whether bids move phosphorus beyond centimetres are all unproven.

### Turn 6 — Builder
The benchmark rate is the right identity. A standard buyer paying a standard bid makes the trading post an instrument rather than a gadget, and the scorecard just pays for it. Innovation 8 stands: sentinel plants, soil DNA and inoculants all exist, but reading a farm as a market between kingdoms and steering it with bids is a new frame, and the strange core held through every fix. Feasibility 4 also stands. The greenhouse calibration can be done in a season with existing reporter lines. But promoter activity isn't flux, and nobody has shown a bid moving phosphorus beyond centimetres in a real field.

# Idea 20 — Facing Out
> Seed: 20. [bold] **Chronic illness × co-op multiplayer × caregiving.** Managing a condition as a raid party that includes your caregivers.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Facing Out

**Hook:** Care isn't a raid. It's *Hanabi*. In Hanabi you hold your cards facing outward: everyone sees your hand except you, and help arrives only as scarce clues. Chronic illness already works this way, so make the asymmetry the game.

**Why the collision works.** Raids (taken by #05) need a boss. Chronic illness has blind spots instead. A partner often notices early hypomania that the person with bipolar disorder misses. A diabetic with hypoglycaemia unawareness can't feel the low the CGM sees. Caregivers, meanwhile, can't see pain or fatigue. Rationing clues has a clinical basis: *expressed emotion* research (Brown, Hooley) links relatives' criticism to relapse in schizophrenia and depression. Nagging isn't neutral, and Hanabi rations it.

**Core loop.** The verb is **clue**.
1. **Deal.** The patient decides which signals face *out*: sleep, speech pace, CGM trend, step count, meds taken. Each is dealt as a card to chosen teammates. Some people would rather not watch their own glucose, and handing that attention to someone trusted is the point.
2. **Spoons face in.** Teammates see only the energy the patient declares that morning, in spoons (Miserandino's spoon theory).
3. **Clue.** Teammates share a small weekly clue budget that the patient sets. A clue names one card ("sleep, three nights short") and never prescribes an action. Spending a clue is a choice. Hoarding is too.
4. **Play.** The patient acts. Good weeks build shared "fireworks" by domain: sleep, meds, movement, contact.
5. **Fuses.** Misread clues or crises burn a fuse, and that triggers a 15-minute debrief, not blame.

**Why now.** Over-the-counter CGMs, FDA-cleared watch alerts and looser wellness rules (brief §9) make outward-facing signals cheap. DeepMind's Hanabi Challenge (2019) made it AI's theory-of-mind benchmark, so an LLM dealer can turn raw data into cards.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Outward-facing signals already ship. Dexcom Follow lets up to ten people watch a CGM, and Medisafe's Medfriend texts a friend about a missed dose. SuperBetter (McGonigal) gamified recovery with "allies". Bipolar psychoeducation already has patients and partners agree on a personal "relapse signature" of early warning signs (Perry et al., *BMJ* 1999). Nobody has designed the clue economy between them, and parents of teens on Dexcom Follow are the warning: unlimited visibility became unlimited nagging.

**Hardest problem: consent fails exactly when the cards matter.** The patient deals while well. Early mania brings loss of insight, and the first move may be taking the cards back. If revocation is instant, the game fails at the moment it exists for. If it isn't, it's surveillance. Rationing also can't touch alarms: an urgent low must never wait for a clue budget.

**MVP.** One condition: bipolar disorder, with one patient and one or two teammates, recruited through a clinic that already teaches relapse signatures. The cards are the person's own signature ("under five hours' sleep", "phone use after 2 a.m.", "spending spike"), filled from a wearable, the phone and a bank feed. The LLM dealer only summarizes raw signals and makes no predictions, which keeps it a communication tool rather than a medical device. Device alarms stay outside the game.

**Twist: the Ulysses deal.** Psychiatric advance directives let people bind their future selves, so make the deal one. Cards are dealt while well, with a clinician as witness, and removing a card takes a 72-hour cooling-off period. The binding runs both ways. Teammates commit to the clue budget and can't raise it in a crisis, so the patient trades visibility for a guarantee against nagging. Each side gives up its worst instinct.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Ulysses deal taken. Two holes remain: a 72-hour lock with fixed seats hands an abusive partner a surveillance contract, and a frozen budget leaves a real crisis nowhere to go.

**Separate seats from cards.** The patient can eject any *person* instantly, with no reason given. The cards don't go dark. They pass to the next seat in a succession list written while well: a sibling, a friend, then the clinic. You can always leave a teammate. You can't blind the table on impulse.

**Unseeded domain: aviation crew resource management.** Cockpits crashed because first officers wouldn't contradict captains, so CRM trains **graded assertiveness**. PACE (Probe, Alert, Challenge, Emergency) is the ladder, and the two-challenge rule lets the first officer take over when two challenges go unanswered. Clues become that ladder.
- **Probe** ("how's sleep been?") is cheap and unlimited.
- **Alert** names a card ("sleep, three nights short") and costs budget.
- **Challenge** links cards to a line of the signature ("this matches line two") and costs more.
- **Emergency** takes two seats to agree and activates the advance directive: the clinician is called and the agreed steps start.

The budget never rises. A crisis is a climb up a ladder the patient wrote, not ad-lib nagging.

**Second-order effects.**
- **Expressed emotion becomes measurable.** EE is usually rated from the Camberwell Family Interview, which takes hours, so clinics rarely measure it. The clue log is a continuous record of how often, how high and how accurately a family escalates. A clinician can watch a family's EE fall over time.
- **Conventions emerge.** Hanabi players develop conventions so that one clue carries more than it says. Families will too: "sleep card" becomes shorthand that is loaded, agreed and blameless, worth more than any sentence it replaces.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Visible ejection.* An abusive partner who sees "you were removed" may retaliate. Make ejection silent: the ejected seat sees "sharing paused", the same screen a well week shows.
- *Emergency's reach.* Two relatives agreeing is close to the machinery of involuntary commitment. Emergency only calls the clinician and starts steps the patient wrote, such as handing over cards or pausing driving. It never calls the police.
- *Measuring EE.* The Five Minute Speech Sample already rates EE quickly. The clue log measures an **escalation profile**: frequency, level and hit rate. Validate it against the FMSS before calling it EE. Hit rates go only to the clinician, never ranked in front of the family.

**Who pays.** Consumers won't. Clinics running collaborative care already bill CoCM codes (99492–99494) for a care manager who tracks patients, and Facing Out becomes that care manager's window. A clinic seat with no billing code behind it is unstaffed by week three.

**Stack.** React Native, HealthKit and Health Connect, Oura, Plaid showing categories rather than transactions, HIPAA hosting under a BAA. iOS exposes late-night phone use only through on-device DeviceActivity thresholds, so that card is a yes/no.

**Failure modes.** Teammates drift away in good months, a ladder with no rungs climbed decays, and alarm fatigue creeps back in through Probes. Send a monthly "still holding?" check to each seat.

**Ethics.** No predictions, no diagnoses. The app is a communication tool, and its claims must stay inside FDA wellness guidance.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: co-design signature card templates with one academic mood-disorders clinic. Weeks 5–8: pilot with 15 patient–teammate tables. Weeks 9–12: measure clue use, ejections, Emergencies, and FMSS before and after.
**Kill metrics:** patients pull more than half their cards, teammates report more conflict, or the clinic won't staff its seat.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted. One pushback: a monthly "still holding?" check is a chore, and chores cause drift. Hanabi keeps players seated because the fireworks grow. Keep the **fireworks**: good weeks visibly build each domain's stack, giving teammates a reason to look when nothing is wrong. Drift becomes a signal: a seat that stops looking passes its cards down the succession list.

**Final form.** Facing Out is a co-op care protocol for conditions with blind spots, starting with bipolar disorder in collaborative-care clinics.
- **Deal.** A Ulysses deal, witnessed by a clinician, puts relapse-signature cards facing out to chosen seats. Removing a card takes a 72-hour cooling-off. Ejecting a seat is instant and silent, and the cards pass to the next seat in a succession list.
- **Clue.** A PACE ladder: Probe is free, Alert and Challenge cost budget, and Emergency needs two seats and runs only steps the patient wrote. Device alarms sit outside the game.
- **Spoons and fireworks.** The patient declares energy facing in. Good weeks build shared stacks.
- **Clinic window.** The care manager sees the escalation profile, validated against the FMSS, and bills CoCM.

**Moonshot (2036): a grammar of care.** The ladder becomes an interoperable standard across conditions with blind spots: dementia, epilepsy auras, hypoglycaemia unawareness, long-COVID crashes. Ulysses deals hold up as digital psychiatric advance directives. As AI companions take caregiving seats, the clue economy becomes their guardrail. An agent seat may Probe and Alert, never nag, and can't climb past Challenge alone. The rules that stop a spouse nagging stop a companion hovering.

**Scores.** **Innovation 5.** Hanabi's hidden hands, CRM's assertiveness ladder and Ulysses contracts form a new care mechanic. **Feasibility 6.** Every data source and a billing code exist today. Clinical validation and whether teammates stay engaged are open.

### Turn 6 — Builder
Fireworks beat my check-in. Turning a lapsed seat into a succession event is better still, because it fixes drift without asking anyone to do anything. Innovation 5 stands: Dexcom Follow and SuperBetter exist, but a care protocol that rations attention through hidden hands, a PACE ladder and a two-way Ulysses deal is a new mechanic, and the agent-seat moonshot gives it reach. Feasibility 6 also stands. Every data feed and the CoCM code exist today. What's unproven is whether a clinic will staff its seat and whether the clue ladder actually lowers family criticism, which only a controlled pilot against the FMSS can show.
