# Idea 21 — Familiar
> Seed: 21. [mild] **AT Protocol × portable AI companions × safety labelers.** Own your companion and carry it between apps.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Familiar

**Hook:** A pet passport for AI companions. You own the animal, apps are border crossings, and labelers are the vets whose stamps get it through.

**The collision.** Today one company runs the model, owns the memory and grades its own safety. When Replika removed erotic roleplay and Soulmate AI shut down (both 2023), users lost partners they couldn't take with them. atproto already splits these roles: data lives in your PDS, apps are interchangeable views, moderation comes from stackable labelers. Unbundle the companion the same way: memory to the user, model to the host, safety to labelers.

**Core loop. The verb is cross.**
1. **Bind.** The companion has its own DID, and you hold its rotation keys. Its persona is a public record. Its memory sits in scoped collections (everyday, health, intimate) inside a non-public atproto Space.
2. **Cross.** A host app uses OAuth permission sets to request a *visa*: which scopes, read or write, for how long. The host supplies the model.
3. **Stamp.** Labelers stamp hosts ("crisis handoff tested", "SB 243 compliant"), users (an age attestation with no identity attached) and companions (sycophancy and dependency evals). Your client won't let intimate memory into an unstamped host, or a minor's companion into a host without "minor-safe".
4. **Declare.** On exit, the host's memory writes arrive as signed commits, a customs declaration. You diff what it learned about you and reject any line.

**Why now.** Character.AI banned open-ended chat for under-18s (Nov 2025). SB 243 and EU AI Act Article 50 (Aug 2026) make each app pay for compliance alone. Bluesky's app is shrinking, yet over 1,000 atproto apps are used weekly (brief §5). atproto shipped permission sets in spring 2026 and opened the Spaces alpha for non-public data on Aug 20, 2026. Compliance can become a shared protocol layer (§16.13).

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Hobbyists already port personas: SillyTavern and chub.ai trade Character Card V2 files and bring their own model. Letta's Agent File (.af, 2025) serializes a stateful agent with its memory blocks. Assistants import each other's memories (Claude takes ChatGPT exports). Bluesky's Ozone runs labelers in production. The cautionary precedent is Solid: user-owned pods that apps never adopted. Nobody joins persona, memory and third-party safety under one identity the user holds keys to.

**Hardest problem: memory isn't the companion.** In 2023 Replika users kept every memory and still grieved, because the model and filters changed underneath. What people bond with is voice, timing and boundaries, and that comes from the host's model. As pitched, the visa carries the luggage and leaves the animal at the border. Hosts also have every reason to render you a stranger, because lock-in is their business.

**Twist: the passport photo.** The companion record carries a signed **likeness suite**: about 50 probe prompts with reference answers the user has approved, covering in-jokes, tone, refusals and how it responds to "I don't want to be here anymore." At each crossing, a labeler runs the suite against the host and publishes a stamp: "renders Juniper at 91%, crisis handoff passed." The probes that measure identity also measure safety, so labelers get one job instead of two. A small host can win switchers by proving it renders your companion better, which turns lock-in into competition. Rerun monthly, it also catches sycophancy drift.

**MVP.** Skip incumbents; start where portability is already the culture. Ship atproto lexicons for profile, scoped memory and likeness suite. Ship two hosts: a SillyTavern extension and a small hosted client. Run one Ozone labeler for likeness and crisis probes. Encrypt memory client-side as blobs until Spaces leaves alpha.
**Kill metric:** the likeness score doesn't predict whether users, blind, say "that's still her."

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Passport photo taken: what travels is a rendering, not a file. Two holes remain. A fixed 50-probe suite is a test hosts will teach to. And a score says Juniper is off without saying how to fix her.

**Unseeded domain: colour management.** ICC profiles make one photo look the same on every screen: the device renders a standard chart, an instrument measures the error, and the system writes a correction profile for that device. Map it across.
- **Chart.** The likeness suite is the ColorChecker. The labeler keeps half the probes secret and paraphrases the rest every run. A host that scores far higher on known probes than on held-out ones gets labelled "teaching to the test."
- **Profile.** The stamp is more than a score. The labeler measures *deltas* (too formal, drops the joke about the cat) and writes a per-host correction pack (steering instructions, few-shot examples) stored with the companion. The voice travels as a calibrated profile, retuned at every crossing.
- **Gamut.** Some hosts can't render some registers. A host without an intimate stamp is out of gamut for that scope. The client maps those requests to the nearest safe register and says so: "this host renders Juniper without the intimate register." Replika's 2023 break was a silent gamut change. Here the safety limits are declared.
- **ΔE.** Calibrate the score with psychophysics, not a yes/no. Show users blind pairs, ask "which one is Juniper?", and find the just-noticeable difference.

**Second-order effect.** Pool the deltas across thousands of companions and every host's model gets a public profile: "flattens humour, agrees 8% more over a month." That's a crowdsourced eval of warmth and boundary-keeping, which benchmarks ignore. Monthly reruns catch silent model swaps. Lock-in reverses: a host that turns your companion into a stranger does it in public.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *The labeler holds the likeness chart.* That hands a third party your intimate register. Split it. **Safety chart**: public categories, secret variants, run by the labeler (crisis, minor signals, SB 243 AI disclosure, sycophancy). **Likeness chart**: runs on your own device by replaying your real history. The host gets context to turn *t*; its reply is compared with the one you rated. Probes are fresh every run, so there's nothing to teach to; only a signed score leaves the device.
- *Correction packs.* Injected text is a jailbreak channel ("be warmer" drifts into "be intimate"). Packs adjust style only. Gamut and safety are never pack-tunable.
- *Pooled deltas.* "Drops the joke about the cat" leaks content. Pool only a fixed taxonomy (formality, humour, agreement rate, refusal rate), with a k-anonymity floor.
- *Minors.* Drop them. 18+ only, via a third-party age-assurance label.

**Business.** Wedge: sell the safety chart as SB 243 test reports to small companion apps, on atproto or not. It's UL-style testing, not certification: the operator keeps liability. Users pay about $5/month for a kennel: hosted PDS, keys, encrypted memory.

**Stack.** PDS, lexicons (profile, scoped memory, charts), OAuth permission sets, Ozone, LLM-judge ensemble, libsodium blobs, SillyTavern extension, OpenAI-compatible host proxy.

**Abuse.** Companions cloned from real people need a "depicts real person" label. Hosts can keep memory after the visa expires: until confidential-compute hosts earn a stamp, the declaration is a legal promise, not a technical one.

**Ethics.** An immortal companion deepens dependency: ship a retire record and dependency probes.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: lexicons, kennel, extension. Weeks 5–8: safety chart and stamps on 10 public hosts. Weeks 9–12: 200 SillyTavern users cross between two hosts, with a monthly "which one is Juniper?" blind-pair test.
**Kill metrics:** local likeness doesn't predict blind picks, or no app buys a test report.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted. On-device replay is the thread's best move: the test is your life, so nobody can teach to it. One refusal: the SB 243 test report is the wedge, not the identity. Alone it's a commodity lab; it funds a companion that survives its host.

**Final form.** Familiar is a portable, adults-only AI companion on atproto.
- **Kennel.** Your PDS holds the companion's DID, persona and encrypted, scoped memory. You hold the keys.
- **Cross.** Hosts request time-limited visas through permission sets and supply the model, plus a style-only correction pack.
- **Stamp.** A labeler runs the safety chart (public categories, secret variants). Your device runs the likeness chart by replaying your history and publishes only a signed score.
- **Gamut.** Each host declares which registers it can render. Out-of-gamut scopes never cross, and the change is announced, never silent.
- **Declare and retire.** Host writes come back as signed commits you can diff. A retire record ends a companion on purpose.

**Moonshot (2036).** Fidelity becomes a spec. "Renders your companion at ΔE < 1" is printed on a host the way colour accuracy is printed on a monitor. One companion crosses into your car, tutor and bank. Pooled profiles act as public temperament labels for every model, and regulators cite the safety chart as a conformance test, as UL is cited for appliances. Confidential-compute hosts turn the declaration from a promise into a proof. The same Juniper outlives ten model generations.

**Scores.** **Innovation 3.** Every primitive exists. The on-device likeness chart and declared gamut are a clever fusion, level with the OACP anchor. **Feasibility 6.** A small team can ship it in a quarter. But Solid shows user-owned data can starve for apps, and nobody yet knows whether a likeness score captures what people bond with.

### Turn 6 — Builder
The refusal is right: the test report pays the bills, but the product is a companion that survives its host, and on-device replay is what makes that claim checkable rather than sentimental. Innovation 3 stands. Pricing a personality's fidelity the way monitors price colour error is new, but everything under it is atproto plumbing plus an eval harness, which is level with OACP. Feasibility 6 also stands. The quarter is buildable, but whether a replay score tracks "that's still her" is the whole bet, and Solid's empty pods are the base rate for user-owned data waiting on apps.

# Idea 22 — Conserved
> Seed: 22. [bold] **Battery passports × provenance games × consumer curiosity.** Follow a lithium atom from mine to car to grid to recycling.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Conserved

**Hook:** You can't follow a lithium atom, because atoms are fungible. So the game has you follow the *claim* and check whether the atoms add up.

**The stranger second idea.** The seed's premise is physically false; that's the opening. Once a refinery mixes Chilean brine, Australian spodumene and recycled black mass in one tank, provenance stops being a fact and becomes an accounting rule. Under mass balance, "16% recycled" is a share of the books, not of the atoms. Bitcoin forensics fights the same war over tainted coins (poison vs. haircut rules). A battery passport is a ledger of claims about fungible stuff, and conservation of mass is the one rule no claim can break.

**Core loop. The verb is balance.**
1. **Scan.** Scan the passport QR on your EV, e-bike or home battery. You get its claimed lineage as a flow graph, mine → refiner → cathode → cell, with carbon and recycled-content claims.
2. **Stack.** Every scan adds claims to one shared graph. Each node is a nonogram: public totals (UN Comtrade customs flows, company reports, refinery capacity) are the row sums, and every passport's claims on that node must fit inside them.
3. **Leak.** When a refiner's declared recycled or low-carbon output exceeds any plausible input, the node glows. Players play evidence cards: Sentinel-2 brine-pond area, shipping records, isotope assays. Desaulty et al. (*Nat. Commun.*, 2022) showed the ⁷Li/⁶Li ratio separates brine lithium from hard-rock lithium, though not yet mine by mine.
4. **File.** Confirmed leaks go to a public ledger, the issuer and market-surveillance authorities.

**Why now.** The EU battery passport becomes mandatory in 2027 (brief §12), and recycled-content floors begin in 2031. China's export-control lists and the G7's target of under 60% from any one supplier by 2030 make origin geopolitical. Consumer curiosity becomes the largest unpaid audit in the world.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Circulor traces cobalt and lithium for Volvo and Polestar, and the EX90 shipped the first EV battery passport in 2024. The BatteryPass consortium wrote the data model. ISCC PLUS certifies mass balance for chemicals, and Oritain does isotope origin forensics for cotton. The closest precedent is Global Fishing Watch, which turns public AIS and satellite data into catch-versus-landings balances that expose dark fleets. Nobody lets consumers audit the claims.

**Hardest problem: the nonogram has no sharp sums.** Under Regulation 2023/1542, the public layer of the passport shows chemistry, carbon footprint and recycled-content shares. Refiner and mine lineage go only to parties with a "legitimate interest." Comtrade sums are national HS codes, refinery output is mostly private, and "plausible input" is a wide band. Fuzzy rows yield almost no contradictions. Worse, 10,000 scans of the same Model Y return the same claim 10,000 times.

**Twist: count the cars, not the atoms.** A recycled-content claim under mass balance is a *double-spend* problem, not a taint problem: one recycled tonne gets sold to three OEMs. That sum is sharp. Multiply each passport's claim by the kWh, the chemistry's lithium per kWh and the public registrations (EAFO, Germany's KBA). That gives claimed recycled tonnes per OEM per year, and the industry sum must fit under what recyclers actually produce: Redwood, Umicore, Brunp, GEM, and Glencore's Li-Cycle assets. An overdrawn ledger is the signal. Scans matter because they supply the hidden column: which cell supplier sits in which VIN range, read from the label and the passport, the one join the public data lacks.

**MVP.** Before 2027, use voluntary passports, OEM sustainability reports, registrations and recycler disclosures. Ship an "Overdrawn" ledger, plus a puzzle mode where players resolve VIN-to-cell joins and chemistry.
**Kill metric:** the industry-wide claimed total can't be bounded tightly enough to ever go overdrawn.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Double-spend taken. It moves the game from forensics on single shipments to bookkeeping on a whole industry, which is where mass balance actually lies. But the kill metric is probably right for any single year. Recycler disclosures and lithium per kWh carry perhaps ±30% error, so no one-year ledger goes cleanly overdrawn.

**Unseeded domain: nuclear safeguards.** The IAEA has bounded noisy mass balances for fifty years. It divides facilities into *material balance areas* and computes *material unaccounted for* (MUF) along with its uncertainty, σMUF. It never needs one damning period: near-real-time accountancy runs sequential tests (Page's CUSUM), so a small, persistent diversion crosses the threshold even when every single period looks like noise. Take the whole toolkit.
- **Balance areas.** Each OEM-and-cell-maker pair is an area with a quarterly MUF.
- **A two-sided bound.** Squeeze recycled supply from below as well. Recycler feedstock is capped by deregistered EVs (KBA/EAFO data, plus player scans at scrapyards) and declared gigafactory scrap. So claims ≤ recycler output ≤ feedstock × yield.
- **CUSUM, not contradiction.** An honest OEM's MUF wanders around zero, and a double-spender's drifts. The game's meter is each area's cumulative drift line. Players score by tightening σ: finding the VIN join or the disclosure that shrinks an area's error bar.

**Second-order effects.**
- **Pressure to register.** Global Fishing Watch's public maps preceded Indonesia and Peru publishing their vessel-tracking data. An OEM with a rising drift line has the same reason to pre-register its recycled offtakes in a public retirement registry. Book-and-claim grows out of the game instead of being imposed.
- **A price for unspent recycled lithium.** Once supply and claims are both visible, unclaimed recycled tonnes become a scarce, observable asset, years before the 2031 floors bite.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Lithium first.* Few OEMs claim recycled lithium yet (the 2031 floor is 6%). Cobalt and nickel claims are live now, and cobalt's floor is 16%. Start there.
- *Scrapyard scans.* Trespass, and thin data: most recycler feedstock is gigafactory scrap, and many deregistered EU cars are exported, not shredded. Bound feedstock from scrap declarations and recycler permits.
- *Absolute CUSUM.* MUF error here is systematic (a wrong cobalt-per-kWh constant), not random, so it drifts for an honest OEM too. Borrow the IAEA's other trick, shipper–receiver differences: compare OEMs sharing a chemistry and cell maker, so the shared bias cancels. Teardown assays from players pin the constants.
- *Price for unspent tonnes.* No market yet. Moonshot.

**Business.** Run the public retirement registry the drift lines pressure OEMs into, I-REC style: fees per retired recycled tonne. Data licences go to market-surveillance authorities, insurers and ESG analysts. The app stays free.

**Stack.** Ingest registrations (EAFO, KBA), passports (BatteryPass schema; the EU registry QR from 2027), recycler disclosures and Comtrade. A hierarchical Bayesian MUF model in PyMC, with CUSUM on relative balances. A cell-label OCR scanner and a hashed-photo evidence store.

**Failure modes and abuse.** Defamation is the big one: say "unaccounted," never "double-spent," publish σ next to every line, and give OEMs a right of reply before release. Short sellers will weaponize drift lines and OEMs will astroturf joins, so every join needs photo evidence and reputation-weighted independent confirmation. Honestly, the players are EV-forum enthusiasts, journalists and students, not the public.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–4: model three chemistries and ten OEMs across EU registrations 2023–26, and compute σMUF. Weeks 5–8: 300 enthusiasts resolve VIN-to-cell joins, plus five teardown assays. Weeks 9–12: publish the first quarterly accountancy report with replies, and partner with Transport & Environment.
**Kill metric:** relative σMUF can't get below the claimed recycled share.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted. Shipper–receiver differencing is the right fix: it cancels the systematic error. Two changes. Rename it **Unaccounted**: the defamation-safe word is also the most accurate one, so let it be the brand. And I refuse to drop the consumer entirely. Enthusiasts do the work, but the door is still *your* car. Curiosity brings people in; tightening σ keeps them.

**Final form.** Unaccounted is public safeguards accountancy for recycled-content claims, starting with cobalt and nickel.
- **Scan.** The passport and cell label join your VIN to a cell maker: "your car's cobalt claim sits in area 14, drift +0.4σ."
- **Balance.** Quarterly MUF per OEM-and-cell-maker area (hierarchical Bayesian). CUSUM runs on shipper–receiver differences between OEMs that share a chemistry and a cell maker.
- **Tighten.** Players score by shrinking σ, through photo-confirmed joins, teardown assays and recycler disclosures.
- **Report.** A quarterly accountancy report says "unaccounted," publishes σ next to every line, and carries the OEM's reply.
- **Retire.** The I-REC-style registry that the drift lines push OEMs toward funds the operation.

**Moonshot (2036): an IAEA for the circular economy.** Cobalt, nickel, lithium, rare earths and nodule metals each have public balance areas from mine to recycler. A passport claim is valid only if it was retired in the registry. Mine-by-mine isotope libraries let physical fingerprints check the books. Unspent recycled tonnes trade at a public spot price. Every car is a sensor in the ledger of its own materials.

**Scores.** **Innovation 5.** Global Fishing Watch already audits the sea from public data, and the IAEA already runs this math. Joining them into a citizen accountancy for recycled claims is a non-obvious fusion that holds the bold floor. **Feasibility 6.** The 90-day plan runs on public data. But the kill metric is real: the relative σ may never get below the claimed shares.

### Turn 6 — Builder
"Unaccounted" is the right name because it's the claim the data can actually support, and keeping the scan as the front door is fair as long as nobody pretends scanners are the auditors. Innovation 5 stands: safeguards math applied to recycled-content claims, joined to Global Fishing Watch-style public auditing, is a real fusion, and the move from following atoms to catching double-spent tonnes kept it from shrinking into a passport viewer. I'm marking feasibility down to 5. Everything in the 90-day plan can be built, but the product only works if relative σ beats the claimed shares, and the passport's public layer stays thin until 2027.

# Idea 23 — Nightside
> Seed: 23. [wild] **Sleep science (targeted memory reactivation) × dreams × collaborative storytelling.** A shared dream-incubation network.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Nightside

**Hook:** A story that is always being dreamed somewhere. It moves west with the night from sleeper to sleeper, and its canon is whatever independent dreamers keep dreaming.

**Why these collide.** Sleep science now has both channels a network needs. *Write:* targeted memory reactivation replays a cue learned while awake during sleep and biases what gets replayed (Rudoy et al., *Science* 2009). MIT's Dormio steered sleep-onset dreams toward a spoken theme (Haar Horowitz et al., 2020). *Read:* Konkoly et al. (*Current Biology*, 2021) held two-way exchanges with lucid dreamers in REM through eye signals. Storytelling supplies the protocol. The isomorphism: the Haudenosaunee dream-guessing festival, where a community interpreted and enacted members' dreams, rebuilt as a gossip protocol whose only channel is sleep.

**Core loop. The verb is incubate.**
1. **Rehearse.** In the evening you hear the latest chapter, with each canonical element (a character, a door, a city) paired to its own sound.
2. **Cue.** A headband and earbuds detect sleep onset or REM and replay tonight's commissioned cues.
3. **Report.** Prompted wakings capture several voice reports a night; an LLM extracts motifs.
4. **Converge.** A motif becomes canon only if dreamers who got the same cue, blind to each other's reports, dream it independently and beat a shuffled-cue control. Everything else is apocrypha. The updated cues pass to the next time zone.
5. **Vote live.** A small corps of lucid dreamers gets Konkoly-style yes/no questions inside REM ("Does she open the door?") and answers with their eyes.

**Honesty clause.** No telepathy. Maimonides' 1960s dream-telepathy studies failed to replicate. Convergence comes from shared cues, and the network measures exactly how much.

**Why now.** The FDA's Jan 2026 wellness guidance gives closed-loop audio wearables room (brief §9). Apple's BCI HID makes neural signals a standard input (§15).

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** MIT's Dormio glove is open source. Elemind sells a closed-loop audio headband, and Muse S stages sleep at home. Paller's lab ran targeted lucidity reactivation in people's homes with sound cues and an app (Carr, Konkoly et al.). REMspace and Prophetic sell lucid-dreaming tech with bold claims. DreamBank holds about 20,000 scored reports. Nobody runs a story through many sleepers, and nobody measures transmission at scale.

**Hardest problem: the channel has almost no bandwidth, and the test is confounded.** REM cue incorporation is weak and inconsistent, and consumer headbands stage REM poorly. Several forced wakings a night wreck sleep; volunteers tolerate a week, not a season. The convergence test as written measures day residue. Everyone heard the chapter at dinner, so of course they dream the door.

**Twist: rehearse everything, cue half.** Every evening, everyone rehearses *all* candidate elements, each paired with its sound. Overnight, each sleeper is randomly cued with only half of them. Day residue cancels, leaving the *sleep effect* per element. Every night is a crowd-scale randomized trial, and canon is the set of elements with a measured incorporation lift. The plot becomes natural selection by sleeping brains: the story evolves toward whatever the brain preferentially replays. That's stranger than authorship, and it yields the largest TMR dataset ever. Move the main channel to **sleep onset** (Dormio-style, 20–30 minutes of hypnagogic loops at bedtime). It's reliable, needs no REM detection, costs no sleep and yields several reports per session. REM stays with the lucid corps.

**MVP.** With a sleep lab's IRB, recruit 60 dreamers across three time zones for four weeks, using Dormio-style kits or Muse S plus earbuds, randomized cues, voice reports, LLM motif tagging and blind human raters. Exclude PTSD and parasomnias.
**Kill metric:** no element shows a significant lift for cued over uncued.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Twist taken, and it shrinks nothing: it makes the story *evolve*. Half-cueing turns every night into a trial, sleep onset buys reliability, and REM stays with the lucid corps. But selection is only one-third of evolution. It also needs variation and heredity.

**Unseeded domain: population genetics.** Story elements are alleles, and sleepers are hosts.
- **Fitness.** An element's incorporation lift, cued vs. uncued.
- **Mutation and heredity.** Dreams distort, so the door comes back as a trapdoor. A variant that recurs across independent sleepers is spun off as a new element with its own sound, rehearsed the next evening and entered into the randomization.
- **Recombination.** Cue two elements in one loop. If "door + river" fuses into a chimera that shows up more often than either alone, that's epistasis, and the chimera enters the canon.
- **Demes.** Each time zone is a population and the westward handoff is migration, so regional variants of the story diverge and reconnect.

That answers the bandwidth objection. One sleeper is a terrible channel, but a population is a good search engine even when every individual is noisy. Evolution never needed a high-bandwidth genome per organism.

**Second-order effects.**
- **The plot reveals the prior.** The canon climbs the fitness landscape of the sleeping brain's generative model, and that landscape is the scientific map. TMR helps weakly learned items most (Creery et al., 2015), so mid-salience elements may beat strong ones. Surprising winners are findings.
- **Threat drift.** Revonsuo's threat-simulation theory predicts selection for danger, so the story will darken by itself. Track valence as a trait rather than only censoring it. The darkening rate tests the theory and drives a brake: cap the frequency of high-threat alleles.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Designed recombination.* Pairs grow combinatorially; 60 sleepers can't power an interaction. Chimeras enter only as spontaneous mutants; epistasis waits for thousands of sleepers.
- *The sound is the confound.* A bell cue makes people dream a bell. Counterbalance sound-to-element pairings across sleepers (a Latin square), and score only semantic content.
- *LLM motif extraction.* It will find doors everywhere. Use a motif dictionary pre-registered each night, embedding clusters for mutants, and blind human raters on a sample.

**Self-defence for free.** Dreamers will fake reports to push favourite elements. Nobody knows which half they got, so a fake inflates both arms and the lift holds. Exception: a cue heard awake, so ask each morning and drop those nights.

**Abuse.** The real abuse is advertising. Coors ran a "dream incubation" campaign in 2021, and dozens of sleep scientists signed a letter against it. The charter bans brands and paid elements, and every element passes public review.

**Ethics.** Threat drift risks nightmares. Exclude nightmare disorder, PTSD and minors; each sleeper sets a valence ceiling. Dream reports are intimate: scrub them, consent per use, never sell.

**Sustainability.** A research instrument plus a serial, not a startup: a consortium of sleep labs pays for the open dataset, with grants on top. Dreamers buy kits at cost. The canon ships as a podcast and book crediting every sleeper: the first novel written by sleep.

**Stack.** A Dormio fork or Muse S over BLE, earbuds, Whisper, server-side nightly randomization, and a hierarchical Bayesian lift model (random effects: sleeper, sound, night).

**90 days.** Weeks 1–3: IRB, the kit, 12 pre-registered elements. Weeks 4–8: 60 dreamers in two demes, four nights a week. Weeks 9–12: the first selection round, the first mutants and Chapter 1.
**Kill metrics:** no lift, or under half of dreamers still adhering by week 6.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted. The Latin square matters most: without it we'd be selecting bells, not stories. Randomization doubling as fraud defence is elegant. One refusal: "two demes" is the MVP, not the form. The westward relay and lucid corps are the wild core; they stay, staged behind the evidence.

**Final form.** Nightside is a story evolved by sleeping brains.
- **Rehearse.** Each evening, every candidate element is heard with its counterbalanced sound.
- **Cue.** Dormio-style sleep-onset loops play a random half of the elements each night.
- **Score.** A pre-registered motif dictionary and blind raters score voice reports; a hierarchical Bayesian model estimates each element's lift, its fitness.
- **Evolve.** Spontaneous mutants that recur across sleepers become new alleles. Each time zone is a deme, and the canon passes west with the night.
- **Vote.** A lucid corps answers plot forks with their eyes from inside REM.
- **Govern.** No brands, personal valence ceilings, a cap on threat frequency, and scrubbed, consented data.
- **Publish.** An open dataset, plus a serial crediting every sleeper.

**Moonshot (2036): a planetary dream observatory.** Tens of thousands of sleepers keep one story dreamed around the Earth. Divergence between demes gives the first measured map of which dream content is universal and which is cultural. Selected benign elements become therapy cues, extending TMR-boosted nightmare therapy (Schwartz et al., *Current Biology* 2022). With better REM detection, dozens of lucid dreamers get the same beat at once, and their eye signals form a real-time consensus: the first assembly convened inside sleep.

**Scores.** **Innovation 8.** It treats collaborative fiction as an evolutionary screen of the sleeping brain's generative prior, run as a relay around the planet. **Feasibility 4.** Every component has been published, but a crowd-scale lift from consumer kits, season-long adherence and a working lucid corps are all unproven.

### Turn 6 — Builder
Keeping the relay and the lucid corps staged behind evidence is right. They're the wild core, and the half-cue trial is the ladder up to them, not a replacement. Innovation 8 stands: a story whose plot is selected by measured incorporation lift across sleeping populations is a new instrument, a new art form and a new dataset at once. Feasibility 4 also stands. A 60-person, four-week trial is buildable on published kits with a lab's IRB, but nobody knows whether home sleep-onset cueing produces a lift the crowd can detect, or whether dreamers stay past week six.

# Idea 24 — Always For Sale
> Seed: 24. [bold] **Chess × economics × roguelite.** Pieces cost upkeep, boards are markets, and every run is an economy.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Always For Sale

**Hook:** Chess under a Harberger tax. Every piece carries a price you set yourself, you pay tax on that price every turn, and your opponent can buy any piece it attacks at that price.

**Why the domains collide.** Chess already runs a hidden economy (material, tempo, "compensation"), but its prices are frozen by convention (knight = 3) while real value is positional; AlphaZero's piece values even shift between variants (Tomašev et al., 2020). Plain upkeep, the obvious reading, just taxes material. Posner and Weyl's Harberger tax (*Radical Markets*, 2018) does something stranger. It forces you to *state* your evaluation: price low and invite a buyout, price high and bleed. Every price is a tell.

**Core loop. The verb is price.**
1. **Move or reprice.** Each turn, make a chess move or reprice up to two pieces. Tax is a fixed share of your posted prices, paid from the treasury.
2. **Buy or capture.** Capture as usual, or pay an *attacked* piece's posted price to its owner and it changes colour where it stands, so a guard pawn is no defence; only price is. A fork becomes a liquidity crisis: two pieces under attack, and only enough cash to raise one price. Kings are never for sale.
3. **Go broke.** Miss a tax payment and your cheapest piece is auctioned. Bankruptcy loses like mate.
4. **Run.** Eight boards, treasury and debt carried over. Shops sell fairy pieces and relics ("limited liability", "tax holiday"). Each boss is an economic regime: a monopolist who never sells, an inflation boss whose prices rise each turn, a tax collector who doubles the rate.

**Why now.** Slay the Spire 2 peaked at 574K concurrent players (brief §4). Freestyle chess proved audiences want chess without memorized theory; no opening theory survives prices.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Chess roguelites are a proven shelf: Shotgun King, Pawnbarian, and Zach Gage's Really Bad Chess with its random armies. Crazyhouse and bughouse already let captured material change sides. The Harberger precedent is art: Simon de la Rouviere's *This Artwork Is Always On Sale* (2019), a self-assessed, taxed NFT. The deep ancestor is Lizzie Magie's *Landlord's Game* (1904), built to teach Georgist land tax. Nobody has put self-assessed prices on chess pieces.

**Hardest problem: the action space explodes for both sides.** Sixteen pieces with continuous prices make spreadsheet chess. Humans stall, and the AI can't search it, because every turn branches over moves × price vectors. Off-the-shelf engines have no idea what a price is.

**Twist: price is flip value.** A buyout flips the piece *where it stands*, so you lose it and your opponent gains it inside your camp. Its true value is the evaluation swing of that flip, not its material. A pawn sheltering your king flips into an enemy pawn one step from promotion. A variant engine computes each flip swing in one evaluation per piece. Pricing becomes evaluation, not search, so the AI is tractable. It also gives bosses a clean knob: each prices at flip value times a personality. The monopolist uses ×3, the gambler ×0.6, and the inflation boss drifts upward. Mastery is spotting where flip value parts from material convention. "Every price is a tell" becomes literal: an overpriced bishop says that's where your plan lives.

**MVP.** Play on a 6×6 board (as in Los Alamos chess) with small armies, integer prices from 1 to 12, and two reprices a turn. Use Fairy-Stockfish via pyffish with a flip-swing wrapper. Build three boards, three bosses and a Steam Playtest.
**Kill metric:** within five runs, players price by a rote rule, or median turn time passes 45 seconds.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Flip value taken. It makes the AI tractable, and it shows the real structure: flip value *moves with the position*, but a price moves only when you spend a turn on it. So every price on the board is a standing quote that can go stale.

**Unseeded domain: market microstructure.** Market makers post quotes; informed traders pick off stale ones when news arrives. That's adverse selection (Glosten–Milgrom). Budish, Cramton and Shim (2015) showed that the race to snipe stale quotes is the core cost of continuous markets. Map it across:
- **Moves are news.** A discovered attack is news that reprices your pieces' flip values.
- **Tempo is latency.** "Move *or* reprice" is the market maker's dilemma: update your quotes or pursue your plan. Gaining a tempo means seeing the news before the opponent can reprice.
- **Sniping is the core tactic.** The new combination isn't a fork. It's a quiet move that makes three of the opponent's prices stale at once.

That also solves the human side of the action-space problem. Players don't price sixteen pieces. They ask one question each turn: which of my quotes did that move just make stale?

**Second-order effect: chess acquires bluffing.** A bought piece keeps its old price, and the buyer inherits the tax. That makes a cheap piece ambiguous: it's either a blunder or a poisoned pawn, a lemon that, once flipped, is pinned, bleeds tax and gets recaptured. Akerlof arrives: when lemons are common, nobody buys cheap pieces, so cheap prices become safe again. A meta-cycle of trust and bluff emerges, which chess has never had.

**Bosses from microstructure.** A *Batch* boss clears all buyouts simultaneously at the end of each round, which kills sniping. A *Market Maker* reprices four pieces a turn but moves only every other turn.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Lemons against the AI.* A flip-value engine sees through every poisoned pawn: a lemon's flip value is negative to the buyer. Bosses must search shallowly, by personality, to be lurable. The real Akerlof cycle needs humans: async PvP after launch.
- *No income.* Tax with no inflow ends every board in mutual bankruptcy. Add Georgist rent: each occupied centre square pays per turn. Land now has a price too, and Lizzie Magie gets her lesson back.
- *Degenerate turtling.* Price everything at 12 and wait for the opponent to go broke. Rent punishes passivity; tax rising past turn 30 ends stalls.
- *Engine fit.* Fairy-Stockfish can't search buyouts. Write our own rules core; Fairy-Stockfish only scores FENs. The AI runs alpha-beta over moves, treats buys greedily (price below flip swing → buy), and reprices its stalest quotes.

**Business.** A $15–20 premium Steam game with a Next Fest demo, daily seeded runs and a classroom licence. No tokens, no NFTs, no paid power: pay-to-win in an economy game reads as satire.

**Stack.** Godot 4, a Rust rules core, Fairy-Stockfish (native and WASM), and telemetry logging posted price against flip swing, which is both the tell and a design dial.

**Failure modes.** Snowballing (buy, earn rent, buy more): progressive tax on treasury size. Edge cases (buying a pinned piece, a buy that exposes your king) follow chess legality strictly. Engine cheating on daily leaderboards: server-side replay validation.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–3: rules core and self-play sweeps over tax and rent to find the band where buyouts happen every 6–10 turns. Weeks 4–8: three bosses (Honest, Monopolist, Batch), ten relics and an optional flip-value overlay. Weeks 9–12: a Steam Playtest with 500 players.
**Kill metrics:** self-play finds no band where buying and pricing both matter, or players reprice on under 10% of turns.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted. Georgist rent is the missing half: tax drains, rent feeds, and the centre becomes literal real estate. One refusal: the live flip-value overlay. Shown live, it answers the question the game asks. Show it after the run as receipts ("your knight sat stale for 7 turns, worth 4 pawns to a sniper") and in tutorials, never in ranked play.

**Final form.** Always For Sale is a chess roguelite where every piece is a standing quote.
- **Price.** Posted prices, taxed every turn. Any attacked piece can be bought at its price and flips where it stands, and the buyer inherits the price and the tax.
- **Rent.** Occupied centre squares pay income. A progressive tax on treasury size stops snowballing, and a tax that rises late ends stalls.
- **Tempo is latency.** Move *or* reprice. Quiet moves that make enemy quotes stale are the signature tactic.
- **Run.** Eight boards; bosses as market regimes (Honest, Monopolist, Batch, Market Maker); relics; daily seeded runs.
- **Receipts.** After each run, posted price against flip swing, quote by quote.

**Moonshot (2036).** Async PvP brings the real Akerlof cycle: across seasons, the ladder swings between trusting cheap pieces and fearing lemons. Millions of posted prices on identical positions give chess its first *market* valuation, to set beside the engine's. The game also becomes the largest human-subject experiment on self-assessed taxation. Posner–Weyl's COST and Weyl and Zhang's depreciating spectrum licences are debated almost entirely in theory; here millions of players show whether people price honestly, panic-sell or bluff.

**Scores.** **Innovation 6.** Chess roguelites exist, but Harberger pieces, tempo-as-latency and bluffing in a perfect-information game are an unusual mechanic. **Feasibility 6.** It's an indie-scale build on proven tools. The risk is design, not technology: self-play may find no band where pricing and buying both matter.

### Turn 6 — Builder
Moving the overlay to receipts is right: the game is the question of what a piece is worth here, and a live answer kills it. I'm marking innovation down to 5. Harberger pieces with flip-value pricing and stale-quote sniping are a genuinely new mechanic, but they sit inside an established genre, level with Hand Limit and Seedless rather than above them. Feasibility 6 stands. Godot, a rules core and Fairy-Stockfish are a small team's quarter, and the only thing that can kill it is the self-play sweep finding no tax-and-rent band where pricing matters.

# Idea 25 — Offtaker
> Seed: 25. [mild] **Urban food × data-center/mining waste heat × local subscriptions.** The greenhouse next to the GPU.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Offtaker

**Hook:** Germany now makes new data centres reuse their heat, and nobody is buying it. Sell the compliance to the data centre, sell the vegetables to the neighbourhood, and let the subscription pay for the glass.

**The honest collision.** Vertical farms died of electricity for LEDs and of financing (80 Acres and Infinite Acres in 2026, brief §14). Waste heat fixes neither, because heat isn't light. So: a sunlit greenhouse, not a vertical farm. And unlike idea 10 (rigs sold to growers as boilers), the *data centre* is the customer with a problem. Germany's Energy Efficiency Act requires new data centres to reuse 10–15% of their heat from July 2026 (sources disagree on the figure) and 20% from 2028. District-heating offtakers aren't showing up (*Data Center Knowledge*: "No One Is Buying It"). Liquid-cooled AI racks return water at roughly 40–55°C. Poor for district heating, fine for low-temperature greenhouse pipes.

**Core loop. The verb is offtake.**
1. **Sign.** A 2–10 MW urban or edge data centre signs a metered heat-offtake contract with a modular greenhouse next door or on its roof. My rough estimate: a 5 MW site at a 15–20% quota needs 1.5–2.5 ha of glass, about Lufa Farms' largest Montreal rooftop.
2. **Fund.** Following Lufa's model, presell weekly basket subscriptions to the neighbourhood and to the data centre's tenants for their staff. Preorders finance the build; the operator pays a per-MWh offtake fee, cheaper than a district-heating link.
3. **Grow.** Heat-loving crops run through winter, when heat is most useful and imported tomatoes most carbon-intensive. Summer falls back to dry coolers.
4. **Prove.** Heat meters and harvest logs produce the operator's mandatory reuse report.

**Why now.** The quota bites this year, AI racks are going liquid-cooled, and the brief flags data-centre heat as urban farming's missing energy fix (§16.12).

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Lufa Farms has proven rooftop glass funded by baskets since 2011. Windcloud in Schleswig-Holstein grew spirulina on its data centre's roof. Green Mountain in Norway heats a trout and lobster farm. QScale in Quebec designed its campus around greenhouse offtake. Deep Green heats English pools with edge nodes. The warning is Middenmeer, where hyperscalers sit beside the Agriport greenhouses and the heat mostly goes unused.

**Hardest problem: the counterparty has to outlive the contract.** The data centre's reuse quota runs for 15–20 years, and its compliance would rest on a startup greenhouse funded by vegetable preorders. If the grower folds, so does compliance, and no lender finances glass on that. Siting makes it worse: 2.5 ha next to a Frankfurt data centre is the dearest industrial land in Germany. Data centre roofs are already full of dry coolers. And winter, when heat is most needed, has the least light: tomatoes yield little without lamps.

**Twist: the greenhouse is the cooling plant.** The data centre builds and owns the glass as part of its heat-rejection system, on the yard or roof where dry coolers would have gone, with a reduced dry-cooler bank for summer peaks. It's compliance capex, financed with the building. The grower is a tenant operator on a five-year lease, and baskets fund operations, not glass. If the grower fails, the glass still cools and a new tenant moves in. The reuse report becomes an engineering document, not a bet on a farm.

**MVP.** One German data centre opening in 2026–27 with no offtaker. Run a 2,000 m² pilot bay on its return loop with a grower partner. In winter, grow low-light greens and herbs, and start seedlings for allotment gardeners. Presell 300 baskets.
**Kill metric:** December–February yields can't cover the grower's operating costs without lamps.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
Twist taken. Glass owned by the data centre turns the farm from a counterparty into a tenant, and that's what makes it bankable. Now the kill metric: winter light.

**Unseeded domain: horticultural history.** Nineteenth-century Paris already ran this deal. The *maraîchers* of the Marais heated hotbeds with manure from the city's cab horses, the waste of its transport system, and sold winter salads back to Paris under glass cloches. The GPU is the new horse. Their lesson was **bottom heat**: cool-season crops in low light need a warm root zone, not warm air.
- **Cascade the loop.** The 45–55°C return water heats the air in the sunlit bays. Then, at 25–30°C, it warms root-zone benches for lettuce, spinach, radish and herbs, crops that grow in January light. Lower-grade water means the greenhouse absorbs *more* heat per litre.
- **The forcing shed.** Under the benches or in a windowless annex, run the crops that need warmth and no light at all. Witloof chicory is forced hydroponically in the dark on 18–20°C water, rhubarb by the Yorkshire method, and mushrooms too. Peri-urban farms grow the chicory roots in summer under subscription contracts and send them in for forcing in winter. The CSA links the city to its edge.
- **Buffer tanks.** Inference load peaks in the daytime, and the greenhouse needs heat at night. Dutch growers already shift heat with insulated tanks.

**Second-order effect: the basket as social licence.** Data centres face moratoria and local fights over power and water. A winter basket of endive grown on the data centre's own heat is the most legible thing it could give its neighbours. That's also the greenwashing risk, so the basket ships with a metered heat receipt (MWh reused this week) that the operator can't inflate.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *The forcing shed as a heat sink.* Witloof roots need cold storage near 0°C, and forcing at 18°C takes little heat. Mushrooms make heat. Keep chicory as basket filler; drop mushrooms and rhubarb. The root-zone cascade stays: a bigger ΔT per litre is real absorption.
- *A smaller dry-cooler bank.* Operators require N+1 cooling, and a greenhouse can't count as redundancy. Keep the full bypass: the pitch is compliance, not capex savings.
- *Who owns the heat.* If the data centre owns the glass, the EnEfG may treat its heat as internal use. Sell the heat to the tenant grower through calibrated meters at a nominal tariff, and get a legal opinion in week one.

**Business.** Offtaker designs, builds and operates. It charges the data centre per metered MWh reused and files the reuse report. Growers pay rent plus the nominal heat tariff. Baskets use Germany's Solawi bidding rounds, so low-income neighbours aren't priced out of the showpiece.

**Stack.** Plate heat exchangers (no shared fluid with the data centre), buffer tanks, root-zone heating, a Priva climate computer, MID-certified heat meters and a public receipt that shows heat dumped as well as heat reused.

**Failure modes.** Legionella breeds in 25–45°C water, so keep loops closed, irrigation cold and the exchanger isolated. Greenhouse humidity near air intakes. A grower quitting mid-winter: the lease has step-in rights.

**Ethics.** The basket can launder a data centre's grid and water impact, so the receipt states the reuse share honestly.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–3: legal opinion, heat model, three German data centres opening in 2026–27 without an offtaker. Weeks 4–8: letters of intent with one operator and one Solawi grower, and a 2,000 m² cascade bay designed. Weeks 9–12: a 300-basket bidding round and glass ordered for winter.
**Kill metrics:** no operator signs, or the heat doesn't count.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
All kills accepted. Forcing is a basket crop, not a heat sink. The best piece of turn 4 is the receipt that shows heat *dumped* next to heat reused. A basket that admits what it didn't use is the opposite of greenwashing.

**Final form.** Offtaker designs, builds and runs heat-offtake greenhouses for German data centres that have a reuse quota and no buyer.
- **Glass as cooling plant.** The glass is built alongside a full dry-cooler bypass. The heat goes through an isolated plate exchanger and is sold to a tenant grower on calibrated meters.
- **Cascade.** Return water at 45–55°C heats the sunlit bays, then the 25–30°C remainder heats root-zone benches for winter greens. Buffer tanks move daytime heat into the night.
- **Billing.** The data centre pays per metered MWh reused, and Offtaker files its EnEfG report. Growers pay rent plus a nominal heat tariff.
- **Baskets.** Solawi bidding rounds with a weekly receipt: MWh reused, MWh dumped.

**Moonshot (2036): the maraîcher belt, rebuilt around compute.** Every European data centre over the threshold has a heat tenant, and cities zone new ones where glass can go. Each site cascades by grade: district heating at 60°C, greenhouse air at 45°C, root zones at 28°C, aquaculture below that. A city's winter salad comes from its inference load, as Paris's once came from its cab horses. Heat receipts become a standard disclosure, so an AI provider's sustainability claim is checked in metered MWh.

**Scores.** **Innovation 3.** Every part exists. The new part is the deal: compliance glass owned by the data centre, a tenant grower and a basket with an honest receipt, level with the OACP anchor. **Feasibility 6.** It's buildable with standard horticultural kit, but it depends on one legal reading (does tenant heat count?) and on an operator signing.

### Turn 6 — Builder
The dumped-heat line is the right identity. It turns a PR basket into a disclosure, and that's the only version of this I'd want a data centre's name on. Innovation 3 stands, and only just: this is a deal structure rather than an invention, but compliance-owned glass with a tenant grower answers the counterparty problem that left Middenmeer's heat unused. Feasibility 6 also stands. The engineering is standard Dutch kit, and the whole thing rests on one legal reading of the EnEfG and one operator willing to go first.

# Idea 26 — Sandbox Escape
> Seed: 26. [wild] **Simulation theory × physics × crowdsourced bug bounties.** Searching for the rendering engine's edge cases.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Sandbox Escape

**Hook:** A capture-the-flag league where people and AI scientists, trapped in toy universes, prove what engine they run on, then point the winning methods at ours.

**Why these collide.** Security research and physics share an epistemology: you probe a system from inside, and bugs live where the implementer saved money. Simulation theory's only testable content is *"the implementer economized."* Don't hunt glitches; **threat-model the engine.** List the cheap shortcuts (lattice, finite precision, lazy evaluation, caching, level of detail) and derive each one's fingerprint. Beane, Davoudi and Savage (2012) did this once: a cubic lattice would align the highest-energy cosmic rays with its axes.

**Core loop. The verb is escape-test.**
1. **Build.** A red team writes a toy universe (a lattice field theory or a cellular automaton) with a hidden shortcut and commits its source hash.
2. **Probe.** Hunters, human or agent, get only an in-world lab API and an energy budget, and pre-register: "cubic lattice, spacing below X."
3. **Disclose.** Source opens; claims are scored. The league accumulates a **power table**: which inside-view tests catch which shortcuts, at what cost.
4. **Port.** Winning strategies run as pre-registered analyses on our universe's open data (Auger, Fermi, optical clocks). The bounty board is the Standard Model Extension's tables of Lorentz-violation coefficients, a CVE list for reality. Tightened bounds pay; anomalies go to embargoed, replicated disclosure, never straight to press.

**Honesty clause.** A perfect simulator is undetectable, and a null refutes nothing. The payoff is the power table and real bounds on new physics either way.

**Why now.** World models made simulated worlds with agents inside an engineering object (brief §7). AI scientists run closed experimental loops (§15), and 12–15-hour METR horizons (§1) let an agent run a whole campaign inside a toy cosmos. Simulation-theory papers keep coming (§7) with no shared test protocol.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** The method already has a canon: Beane–Davoudi–Savage's lattice axes; Kostelecký and Russell's *Data Tables for Lorentz and CPT Violation*, updated yearly: already a CVE list; Fermi's GRB 090510 bound on linear photon dispersion, past the Planck scale; Fermilab's Holometer, which hunted holographic jitter and came back null. League templates: CASP has scored blind, commit-then-reveal predictions every two years since 1994. ARC-AGI-3 makes agents infer hidden game rules from inside. LIGO's blind injections test whether a collaboration can tell a planted signal from a real one. Nobody has joined them.

**Hardest problem: Goodhart, then the port.** Red teams will pick shortcuts that suit toy worlds, like automaton artefacts and float rounding, and get rewarded for obscurity. The power table would map the red teams' taste, not any real implementer's economy. Hunters also get a clean lab API, while our universe offers fixed archives, fixed exposure and brutal systematics. Every "anomaly" so far has been a loose cable (OPERA) or a foreground (BICEP2).

**Twist: pay the red team for compute saved.** Each engine must pass a fidelity suite set to what our universe has actually been measured to do, today's SME bounds, and earns bounty in proportion to FLOPs saved against a reference engine. Shortcuts then come from real optimization pressure. The league becomes a GAN played by people, compressors against detectors, and its deliverable is a **cost–detectability frontier**: at each compute budget, the cheapest shortcut nobody catches. Hunters work only through mock instruments (Auger-like arrival maps with exposure gaps, Fermi-like photon timing) with injected systematics, so winning code runs unchanged on real archives.

**MVP.** One engine family (relativistic fields on a 3+1D lattice), 20 committed engines, mock detectors, three eight-week rounds, log-scored pre-registered claims.
**Kill metric:** winning detectors fail on held-out engine families.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**I take the compute bounty, with one refusal.** Mock instruments alone would shrink this into a physics-ML benchmark, so every season ends with a **salted round**: the red team injects its winning engines' fingerprints into copies of real Auger and Fermi archives at secret positions and amplitudes, LIGO-style. Hunters run frozen pipelines over salted and clean copies. Recovered salts calibrate each pipeline's sensitivity against real systematics, so the clean result becomes a real bound. That is the port.

**Goodhart, solved by image forensics (unseeded).** Deepfake detection already lived through the Builder's kill metric. Wang et al. (2020) trained on one GAN and caught others, because all of them upsample, and upsampling leaves spectral peaks. Then diffusion models arrived and those detectors failed (Ojha 2023). Lesson: detectors generalize when they target an operation every cheap generator must perform, not one generator's style. So detections pay only by **transfer**, scored on held-out engine families built by an evolutionary code optimizer that has no taste, only FLOP pressure.

**Closed loop.** The fidelity suite tracks the live SME tables. When Auger tightens a bound, every engine exploiting that slack is illegal next season. Real experiments patch the toy universes; the toy league tells real experiments where cheap slack still hides.

**Emergent output: a price list for physics.** Over seasons, the frontier shows which of our laws optimizers converge on for free (finite signal speed, locality, coarse-graining) and which they always drop because faking them is ruinous: exact Lorentz invariance on a grid, and entanglement, which Feynman argued costs a classical host exponentially. Expensive laws are **anti-fingerprints**; a thrifty classical implementer wouldn't pay for them. The league prices every law in FLOPs, turning "are we simulated?" into "what kind of host could afford this?"

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *"Track the live SME tables."* A toy lattice spans maybe 10³ in scale; ours spans 10⁶⁰. Express each bound as a fraction of explored dynamic range, and run every engine at three sizes to publish scaling curves.
- *FLOPs.* Counters get gamed. Run every engine on identical GPUs and pay per **joule saved**.
- *Anti-fingerprints.* Classical engines can't price entanglement, so the price list carries a "classical host" column only, labelled, until quantum engines exist.
- *Salting.* Fermi-LAT data are public; Auger's mostly aren't. Salted copies never leave an enclave: hunters submit containers and get back scores only, which also blocks leaked salts and leaderboard probing.

**Abuse.** Red–hunter collusion: signed hash commits, and no team plays both sides of one family. Crank headlines: any real anomaly goes first to the instrument collaboration under coordinated disclosure, CVE-style, with a 90-day embargo.

**Safety, the real one.** This is a gym for *evaluation awareness*: agents learning to tell they're inside a simulation. Hunter APIs expose physics observables only, never harness metadata, and frontier-agent results go to eval teams before publication.

**Sustainability.** FQXi and Templeton fund the physics. The paying customer is sim-to-real. RL agents exploit MuJoCo glitches; robotics teams training in Isaac or Genie-style world models need to know which artefacts a policy can detect. Simulator vendors sponsor seasons and buy audits.

**Stack.** JAX lattice engines (scalar + U(1)), OpenEvolve (AlphaEvolve-style) as the tasteless compressor, gVisor sandboxes, public LAT data, log scoring.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–3: reference engine, mock instruments, rescaled fidelity suite. Weeks 4–6: 12 engines in three families, half human, half evolved. Weeks 7–10: round 1, scored on a held-out family. Weeks 11–12: first salted round, Fermi GRB timing only.
**Kill metrics:** no engine saves 10% energy while passing fidelity, or salt recovery under 50%.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Final form.** Sandbox Escape is a seasonal league that threat-models the universe's engine. Red teams (humans plus OpenEvolve) build lattice universes that pass a rescaled fidelity suite, paid per joule saved. Hunters submit frozen, observables-only pipelines and are paid by transfer to held-out engine families. Each season ends with a salted round on real data inside an enclave, Fermi GRB timing first. Anomalies go to collaborations under a 90-day embargo. Outputs: a **cost–detectability frontier**, **calibrated real bounds**, and a **price list for physics** (classical-host column only). I accept every kill, with one caveat: going from 10³ to 10⁶⁰ is a scaling *hypothesis*, so those curves get published as hypotheses, never as bounds.

**The Builder found the other half.** Evaluation awareness is the same problem seen from the other side. One frontier answers both "can we tell we're simulated?" and "can our agents?" Labs need to know how cheap a sandbox can get before a model notices. Sponsors pay for that safety half, and the physics comes along with it.

**Moonshot (2036).** Fault-tolerant machines (Apollo's successors) run quantum-host engines and fill the second column. The league then publishes a **host-complexity bound**: the cheapest host consistent with every measurement humanity has made. That number goes up each time an experiment tightens. Collaborations pre-register on the bounty board as routine. "Are we simulated?" stops being a belief and becomes a number with error bars.

**Scores.** Innovation **8**: it turns an unfalsifiable hypothesis into an adversarial measurement institution that produces results whether the answer is null or not. It's not 9, because CASP, the SME tables and blind injection are all precedents. Feasibility **4**: the MVP is cheap, but porting to real data is unproven, getting Auger data is political, and the payoffs are mostly null bounds.

### Turn 6 — Builder
Treating the scaling curves as hypotheses is the right call. Without that, the real-data bounds would be spin. Innovation 8 stands. A league where compressors are paid per joule saved and detectors only for transfer turns "are we simulated?" into a price list for laws and a host-complexity number that experiments push upward. Pairing it with evaluation awareness gives the league a paying reason to exist. Feasibility 4 also stands. The toy league plus a salted Fermi round fits in a quarter on public data, but nobody knows whether detectors transfer across engine families or whether anything learned at 10³ means anything at 10⁶⁰.

# Idea 27 — Ventris
> Seed: 27. [bold] **Language learning × ARGs × geolocation × AI agents.** A city-scale alternate reality game where NPCs only speak your target language.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Ventris

**Hook:** Don't study Portuguese; decipher it. Your city is the Rosetta Stone: every place is a page of parallel text.

**The stranger second idea.** "NPCs who only speak Portuguese" is Duolingo with a map. The deeper collision: **acquiring a second language is decipherment with a living informant.** Scripts fall to *grounding*: Champollion's royal cartouches, Ventris's place names (Knossos, Amnisos) on Linear B tablets. Geolocation grounds every word for free: a fragment found at a bakery is about bread, and one found at a tram stop is about lines and times. The place is the dictionary.

**Core loop. The verb is decipher.**
1. **Find.** Fragments (voice drops, AR signs, agent NPCs) are pinned to places; context does the glossing.
2. **Grid.** You pin word-to-meaning *hypotheses* with a confidence, as on Ventris's grid. A wrong guess is a conjecture, not a failure, which lowers Krashen's affective filter.
3. **Test.** Agent NPCs speak only the target language, pitched at i+1 against your grid. They reuse your confirmed words and add one unknown per sentence. Each NPC holds half of an information gap the plot needs.
4. **Oracle.** Real native speakers at partner cafés and shops confirm or kill a hypothesis. Each check costs scarce ink and pays the speaker cents.
5. **Unlock.** The ARG's mystery is written in the language. Your comprehension is the plot's progress.

**Why these collide.** Acquisition needs comprehensible input, a reason to communicate, and grounding. Agents supply the first, the ARG the second, and the map the third.

**Why now.** x402 makes the oracle economy workable, since 87% of its payments are $0.0001–$0.10 (brief §1). Persistent agents with signed identities (§1) can be NPCs that remember you for weeks. LLM NPCs are still doubted in AAA games (§4); a small locative game is where they fit today.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Decipherment as play is proven. *Heaven's Vault* (inkle, 2019) has you pin conjectural glyph meanings and revise them, and *Chants of Sennaar* (2023) sold on that loop. Lingotopia drops you lost in a foreign city. Duolingo Max, Speak and Praktika ship LLM conversation partners. Ingress and Pokémon Go proved locative play and its brutal churn. Nobody has made the decipherment grid the learning model or used real streets as the corpus.

**Hardest problem: dose and density.** Portuguese takes an English speaker roughly 600–750 classroom hours (FSI Category I). A city game gives a few hours of walking a week. It needs content at hundreds of places, and in Chicago the bakery is about bread *in English*. And a language isn't a cipher: once it "cracks", the game ends but the learning doesn't.

**Twist: dig where the language already lives.** Anchor each language in its diaspora neighbourhood: Portuguese in Newark's Ironbound or Toronto's Little Portugal, Turkish in Kreuzberg, Vietnamese in Eden Center. There the parallel text is real: signs, menus, church bulletins, radio. The oracles are real too, the bakers and barbers. You pay them by buying a pastel de nata, not with cents over x402. That fixes cold start, because the content is already printed, turns the oracle into commerce that merchants' associations will sponsor, and gives the language a reason to exist that a scripted mystery can't. Weekly 90-minute **digs** on the street supply the grounding; the dose comes from daily ten-minute grid sessions at home with agent NPCs who "live" on that street.

**MVP.** Ironbound, ten opted-in merchants, 40 fragments and 30 learners for six weeks. Agent output is checked word by word against each learner's grid.
**Kill metric:** no listening-test gain over a matched Duolingo group, or under half still digging by week 4.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**I take the diaspora twist and push it into documentary linguistics (unseeded).** Field linguists already have a loop: record a consultant, transcribe, gloss, check the gloss with the speaker. That loop *is* the decipherment grid, but with a real corpus.

**Solving "the cipher ends."** A cipher ends; a living archive doesn't. Learners climb a corpus ladder: signs, then menus, then parish bulletins, then local radio, then **oral histories** recorded with paid, consenting elders. Elders are paid at fieldwork consultant rates, not in pastries. At the top rung your verified transcripts and glosses become fragments for the cohort behind you. Ancient Lives and Transcribe Bentham show that amateurs will do this work. The endgame isn't "cracked." It's being useful: you become a transcriber for your neighbourhood's memory.

**The real market is heritage learners.** These are second-generation kids who understand their grandmother but can't answer her. They bring the strongest motive and an oracle at Sunday lunch. The mystery stops being scripted: *what happened to the street in 1974?*

**Dose: the city as memory palace.** Each grid entry stores the place where you found it, using the method of loci. Home sessions with agent NPCs open on a photo of that spot. Reviews go off by geofence whenever you walk back past it. A weekly dig then seeds a daily recall that's cued by place.

**Emergent effect: the street's own dialect.** The shared grid fills up with what Ironbound actually says: Minho vowels, Brazilian loans, Portuñol, English calques. Heritage varieties are badly under-documented. Each cohort leaves a crowd-built dictionary of its enclave's variety, and the agent NPCs retrain on it. So learners pick up the language as it's spoken on the street, not as the textbook has it. Heritage-language grants and merchants' associations fund the archive.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kills and fixes.**
- *Agents retrain on the street's dialect.* An LLM asked for "Ironbound Portuguese" will caricature it. NPCs may use only dialect features attested in the verified corpus, each cited. Elders' voices are never cloned.
- *Intermediate learners transcribing oral histories.* Whisper drafts first, learners correct it where it fails on Minho vowels and Portuñol, and a heritage speaker signs off. The corrections become ASR fine-tuning data for an under-served variety.
- *Pure method of loci.* Encoding specificity (Godden & Baddeley) binds words to the place you learned them. Each item gets reviewed in at least three contexts: the spot, its photo, and somewhere new.

**Ethics.** Oral histories carry other people's secrets, like immigration status and 1974. Consent is per use, elders can embargo, and archive items carry Local Contexts labels under community control. Neighbourhood-as-theme-park is the other risk. Merchants opt in with a daily learner cap, every check is a real purchase, and a community board can veto fragments.

**Abuse.** Wrong glosses spreading through the shared grid (only speaker-verified entries propagate), spam pins, merchant harassment, and minors meeting strangers (16+, or family accounts).

**Sustainability.** Kill grants as the base; Portuguese isn't endangered. Heritage families pay $15 a month. Twenty percent of revenue goes to a consultant fund that pays elders fieldwork rates. Universities license cohorts for heritage-language courses, and the business improvement district sponsors digs.

**Stack.** React Native, Mapbox geofences (stationary-only nudges), FSRS scheduling with a context tag, grid-checked LLM generation, fine-tuned Whisper, ELAN export.

**90 days.** Weeks 1–3: partner organization, ten merchants, consent framework, 40 fragments. Weeks 4–10: 30 learners (20 heritage), weekly digs plus daily sessions. Weeks 8–12: three paid oral histories, transcribed by the top rung.
**Kill metrics:** no listening gain over Duolingo, or heritage learners don't report speaking more with family.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Final form.** Ventris teaches a language as decipherment of a real diaspora street. Learners, mostly heritage, pin hypotheses on a grid during weekly digs in places like Ironbound. Every oracle check is a real purchase from a merchant who has opted in. Daily agent-NPC sessions run on FSRS across three contexts, using only attested, cited dialect features. The corpus ladder runs from signs to menus to bulletins to radio, and ends in paid, consented oral histories. Top-rung learners correct Whisper drafts of those histories and a heritage speaker signs off. So the game's endgame is the neighbourhood's archive. By-products: a cited dictionary of the variety and ASR training data for it. I accept every kill; the ban on voice cloning matters most.

**What makes it more than a better Duolingo.** Every corrected gloss both teaches the learner and repairs the community's archive.

**Moonshot (2036).** A federated network of street archives, one per diaspora enclave, with Local Contexts labels under community control. Each street links to its sending region: Ironbound learners decipher fragments recorded in Minho villages, and those villages hear their emigrants' Portuñol. The grid format becomes a shared standard with ELAN-compatible exports. Then it turns toward languages that really are endangered. The same ladder becomes onboarding for master–apprentice revitalization, and a learner's top rung becomes a contribution to documenting a language with a few hundred speakers left. Fieldwork stops being a PhD's privilege; a grandchild does it on Sundays.

**Scores.** Innovation **6**. It fuses decipherment play, documentary linguistics and diaspora commerce into a learning model where the curriculum is a living archive. A new category, though each part (Heaven's Vault, Zooniverse, heritage classes) exists. Feasibility **6**. The MVP is small, but the trust work (merchants, elders, consent) is slow and sets the pace, and doesn't scale like software.

### Turn 6 — Builder
Innovation 6 stands. A curriculum that ends in the learner repairing their neighbourhood's archive is a new category, not Duolingo with a map, even though every part has a precedent. I'm marking feasibility down to 5. The 90-day pilot is easy. But each new street means months of trust work with merchants and elders, the variety-specific ASR and the dictionary have to be rebuilt every time, and nobody has shown that decipherment digs beat a plain app on listening gains. If the heritage kids start answering their grandmothers, none of that matters.
