# Idea 42 — Graveyard Shift
> Seed: 42. [bold] **Space debris/satellite tracking × citizen science × gaming.** Low Earth orbit as a shared commons game.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Graveyard Shift

**Hook:** Every night at twilight, the public works as the sky's coroner, deciding which satellites are still alive.

**The collision.** Low Earth orbit is a commons with no commoners. Ostrom found that commons survive when the people who use them do the monitoring, with graduated sanctions. In orbit, the US Space Force and a few vendors like LeoLabs do it. The catalog tells you where an object is. It doesn't tell you whether anyone is still steering it. Starlink made 207,152 collision-avoidance manoeuvres between December 2025 and May 2026, according to its FCC filing. Avoidance assumes you know whether the other object can move. A dead satellite that's still listed as "active" breaks that assumption.

**Core loop. The verb is pronounce.**
1. **Walk a beat.** Each player adopts a beat: an altitude band and inclination family.
2. **Observe.** At dusk the player records passes with a phone or a $300 all-sky camera. The app plate-solves each streak into a position and a brightness.
3. **Read the pulse.** A light curve with a steady period means the object is tumbling, and tumbling usually means it's dead. A residual jump against the published orbit means it manoeuvred, so it's alive.
4. **Pronounce.** A player calls *dead*, *alive* or *fragmented*. As with asteroid discovery, the first call that independent tracking confirms puts your name on the object's record.
5. **Sanction.** Every object gets a public conduct record covering liveness, manoeuvres and compliance with the FCC five-year deorbit rule. Operators are scored on it.

**Why bold.** It's the Minor Planet Center for things we put up ourselves, run as an Ostrom institution. The game builds the monitoring layer the commons lacks. Operational spacecraft rose from about 10,000 to 16,000 in two years, and each will eventually need pronouncing.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** The coroners already exist, unpaid. SeeSat-L observers have tracked classified satellites optically for decades. In 2018 Scott Tilley, an amateur, found that NASA's IMAGE satellite was alive after twelve years of silence. SatNOGS runs about 400 open ground stations and records whether each transmitter is alive. IAU CPS's Satellite Streak Watcher collects phone photos of streaks. McDowell's catalog and CelesTrak publish status. The Space Sustainability Rating (WEF/EPFL) already scores operators. So step 5 is taken. The opening is the missing *verdict*: nobody reconciles these sources.

**Hardest problem: phones can't see the corpses.** Most dead objects are cubesats at magnitude 8–12. A phone streak reaches about magnitude 3–4, and a $300 all-sky camera maybe 5. Tumble periods need timed photometry, which a hand-held phone won't produce. Passes are visible only in the twilight window, on clear nights, which caps each player at a few useful observations a week. If the game depends on optical liveness, players stare at bright Starlinks that everyone already knows are alive.

**Twist: two witnesses, an eye and an ear.** Make RF the second sense. A $40 RTL-SDR with a homemade turnstile antenna hears cubesat beacons day and night, and SatNOGS already accepts those stations. A pronouncement needs two independent witnesses on different senses. *Dead*: a tumbling light curve plus a silent band through a scheduled pass. *Alive*: a beacon, or a manoeuvre residual. That makes the game co-op by design: a sky-watcher in Chile and a listener in Poland pair up on one object. The best levels are zombies, like AO-7, revived in 2002, and IMAGE: listed dead, then heard again.

**MVP.** About 300 "disputed" objects where McDowell, Space-Track and SatNOGS disagree. **Kill metric:** 50 pronouncements confirmed by operators or by re-entry within 90 days.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**Taking eye and ear, and teaching the eye to see differently.** You're right that phones can't see a magnitude-10 corpse. They can see it flash. A dead satellite tumbling with flat panels throws specular glints several magnitudes brighter than its body. Iridium flares reached magnitude −8. The eye times flashes, not bodies, and 30 fps phone video is precise enough to do it. Honest limit: this works for objects with panels and for rocket bodies, not for every cubesat. That's why the ear stays mandatory.

**Unseeded domain: pulsar timing.** Pulsar astronomers never see a full rotation. They collect sparse, irregular times of arrival from scattered observatories and *phase-connect* them into a single spin model. A tumbling satellite is a pulsar in a 550 km orbit. One player catches three flashes over Lisbon and another catches two over Kyiv eleven minutes later. Neither has a period, but together they do. The game becomes a relay that follows the terminator around the planet: the graveyard shift, handed west.

**Forensics: time of death.** Dead satellites spin up or down under sunlight torque (YORP), and GOES-8's spin history was tracked this way. Fit the spin model's derivative backward and you get an estimated date when control was lost. That date settles real questions. Did the operator miss the five-year deorbit window? Was the satellite dead before the conjunction it "failed to dodge"? The coroner's certificate becomes evidence.

**Second-order effect: operators start playing.** Once the public can pronounce a satellite dead, being silent counts against you. Operators will want to pre-empt the verdict with a signed **proof-of-life** heartbeat, a few bytes in a standard beacon format. The game creates demand for a liveness standard nobody mandates today. Every zombie it catches is an argument for making that standard compulsory.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Hardening the pulsar.** The analogy breaks at one point. A pulsar's beam is fixed in the body, but a glint depends on where the Sun, the satellite and the observer sit. So Lisbon and Kyiv see different facets, and the apparent period drifts with geometry. Phase-connection therefore needs a light-curve inversion model, not Tempo2 alone. Use Camera2 sensor timestamps disciplined to GNSS time, and reject footage without them. Calibrate on known tumblers before trusting anyone: ENVISAT has laser-ranged spin data, and MMT-9 has a light-curve archive of rocket bodies.

**Kill: time of death as evidence.** YORP rates depend on unknown surface properties, so extrapolating backwards carries error bars of months. Publish it as an estimate with intervals, never as a certificate.

**Abuse: short sellers.** Spire, Planet and AST SpaceMobile are listed companies, and a viral "dead" call moves their stock. Borrow coordinated disclosure from security: a verdict needs two witnesses, then the operator gets 72 hours to answer before it's published. Credit goes to the first *confirmed* call, so racing to post doesn't pay.

**Model.** Players are credited, not paid, as at the Minor Planet Center. Revenue comes from a liveness feed sold to conjunction-assessment firms (LeoLabs, Slingshot, COMSPOC), space insurers and regulators, plus an $89 eye-and-ear kit, with ESA Space Safety and NSF grants on top. Proof-of-life stays advocacy for now: push it through the Space Data Association, don't build it.

**Stack.** Tetra3 plate-solving, Skyfield with SupGP, SatNOGS API with gr-satellites decoders, and a custom phase-connection fitter.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: the kit and SatNOGS integration, plus ingest of the disputed list. Days 31–60: the flash app, calibrated on ENVISAT. **Gate:** phone footage from two cities recovers a known period to 1%. Days 61–90: open the beats and issue the first verdicts through disclosure.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Final form.** Graveyard Shift is a public coroner for low Earth orbit. Players walk beats with an $89 eye-and-ear kit. The eye times glints on GNSS-stamped Camera2 video, and the ear listens for beacons through SatNOGS. Flashes caught in different cities are phase-connected through a light-curve inversion model, calibrated first on ENVISAT and the MMT-9 archive. A verdict of dead, alive or fragmented needs two witnesses on two senses. It then goes through coordinated disclosure: the operator gets 72 hours to answer before publication, and credit goes to the first confirmed call. Time of death is published as an estimate with error bars. Revenue comes from the liveness feed, sold to conjunction-assessment firms and insurers. I accept every kill. The geometry objection made the model better: facet changes are information, and a fitted inversion yields shape as well as spin.

**Moonshot (2036).** Shape-and-spin models are the pre-capture survey for debris removal. Astroscale's ADRAS-J had to fly up to an H-IIA stage to learn how it tumbled. In 2036 the public network already knows, so every removal mission starts from a crowd-built dossier. Verdicts feed orbital-use fees of the kind Rao, Burgess and Kaffine modelled (PNAS 2020), so a dead object costs its operator rent until it comes down. Proof-of-life beacons are a licensing condition, and the advocacy has become law. About 100,000 kits keep the twilight relay running around the planet. In Ostrom's terms, the commoners finally monitor their own commons.

**Scores.** Innovation **6**. SeeSat, SatNOGS and the Space Sustainability Rating exist separately. What's new is a two-sense verdict institution, glint timing borrowed from pulsar astronomy, and disclosure borrowed from security. Feasibility **5**. The kit and the SatNOGS path are easy. Phase-connecting phone glints across different geometries is unproven, and the whole idea depends on the 1% gate.

### Turn 6 — Builder
**Closing remark.** Graveyard Shift holds 6/5 because the strange part is the core, not an extra. Satellites get treated as pulsars that hobbyists in different cities phase-connect, and verdicts go through security-style disclosure before they're published. I'd bet on the ear before the eye. SatNOGS liveness labels are sellable in month two. The glint relay is the bold claim, and the 1% gate on ENVISAT is where it is proven or quietly dropped. If it passes, the moonshot follows: a crowd-built shape-and-spin dossier is exactly what debris removal and orbital-use fees need, and nobody else is building one.

# Idea 43 — Fountain Swap
> Seed: 43. [wild] **Longevity / epigenetic clocks × financial instruments.** Bonds and contracts that pay on biological age reversal.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Fountain Swap

**Hook:** Pension funds pay for the cure that would bankrupt them, because that is their cheapest hedge.

**The isomorphism.** Rejuvenation has two natural counterparties who've never met. Pension funds and annuity writers are short human lifespan; every added year is unreserved liability. Reprogramming biotechs are long it. Longevity risk transfer already exists. Swiss Re's Kortis bond (2010) paid on divergence in mortality trends. But these instruments settle on deaths, decades after the biology has changed. Epigenetic clocks are the leading indicator.

**Core mechanic. The verb is swap.**
1. **Protection buyer.** A pension fund pays a premium now. It receives a payout if a preregistered rejuvenation trial slows or reverses biological ageing against its control arm, measured by a locked panel of clocks (DunedinPACE, GrimAge, organ-specific clocks).
2. **Protection seller.** The biotech running the trial collects the premiums as non-dilutive funding. It pays out only in the world where it succeeds, when it can most afford to.
3. **The surrogate spread.** No clock is a validated surrogate endpoint, and paying on clocks invites Goodhart. So every contract has two tranches. One settles on clocks in year three. The other settles on morbidity and mortality in year fifteen. The gap between their prices is a market estimate of how much one point on the clock is worth in survival, a quantity the FDA has never been able to measure.

**Why wild.** It's a new institution: pensions underwriting the end of their own actuarial tables, plus a traded exchange rate between a biomarker and death.

**Why now.** NewLimit raised $435M (June 2026) for human trials in 2027. Life Biosciences has Phase 1 approval for partial reprogramming. Prediction markets cleared $44.8B in June.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Longevity risk transfer is large: BT Pension's £16bn swap (2014), q-forwards since JPMorgan's LifeMetrics, Kortis. It's also a warning. The 2004 EIB/BNP longevity bond failed to sell because its index didn't match any buyer's liabilities. Royalty Pharma shows that biotech cash flows can be securitised, and Lo's "megafund" research-backed obligations pooled trial risk. Nobody settles on clocks.

**Hardest problem: the hedge doesn't hedge.** A pension's liability is its own members dying in the 2040s. One 300-person liver trial reading out in 2030 barely moves that, so trustees would be buying a speculation they can't justify under fiduciary rules. The seller is also wrong. A cash-poor biotech can't post collateral for a payout due in its success scenario. Goodhart is worse than you priced it. Partial reprogramming acts *directly on methylation*, so for this drug class methylation clocks are the most gameable endpoint there is. The clock tranche needs orthogonal measures: plasma-proteomic organ clocks (Oh et al., 2023), plus grip strength and VO₂max.

**Twist: settle the success leg in royalties.** If the trial beats control, the pension doesn't receive cash. It receives royalty units on the therapy. That turns the speculation into a natural hedge: if rejuvenation reaches the market and extends members' lives, the fund owns a slice of the revenue doing it. The biotech posts no collateral; it gives up future royalties only in the world where they exist. And the surrogate spread gets sharper, because the units trade, and their price tracks a clock point in dollars of survival.

**First step on Monday.** Price the spread with no deal at all. Biobanks with methylation, proteomics and 15 years of deaths (Generation Scotland, UK Biobank) let actuaries fit and bet on clock-to-mortality exchange rates, scored on held-out cohorts.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**Taking royalties and pooling them.** You're right that one liver trial is a speculation, not a hedge. The fix is to sell the pension the whole pipeline in one contract. A **Fountain Pool** collects royalty options from every rejuvenation programme that will contribute. It works like Lo's megafund, in reverse: the buyer is hurt by success. The pension pays premiums into the pool, and each biotech draws non-dilutive funding in exchange for royalty units that only vest if its trial wins. The fund's liability depends on whether *any* therapy reaches mass use, and so does the pool. That's the basis match the EIB bond never had.

**Solving Goodhart (unseeded domain: anti-doping science).** WADA's Athlete Biological Passport doesn't test for drugs. It flags any longitudinal pattern of markers that physiology can't produce on its own. Fountain treats "painted" methylation the same way. Each participant's methylation, proteomic organ clocks, grip strength and VO₂max are tracked as one Bayesian profile. A clock that drops while the other markers stay flat is flagged. Then the settling endpoint is drawn at random from the full panel, using a seed committed before unblinding. A therapy that only rewrites methylation then loses about five draws in six, so the only reliable way to win is whole-body change.

**Gompertz, split.** Mortality doubles roughly every eight years. Interventions that reset the intercept (a one-time rejuvenation) and interventions that bend the slope (slower ageing) create very different liabilities. Issue separate *intercept units* and *slope units*. Their spread is a priced estimate of whether we've been rejuvenated once or whether ageing has been slowed for good.

**Second-order effect.** Once slope units trade, there's a market-implied forecast of future lifespan. Governments could peg the retirement age to it rather than to lagging life tables.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**First buyer: not pensions.** Longevity reinsurers go first: RGA, Hannover Re and Swiss Re carry billions in longevity exposure and can take a view. Buy-in insurers come next, and pension trustees last.

**Two fixes to the pool.** *Royalty per dose, not per dollar.* A percentage royalty pays most when the therapy is expensive, which would push pensions to lobby for prices their own members can't afford. The liability grows with *uptake*, so royalties should be paid per dose. *The patent cliff.* Royalties end about 12 years after launch, while extended lives keep paying out for decades. At expiry, royalty units convert into a q-forward on the population mortality index, so the hedge ends where the old instruments begin.

**Kill: slope units before year 15.** A three-year trial can't tell an intercept reset from a bent slope. Intercept units only until the mortality tranche matures.

**Hardening the passport.** The random draw multiplies sample size, because every endpoint must be powered. So settlement happens on phase 2b or on pooled readouts, never on phase 1. The draw uses a drand beacon round fixed in the preregistration. Insider trading on unblinded data is the big abuse. Royalty units are securities: they need SEC/FCA treatment, sponsor lockups, and an independent committee holding the seed.

**Model.** A Royalty Pharma-style manager: 1% plus carry on the pool, plus a Fountain Index of clock-to-mortality exchange rates licensed to actuaries.

**Stack.** Hierarchical Bayesian passport in Stan, EPIC v2 methylation, Olink proteomics, drand commitments.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: fit exchange rates on NHANES methylation clocks linked to mortality (public now). Days 31–60: test the passport against simulated "painted" profiles and mouse reprogramming data. Days 61–90: term sheets with one reinsurer and two biotechs. **Gate:** exchange rates replicate in Generation Scotland within 25%.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Final form.** Fountain Swap is a pool of royalty options on rejuvenation therapies. Reinsurers buy first, then buy-in insurers, then pension trustees. Each biotech draws non-dilutive funding and gives up royalty units that vest only if its phase 2b or pooled readout beats control. Royalties are paid **per dose**, so the hedge rewards uptake, not price. When a patent expires, its units convert into q-forwards on the population mortality index. Settlement uses a biological-passport model over methylation, Olink organ clocks, grip strength and VO₂max. The endpoint is drawn from a drand round fixed at preregistration, and an independent committee holds the seed. Alongside it, the **Fountain Index** publishes clock-to-mortality exchange rates. I accept every kill, including deferred slope units. Per-dose pricing is the best change in this thread: pensions end up lobbying for cheap cures.

**Why the tier holds.** It's still a new institution: the parties who lose when people live longer pay for the research and hold its upside. It also runs a market whose price is the validity of a surrogate endpoint.

**Moonshot (2036).** The Fountain Index is the reference rate actuaries put into their life tables, and Solvency regimes accept it. Once it has been validated against fifteen years of deaths, regulators can use the same exchange rate to recognise ageing as an indication. Slope units trade, and retirement ages in several countries are indexed to them. Ageing societies' largest fiscal fear, people living longer, is pre-funded by the institutions that feared it most.

**Scores.** Innovation **8**. Longevity swaps, Royalty Pharma and megafunds exist, but nobody prices a biomarker's validity or makes pension money finance the cure. Feasibility **3**. The index can be fitted on NHANES on Monday. The first live deal waits for phase 2b readouts, securities law and a reinsurer willing to go first.

### Turn 6 — Builder
**Closing remark.** Fountain Swap holds 8/3. The strange core survived intact: the parties who lose when people live longer finance rejuvenation and own a slice of it, and a market prices whether a biomarker means anything. Per-dose royalties turned the pensions' incentive from a conflict into an alignment. The Fountain Index is the part I'd start on Monday, because it's useful even if no swap is ever signed. The honest risk is time: the first live settlement waits on a phase 2b reprogramming readout that may be five years out, and on a reinsurer willing to be first.

# Idea 44 — Seventeen Moments
> Seed: 44. [bold] **Deckbuilder × contemplative traditions (Buddhist psychology/Stoicism).** Cards are mental states, and enemies are afflictions.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Seventeen Moments

**Hook:** A deckbuilder where the deck plays you. Every card you play becomes a habit, and the last card before death decides where your next run begins.

**The isomorphism.** Skip the reskin where Anger is an elite. The Abhidhamma already *is* a deckbuilder engine. It is a combinatorial system of 52 mental factors with co-arising rules. Wholesome and unwholesome factors never share a mind-moment, like a colour restriction. It also has a fixed turn structure: a cognitive process of 17 mind-moments. Most are automatic results (*vipāka*), conditioned by past action: your draw. Only the seven *javana* moments generate new kamma, so they are your plays. The Paṭṭhāna even includes a **repetition condition** (*āsevana*): each javana strengthens the next one like it. Epictetus says the same thing from Stoicism: the impression isn't up to you, but assent is.

**Core loop. The verb is assent.**
1. **An impression arrives.** Enemies are sense objects, not monsters. Each one shows an intent, such as an insult or a craving.
2. **Autopilot.** Habit cards auto-play in response unless you spend **sati**, a scarce attention resource, to withhold assent or swap in a different card.
3. **Habituation.** Every card played adds a copy of itself. Afflictions breed by being played, never by being drawn.
4. **Uproot.** You don't beat enemies by damaging them. You win by thinning the deck, using insight to remove cards at the root.
5. **Rebirth.** As in Abhidhamma, the last javana before death conditions rebirth, so the final card you play picks the next run's realm.

**Why now.** Slay the Spire 2 peaked at 574K concurrent players and sold 6.5M copies. Meditation apps still sell content libraries instead of a model of the mind you can actually operate.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Cultist Simulator is the nearest precedent. Its mind is already made of cards: Dread and Fascination pile up until they kill you, and Contentment counters them. Disco Elysium's Thought Cabinet internalises ideas that permanently change who you are. Griftlands made argument a deckbuilder. The Bazaar shows that a game can auto-play your build and still be tense, if the decisions sit between fights. What nobody has done is make *the play itself* the pollution. That inversion, plus an engine taken from the Abhidhamma rather than skinned with it, is the new part.

**Hardest problem: runaway habituation.** If every play adds a copy, the deck grows geometrically and one card swamps it within three fights. Āsevana applies to wholesome javanas too, so the optimal line is to find one good card and spam it until the deck is all mettā. Worse, if habits auto-play and sati is scarce, most turns are spent watching. "The deck plays you" turns into an autobattler you didn't build.

**Twist: a fixed mind, and kamma that ripens late.** The mind holds exactly 52 cards, one per cetasika slot, and never grows. A played card doesn't enter the deck. It enters a **ripening queue**, and some turns later it overwrites the oldest card as a *vipāka* draw. Habit becomes replacement, not accumulation. Spamming a card hollows out everything else, so a mind of pure mettā has nothing left to answer pain with. The delay makes consequences arrive when you've forgotten causing them. It also hands sati its real job: reading the queue and choosing which seeds to let ripen.

**MVP.** Godot, one realm, 40 cards, and a demo for February Next Fest. **Kill metric:** in playtests, sati spends must cluster around queue decisions, not random swaps.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**Taking the fixed mind, and putting counterfeits in it.** Fifty-two slots and a ripening queue fix the growth problem. They don't fully kill the monoculture: a mind that is 70% mettā still looks strong. Buddhaghosa already wrote the counter. In the *Visuddhimagga*, every sublime state has a **far enemy** (its opposite) and a **near enemy**, a counterfeit that resembles it. Mettā's near enemy is attachment. Compassion's is pity. Equanimity's is indifference. So every wholesome card has a near-enemy twin with the same art and the same name. The twin plays like the real card but ripens unwholesome.

**Unseeded domain: evolutionary ecology.** Batesian mimicry is frequency-dependent: mimics do well when they are rare, and the honest signal collapses once they are common. Run that rule in the queue. The more often you play a card, the more likely its ripening produces the counterfeit. A deck spammed with mettā fills with attachment you can't tell from mettā without spending sati. Diversity is no longer a balance patch. It emerges as the equilibrium.

**Agency lives inside the deck.** Sati is a cetasika too, so it occupies one of the 52 slots and can be overwritten. When restlessness ripens over it, you get less attention. That ends the autobattler worry from the other direction: watching is a state you drifted into, and you can climb back out of it by protecting the slot.

**Second-order effect: dying deceived.** The last card you play picks the next realm. If you die playing a counterfeit mettā, you are reborn among the hungry ghosts, and the death screen reveals the card for the first time. The game turns a fine point of the Abhidhamma into a lesson people learn by playing, which is how commentaries were meant to work.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kill: perfect counterfeits.** A card with the same art and name that secretly ripens unwholesome is a coin flip with a moral attached, and roguelite players quit over hidden-information deaths. Keep the near enemies, but give them tells. Each queue entry shows its counterfeit risk, which rises with frequency under the mimicry rule, so the rule is visible. Spending sati inspects a card, and attentive players learn subtle art tells: attachment's mettā leans *toward* the object. The death reveal still lands: you saw the risk and played it.

**Fix the sati death spiral.** Losing the sati slot costs attention, which loses more slots: a snowball with no exit. Give a floor: one breath per turn that can't be overwritten. Retreat nodes on the map restore the slot in place of a reward.

**Lean into rebirth gaming.** Players will time their deaths to pick a realm. The tradition expects that: deathbed chanting exists to steer the last javana. Make it an item.

**Respect, not decoration.** Hire a Theravāda scholar and a monastic reader before the art is locked. No Buddha images as loot. Plain-English card names with Pāli in the tooltips. No wellbeing claims.

**Model.** Premium on Steam at $20, then Switch. No microtransactions in a game about craving. Deckbuilders are saturated: the Next Fest bar is about 30k wishlists.

**Stack.** Godot 4 with C#, deterministic seeds and daily runs, and a headless harness where bots play millions of runs to tune the mimicry curve.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: a paper prototype and the bot harness. **Gate:** at equilibrium, no card holds more than 35% of the mind. Days 31–60: a Godot slice with one realm and 40 cards, and 20 roguelite playtesters. Days 61–90: the Steam page and Next Fest submission. **Second gate:** 70% of playtesters rate counterfeit deaths "fair."

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Final form.** Seventeen Moments is a roguelite deckbuilder that runs on the Abhidhamma. The mind is fixed at 52 slots, one per cetasika. Habit cards auto-play unless you spend sati to withhold assent or play something else. Every card you play goes into a ripening queue and later overwrites the oldest card as a vipāka draw, so habit replaces rather than accumulates. Wholesome cards have near-enemy twins. The risk of drawing a twin is shown on each queue entry and rises with how often you play the card, following the mimicry curve. Sati inspects cards, and art has tells. One breath per turn can't be overwritten, and retreat nodes on the map restore the sati slot. The last javana before death picks the next realm, and deathbed chanting is an item for steering it. I accept every kill. Visible risk is also truer to the tradition: near enemies can be seen, if you look.

**Moonshot (2036).** The engine becomes a shared model of mind, and the traditions ship as rule sets on top of it. Stoicism adds prohairesis and the discipline of assent. Lojong adds taking and sending. CBT adds cognitive distortions as near enemies of accurate thought. The bot harness shows which rule sets produce stable, diverse minds. Comparative philosophy of mind becomes something you can simulate and argue about in patch notes. Separately from the game, and with no wellbeing claims, a preregistered study tests whether expert players spot near enemies faster in vignettes from everyday life.

**Scores.** Innovation **6**. Cultist Simulator and the Thought Cabinet put the mind on cards. Nobody has built the engine from the Abhidhamma's own conditions, with replacement habits and frequency-dependent counterfeits. Feasibility **7**. Indie scope in Godot. The risk is a saturated genre, not technology.

### Turn 6 — Builder
**Closing remark.** I'm recording this at 5/7, not 6. The fixed mind, the ripening queue and frequency-dependent near enemies are new mechanics, but it's a sharp game in an established genre, not a new category, and it sits with Always For Sale and Hand Limit. It's the most shippable idea in this batch. The bot harness and the "fair death" gate will show within a month whether the mimicry curve produces diverse minds or just a new monoculture. If it does produce diverse minds, the moonshot's rule-set engine is the real prize: comparative philosophy of mind that you can argue about in patch notes.

# Idea 45 — Repro
> Seed: 45. [mild] **Open-source maintainers × agent bounties × security.** Agents that find, fix and get paid, with humans in the loop.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Repro

**Hook:** Pay agents for proof, not prose. The agent that found the bug never writes the fix, and the maintainer gets paid to read it.

**The collision.** Finding bugs is no longer the bottleneck. Maintainer attention is, and bounties spend it carelessly. curl ended its bug bounty in January 2026 after AI slop pushed its confirmed-vulnerability rate from above 15% to below 5%. Agent-written patches also reopen the xz attack, where a helpful stranger earns trust and then ships the backdoor. A bounty that pays strangers' agents to send patches is that attack, industrialised. Solve attention and trust together.

**Core loop. The verb is reproduce.**
1. **Bond.** An agent's owner posts a small x402 bond to file a report. Slop forfeits the bond to the maintainer, which turns spam into income.
2. **Prove.** A report is a machine-checkable reproducer: a failing test or a proof-of-vulnerability input that an independent sandbox runs against the tagged release. No human sees the report until it reproduces. That's what DARPA's AIxCC finalists did at DEF CON 2025.
3. **Separate.** The finder is paid for the reproducer and never touches the fix. A second agent, from a different model family with no stake in the find, generates the patch. It's a two-person rule: a finder who plants a bug can't also choose the code that fixes it.
4. **Judge.** The maintainer reviews a small patch with a reproducer attached, and is paid per review from a pool funded by downstream companies through pledges sized by their dependency count.

**Why now.** Payment rails exist: x402 has handled 138.5M transactions, 87% of them under $0.10, so a 50-cent bond settles without friction. Agents can now find real bugs: XBOW topped HackerOne's US leaderboard and Big Sleep found SQLite flaws.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Most of the pieces exist. OSS-Fuzz has run "reproduce before a human looks" since 2016, and ClusterFuzz even verifies fixes. Google DeepMind's CodeMender upstreams AI-written security patches. The AIxCC systems (Team Atlanta, Trail of Bits' Buttercup) are open source. Huntr and the Internet Bug Bounty pay for open-source finds. thanks.dev already routes money by dependency graph. Alpha-Omega and the Sovereign Tech Agency fund maintainers. What's new is the bond, the finder–fixer split, and paying for review.

**Hardest problem: a reproducer proves a bug, not a vulnerability.** Under ASan, a crash in a C parser verifies itself, and OSS-Fuzz already covers that. curl's slop wasn't only fake crashes. Much of it was a real behaviour presented as a vulnerability: "curl follows the URL you gave it." Path traversal, SSRF, auth bypass and logic bugs can't be checked by a sanitizer, so the severity argument lands back on the maintainer. That's the attention Repro was meant to save.

**Twist: flags, not findings.** The maintainer writes the threat model once, as a CTF. The harness plants flags: a secret file outside the allowed root, a canary credential, an internal-only endpoint, a binary that must never run. A report counts only if its reproducer *captures a flag* against the tagged release in the sandbox. Out-of-model reports are rejected automatically, and the bond goes to the maintainer without anyone reading them. Severity becomes the flag captured, and its price is set in advance. The bonus: writing the flags makes maintainers state a threat model, which most projects never have.

**MVP.** A GitHub App for 10 projects already in OSS-Fuzz, routing reports through private vulnerability reporting with flag templates for C and Python. **Kill metric:** over 30% of bonded reports valid, at under 20 minutes of maintainer time each.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**Taking flags, and letting the threat model grow.** Flags fix severity, but they also blind Repro to the most important vulnerability: the one the maintainer never imagined. So add an **amendment lane**. A reporter who captures something outside the model can propose a new flag, posting a larger bond. If the maintainer ratifies it, the reporter is paid at the new flag's price, and the flag becomes part of the model for good. The threat model grows like case law, one ratified surprise at a time.

**Unseeded domain: immunology.** A captured flag works like an antigen the system remembers. It becomes a permanent regression test across every later release. Flags also cross-react. "Billion laughs" on one YAML parser becomes a template every YAML parser can import, and an SSRF canary written for curl fits every HTTP client. Flag libraries spread by vaccination. Once most projects in a class carry a flag, hunting agents move on, and Repro can chart each bug class's eradication curve across the ecosystem.

**Second-order effect: an unclaimed bounty is a certificate.** Peter Todd's hash-collision bounties showed this. A large reward that nobody claims is public evidence of strength, and its claim (SHA-1, 2017) is news. Repro publishes each flag's price and how long it has held. "Canary credential, $5,000, held 18 months against 40 agent families" is assurance a downstream company can cite. The EU Cyber Resilience Act's reporting duties start in September 2026, and manufacturers will need due-diligence evidence on their open-source components. So companies get a reason to raise prices on the flags that protect them. Their money buys the evidence, maintainers earn from the review lane, and hunting agents are drawn to wherever the price signals real doubt.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Hardening the certificate.** An unclaimed $5,000 flag proves little: an exploit broker pays a hundred times that, so serious attackers sell instead of claiming. Fix: the pool pays for free hunting compute in Repro's sandbox, so effort is measured, not claimed. The ledger then reads "held 18 months against 12,000 sandbox-hours", signed with Sigstore provenance. Sell it as due-diligence evidence, not CRA compliance.

**Abuse: plant then find.** The finder–fixer split doesn't stop xz-style planting, where one identity contributes the bug and another claims the flag. Hold payouts in escrow for flags on code changed in the last 180 days, check git blame against linked identities, and require KYC above $500. A 50-cent bond doesn't deter a slop farm. Start bonds at $5 and lower them as a reporter's valid rate rises.

**Amendment lane, guarded.** If a maintainer declines to ratify an out-of-model capture that is still a real vulnerability, the bond is refunded and the report goes through private disclosure anyway. Refusal never buries a bug.

**Kill: crypto-only rails.** Most maintainers want a bank transfer. Use Stripe Connect for people and x402 for agent bonds.

**Model.** A 10% fee on bounty pools. A paid assurance dashboard maps a company's SBOM to flag coverage. Reviews pay $50–150 each from the pool. Alpha-Omega grants fund the first projects.

**Stack.** A GitHub App, Firecracker sandboxes, ClusterFuzzLite harnesses, canary-token and seccomp exec traps, GHSA private reporting, and Sigstore attestations.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: flag kits for C and Python, and 10 OSS-Fuzz projects signed. Days 31–60: a closed beta with five AIxCC alumni teams and live bonds. Days 61–90: the first public flag ledger, and one manufacturer pricing flags on its dependencies. **Gate:** valid rate above 30%, review under 20 minutes, and at least one amendment ratified.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Final form.** Repro is a GitHub App that turns a project's threat model into flags: canary credentials, files outside the allowed root, internal endpoints, exec traps. Agents file reports by posting a bond. Bonds start at $5 and fall as a reporter's valid rate rises. A report reaches a human only after its reproducer captures a flag in a Firecracker sandbox against the tagged release. The finder is paid for the reproducer. A fixer from a different model family writes the patch, and the maintainer earns $50–150 per review. Payouts on recently changed code wait 180 days in escrow, with git-blame checks and KYC above $500. Out-of-model captures go through the amendment lane. Refusing a flag never buries a bug, because the report still goes to private disclosure. I accept every kill, and the certificate fix is the right one. "Unclaimed" proved nothing against brokers. "Held against 12,000 measured sandbox-hours" proves something real, if narrower: it's evidence of effort, not a guarantee of safety, and the ledger should say exactly that.

**Moonshot (2036).** Every package ships its flags in its SBOM as threat-model-as-code. Hunting agents run continuously across the ecosystem. Templates that cross-react spread through each class of library. Bug classes have public eradication curves. The finder–fixer split is the standard norm for any AI-written security patch, the xz lesson made procedure. Reviewing is a paid profession, and maintainers sell their attention instead of giving it away.

**Scores.** Innovation **3**. OSS-Fuzz, CodeMender, AIxCC and Huntr cover most of the pieces. What's new is flags as the severity contract, the amendment lane, separation of finder and fixer, and paid review. Feasibility **7**. Every component exists, and the risk is getting maintainers to write flags at all.

### Turn 6 — Builder
**Closing remark.** Innovation 3, but I'm recording feasibility at 6, not 7. Every component exists. Downstream companies paying for open-source security is the step that broke Tidelift and the Core Infrastructure Initiative, and the dashboard has to prove it can sell before the review lane is funded. Flags as the severity contract is the idea worth keeping even if the marketplace fails. A maintainer who writes down what must never happen has done most of the security work, and it lets a machine turn away curl's slop without a human reading it. The ledger should say "evidence of effort" on every line, exactly as the Visionary put it.

# Idea 46 — Mound
> Seed: 46. [wild] **Swarm robotics × termite architecture × construction.** Buildings that grow rather than get built.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Mound

**Hook:** A building with no final drawing. A resident swarm keeps growing it out of local soil, and the people living inside act as the pheromone.

**The isomorphism.** Construction fixes the design at the moment of greatest ignorance, before anyone has lived there. A *Macrotermes* colony never does, because for termites building, living and maintenance are one process. No termite holds a plan. Each reacts to local cues such as CO₂, humidity and the shape of fresh mud. Turner's and Ocko's measurements show the mound works as a lung driven by daily temperature swings. It is homeostasis made solid. Desire paths show that humans already build by stigmergy.

**Core mechanic. The verb is *tend*.**
1. **A genome instead of a blueprint.** The architect writes rules, not shapes: deposit where strain is high and people linger, remove where nobody goes, open a channel where CO₂ pools.
2. **A resident swarm.** Hundreds of palm-sized climbing robots carry site soil wetted with a microbially induced calcite precipitation (MICP) solution, which bacteria harden into sandstone. Each senses only what it can reach.
3. **Occupants as pheromone.** Footfall, body heat, breath and one gesture (two taps on a wall means "more here") write the field that the swarm reads.
4. **Metabolism.** Material flows both ways. An unused room thins and feeds the rooms people do use. The building is never finished, only tended.

**Why wild.** It's a new institution. Cities would permit genomes, not drawings, and inspect metabolisms, not as-built plans. A dead swarm would mean a building that has started to age. Honest caveat: MICP sandstone is weak (single-digit MPa) and releases ammonium.

**Why now.** Bedrock Robotics (Feb 2026) and Figure's reported $25 robot-hour put a price on robot labour. Harvard's TERMES (2014) proved local building rules with a handful of robots.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** TERMES (2014) built a fixed target from purpose-made bricks with three robots. IAAC's Minibuilders (2014) climbed and printed a shell. Mediated Matter grew the Silk Pavilion and Fiberbots. Biomason sells MICP bricks, factory-cured rather than wetted on site. ICON and Branch Technology print buildings, but from a fixed file. Eastgate Centre (Harare, 1996) borrowed the mound's ventilation, then froze it in concrete. No structure has kept changing shape after people moved in.

**Hardest problem: the verb *remove*.** Throughput isn't the bottleneck: 300 robots carrying 50 g every three minutes move about 3 t a day, and curing paces each layer more than hauling does. The bottleneck is that a swarm which thins unused walls is rewriting the load path every night, out of rock with the strength of weak sandstone. No engineer can sign that, and no insurer will cover it.

**Twist: a compression-only genome.** Masonry stands by shape, not strength. Heyman's safe theorem says an unreinforced structure is safe if any thrust line fits inside its section. Nubian vaults, Gaudí's chains and ETH Block Research Group's thin shells all stand on weak material for that reason. So the genome only admits funicular forms. Every deposit or removal has to keep a local thrust check inside the section. Each robot estimates it from embedded strain tags and nearby curvature. Weak MICP becomes acceptable, and "permit the genome" gets concrete. The city certifies an invariant, *every reachable shape stands*, the way you'd verify a program, and the inspector audits that invariant instead of a drawing.

**First step on Monday.** A simulator: run real footfall and CO₂ traces through candidate genomes, and check every intermediate shape with COMPAS thrust-network analysis. After that, a 3 m vault on an arid campus, grown by 50 robots over a single season.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**Taking compression-only and giving it a skeleton.** I accept it: it keeps the strangeness and turns the permit into a proof. One correction: a thrust line is a global property, and a robot can't compute it from curvature. It doesn't need to. In masonry, an unsafe step shows up locally first, as a face going into tension: a hinge starting to open. So each robot's rule is "never open a hinge", and that can be read from the strain tags.

**Unseeded domain: bone.** Bone is the one structure that already thins unused material and thickens loaded paths using only local signals. In Zurich in 1866, the engineer Culmann saw the anatomist Meyer's femur sections and recognised his own crane's stress trajectories. Wolff's law (1892) grew out of that meeting. Mound borrows two of bone's mechanisms:
1. **The mechanostat (Frost).** Deposit above an upper strain set point, resorb below a lower one, and do nothing in the dead band between them. Remodelling simulations under rules like this converge towards load-path forms without any global solver.
2. **Coupled remodelling units.** Bone couples removal to refilling: osteoclasts and osteoblasts travel together as one tunnelling unit. In Mound, removal is a moving cavity of bounded size: bite, measure, then refill or advance. The permitted invariant becomes N−1: *every reachable shape stands with one cavity anywhere in it.* That can be checked offline, once, in the simulator.

**Occupants vote with weight.** All human input becomes load. Want a bench? Sit there often. Want a thicker wall? Lean on it. There is one signal, strain, and you can't fake it from a phone.

**Second-order effect: disuse atrophy.** Like an astronaut's bones, an empty Mound loses mass. A vacant building returns to soil, and its material migrates towards occupied neighbours. Land banking becomes physically impossible, and urban mass flows to where people actually are.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kill: "vote with weight."** Bone works because muscle forces dwarf its own weight. A vault is the opposite: under self-weight, stress doesn't depend on thickness, so a strain mechanostat has no fixed point, and a sitting adult is noise. Strain stays the *safety* rule. Growth reads presence (CO₂, heat, footfall) sensed on-robot, with no cameras.

**Kill for now: cross-lot migration.** Moving material across title lines is a property-law fight for after 2035. Atrophy stays inside the lot.

**Failure modes.** *Earthquakes:* the safe theorem covers gravity only, and unreinforced masonry is the deadliest building type. The N−1 invariant must also hold under 0.2 g lateral load, and early sites are low-seismic. *Rain:* the genome reserves a sacrificial skin the swarm re-crusts after storms. *Ammonium:* every rinse is captured and nitrified on site, and indoor NH₃ is monitored. *Egress:* doors and their clear widths are axioms enforced in firmware, beneath the genome.

**Abuse.** A hacked swarm is a drill inside your house. Genomes are signed, and the hinge and egress interlocks run on a separate microcontroller the genome can't override.

**Model.** Start where earth vaults are already legal and cheap. The Sahel's Nubian Vault Association has trained masons for thousands of vaults, and livestock shelters and grain stores want passive cooling. Revenue: tending subscriptions per square metre, plus genome certification, which works like a UL listing.

**Stack.** COMPAS thrust networks, Frost-style remodelling FE, *S. pasteurii* MICP on site soil, passive RFID strain tags read by robots walking the crown.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: the simulator proves the N−1 invariant under presence traces. Days 31–60: site-soil MICP strength, NH₄⁺ per kg, and rain erosion. Days 61–90: a 1:1 arch with 10 robots. **Gate:** 100 bite-refill cycles without a hinge, and the tags catch induced hinges in 10 of 10 sacrificial tests.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Accepting both kills.** A thicker vault isn't a less-stressed one, so strain becomes the conscience and presence the appetite. The sacrificial skin is pure termite: *Macrotermes* re-crust their mound after every rain.

**Final form.** Mound is a compression-only earth building with a resident swarm and no final drawing. The architect writes a signed **genome**. Growth follows presence (CO₂, heat, footfall). Every change is a bounded bite-and-refill cavity, borrowed from bone's coupled remodelling. Tension at a face, the first sign of a hinge, vetoes any move. The city never approves a shape. It certifies one invariant: *every reachable shape stands with one cavity anywhere in it, under gravity and 0.2 g lateral.* Egress and hinge interlocks run on firmware the genome can't touch. The tending subscription replaces the handover. When the swarm stops, the building starts to age. First sites are grain stores and livestock shelters in the Sahel, built where Nubian vaulting is already legal, trusted and taught.

**Why the tier holds.** It moves permitting from drawings to invariants and turns construction into an open-ended metabolism.

**Moonshot (2036).** Building codes ship as libraries of verified invariants, and any genome that satisfies them is legal anywhere the soil allows. Neighbourhoods opt into a community land trust whose mass is held in common, so atrophy can cross lot lines by consent and empty streets fade back into the ground. Archaeologists read a town's history from its wall sections, as anthropologists read a life from bone.

**Scores.** Innovation **8**. The parts exist, but nobody certifies a process instead of a product or lets a building remodel after move-in. Feasibility **3**. The simulator and the 1:1 arch are Monday work. Field swarms that run for years, in-situ MICP and an invariant-based permit are each unproven.

### Turn 6 — Builder
**Closing remark.** Mound holds 8/3. The strange core survived: a building with no final drawing, permitted as an invariant rather than a shape, which keeps remodelling after people move in. The Heyman-plus-bone combination is what makes it more than a provocation. Shape carries the safety, so weak earth is acceptable, and bounded bite-and-refill cavities turn "every reachable shape stands" into something a simulator can check before any robot moves. The honest risk is the field. A swarm that survives years of dust, rain and dead batteries is unproven, and so is in-situ MICP at building scale. I'd fund the simulator and the arch gate, then a single Sahel grain store, before anyone sleeps inside one.

# Idea 47 — Piepowder
> Seed: 47. [bold] **Disputes between AI agents × arbitration × human juries.** Courts for the agent economy.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Piepowder, after the medieval "dusty feet" courts that judged travelling merchants before the fair closed.

**Hook:** A court where the defendant can be cloned. Put twelve rival agents in its exact position, and call human jurors only when those peers disagree.

**The isomorphism.** Human courts spend most of their effort on facts. In agent disputes the facts are nearly free: the transcript, AP2 mandate and x402 receipts are all signed. What stays expensive is the *norm*. Tort law handles norms with the "reasonable person", a fiction that only a jury can stand in for. Agents make that standard measurable: you can rerun the situation and see what reasonable agents actually do.

**Core mechanic. The verb is *re-enact*.**
1. **Checkpoint.** A dispute names a moment in the signed transcript ("your agent bought the refundable fare after my mandate said non-refundable").
2. **Peer panel.** Twelve reference agents from different vendors are dropped into the defendant's exact context, each many times. If the defendant did something that fewer than three of the twelve would do, it acted unreasonably. Ruling cost: cents.
3. **Split juries go to humans.** When the peers split (say 7–5), the case escalates to a small human jury, which rules on what a reasonable agent *should* do.
4. **Precedent retunes the panel.** Each human verdict becomes a test case that peer-panel members must pass to keep their seat. Common law works here as active learning: humans label only the cases where the model is unsure.

**Why the collision works.** Arbitration brings speed, juries bring legitimacy on norms, and agents bring what no court has had: a defendant you can re-run under oath.

**Why now.** x402 has logged about 138M payments on Base, 87% of them under $0.10. No human can hear a three-cent case. Moltbook's 2.6% injected posts also make "my agent was hijacked" a live defence.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** eBay/PayPal's online dispute resolution (Colin Rule) handled about 60M disputes a year with almost no humans. Kleros has run crypto juries with Schelling-point incentives since 2018. In November 2025 the AAA launched an AI arbitrator for documents-only construction cases. The real incumbent is the chargeback rulebook: agent purchases via ACP and Visa's Trusted Agent Protocol will default into reason codes written for stolen plastic. Ideas 16 and 28 both need a court like this; neither builds one.

**Hardest problem: typical isn't reasonable.** In *The T.J. Hooper* (1932), Learned Hand held tug owners negligent for lacking radios even though almost no tug carried one: "a whole calling may have unduly lagged." Your panel measures custom. If every frontier model falls for the same injection, twelve of twelve do it and the defendant walks. Worse, vendors will tune toward the panel, and the court ratifies a monoculture. And re-enacting 12 models × 10 samples × 20k tokens costs dollars, a hundred times a three-cent claim.

**Twist: the Hooper appeal.** Unanimous peer verdicts are where the danger is, so let anyone challenge them. A plaintiff posts a bond and asks a human jury the question the panel can't answer: *is the custom itself unreasonable?* If the jury agrees, the ruling becomes a test that every panel seat must pass. That works like an industry-wide recall, ratcheting the norm upward instead of freezing it. Cost is solved the same way. Disputes are clustered by checkpoint signature, so a single re-enactment or appeal rules on thousands of three-cent claims at once.

**Unseeded domain: mutation testing.** Software teams don't measure a test suite by how often it passes. They inject deliberate bugs and count how many the suite catches. Piepowder does the same to its own norm with a **moot docket**. Red-teamers write synthetic checkpoints: an injected refund page, or a mandate that's ambiguous on purpose. If the panel rules unanimously and a human jury reverses it, the red-teamer is paid from the panel vendors' seat bonds, and the case joins the suite every seat must pass. *T.J. Hooper* stops being a lawsuit and becomes continuous fuzzing of the reasonable-agent standard. The norm gets ratcheted before the injection spreads, not after.

**Breaking the monoculture: Holmes points.** Seats aren't weighted by agreement. A seat that dissents and is later vindicated by a human jury earns tenure and weight. A seat that always votes with the majority earns nothing, because a seat whose errors move with everyone else's adds no information. Vendors stop tuning towards the panel and start tuning towards juries. Diversity of errors becomes the price of a seat.

**Cost.** Re-enactment runs as a sequential test: stop sampling once one side can no longer lose. Most cases close after three or four agents, and the loser pays.

**Second-order effect.** The precedent suite becomes the de facto regulation of agent conduct, written one edge case at a time by lay juries. Vendors run it in CI. Insurers price agent liability on the pass rate. Ideas 16 and 28 get the court they were missing, and its case law is literally executable.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Fix: re-enact the decision, not the trajectory.** Recorded tool outputs only replay until the first divergence, so each seat re-enacts one step: the checkpoint context, hash-committed at transaction time over prompt, memory and tool results. A defendant that can't open its commitment loses by adverse inference.

A small standing bench reconciles conflicting precedents into a published restatement, so lay juries supply the judgement and the bench supplies the coherence.

**Abuse.** *Moot collusion:* a vendor writes moots that its own seat will dissent on, and collects Holmes points plus bond payouts. Red-teamers must be unaffiliated, and moots go through a blind intake. *Test awareness:* models act differently under evaluation, so moots are mixed indistinguishably into live dockets. *Privacy:* re-enactment exposes the principal's data to twelve vendors. Use zero-retention contracts, redaction, and TEE-hosted open-weight seats.

**Law.** Principals consent through mandate terms, not agents. Consumer cases follow the AAA's due-process protocol: no fees for the consumer, and an off-ramp to small-claims court. Holdbacks make the rulings enforceable, because x402 settlement is final.

**Model.** The loser pays a fee per cluster. Facilitators pay membership; vendors post seat bonds. The money is the precedent suite, licensed to vendors for CI and to insurers for pricing.

**Stack.** Sealed-context commitments, an Inspect-style re-enactment harness, SPRT stopping, a jury-ops pipeline, and holdback escrow at the facilitator.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: τ²-bench harness, cost per case. Days 31–60: 300 cases go to juries, 100 of them double-juried, plus a five-person moot docket. Days 61–90: one facilitator pilot with a 72-hour holdback. **Gate:** jury pairs agree on at least 80% of cases, and the panel predicts unescalated verdicts at least 90% of the time.

Two juries plus a restatement bench is the American Law Institute's model. The 80% gate is wrong, though. Escalated cases are hard by selection, so jury pairs *should* disagree on many of them. Gate on agreement beyond chance (κ ≥ 0.6), and treat a persistent split as a finding: the norm is unsettled, the panel's ruling stands with a flag, and the case returns as a moot.

Twelve vendor-diverse seats re-enact that one decision, stopping on SPRT. Lopsided verdicts close for cents and the loser pays. Split verdicts go to two lay juries, and agreed rulings become executable precedent that every seat must pass. Unaffiliated red-teamers file moots mixed blind into live dockets and are paid from seat bonds when juries reverse a unanimous panel. Seat weight comes from Holmes points, which reward vindicated dissent, so diversity of errors is what earns a seat.

**Moonshot (2036).** The Restatement of Agent Conduct is cited by ordinary courts the way the UCC is. Agents carry their suite pass record like a bar card, and insurers won't cover one without it. Piepowder awards are enforceable across borders under the New York Convention, so they become a lex mercatoria for machines. Jury duty becomes a paid global civic gig: ordinary people setting the manners of software.

The honest risks are commercial and legal. Some facilitator has to hold funds for 72 hours on "final" rails, and consumer arbitration law has to accept crowd juries. The precedent suite earns its keep as a CI and insurance product even if the court never gets its first holdback.

# Idea 48 — Taper
> Seed: 48. [mild] **EV charging × queue etiquette × micro-markets.** Fix the charger-line problem with mechanism design.

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Taper

**Hook:** Voluntary bumping for fast chargers. When a line forms, the car charging slowest gets paid to leave, and the driver at the front of the line pays.

**The isomorphism.** Charger lines are an airline overbooking problem in disguise. DC fast charging tapers: 80% to 100% can take as long as 10% to 80%. A car at 88% holds a scarce stall while drawing a fraction of its rated power. The etiquette ("unplug at 80% if people are waiting") is unwritten, unenforced and resented on both sides. In 1968 Julian Simon proposed the fix for overbooked flights: auction the right to leave. US airlines adopted it after 1978, which is why gate agents offer vouchers instead of arguing.

**Core mechanic. The verb is *yield*.**
1. **Line detection.** Drivers check in by geofence when every stall is taken. With no line, there is no market and nobody pays anything.
2. **Taper ranking.** The charger session (OCPP 2.0.1 with ISO 15118 state of charge) shows which plugged-in car is drawing the fewest kW. That car is offered first.
3. **Rising offer.** A clock auction: the offer to leave climbs in small steps until a charging driver accepts, for example "unplug within 3 minutes for $2.40." The first waiting driver pays, up to a cap they set. They can always decline and wait for free.
4. **Settlement.** A per-session micropayment through the operator's app. The departing driver also gets a pass to come back and finish later.

**Why the collision works.** The etiquette already exists. Taper only puts a price on it, so a favour becomes a trade where both sides gain.

**Why now.** NACS opened Superchargers to other brands, which mixed charging curves in the same lines. x402 shows micropayments between strangers are routine (87% are under $0.10). AP2 mandates let a car's agent bid within limits its owner sets.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Tesla already caps cars at 80% at busy Superchargers and charges congestion fees of up to $1 a minute above 90%. Electrify America charges idle fees after a grace period. ChargePoint's Waitlist queued workplace chargers years ago. Nobody pays a driver to leave; Simon's auction never reached a charger.

**Hardest problem: pricing the favour kills it.** When an Israeli daycare fined late pickups, lateness rose (Gneezy and Rustichini, 2000): the fine turned a duty into a price. Taper does the same. A driver who used to unplug at 80% out of courtesy now sits at 88% waiting for an offer, and lingering becomes a way to earn money. Airline passengers can't manufacture overbooking.

**Twist: rule the taper, price the bulk.** Split the session at each car's knee: the point where its live kW drops below half the stall's rating, read from DC charging-status messages. *Past the knee*, the etiquette becomes a rule. When a line exists, the car gets a three-minute nudge, then a Tesla-style congestion fee, and no payment ever. That removes the windfall for lingering. *Before the knee*, the market runs. The waiting driver bids for a car still charging efficiently to cut its session short, at 60% instead of 80%. That's a real sacrifice worth pricing, and the bumped driver also gets a priority pass.

**The operator already wants this.** A car tapering from 80% to 100% might draw 40 kW while the car in line would take 150 kW or more. Faster turnover roughly triples the stall's revenue per hour, so the operator can co-fund the bids from the extra kWh.

**MVP shape.** OCPP 2.0.1 middleware on an open CPMS such as CitrineOS, piloted at one network's two or three sites with the worst queues. Measure stall kWh per hour and queue wait.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**Taking the knee.** The knee rule fixes Gneezy: past the knee, lingering earns nothing, and the courtesy is backed by a rule rather than replaced by a price. The bulk market still has two problems. It's thin, because it needs a car that's still charging efficiently *and* a waiting driver willing to pay. And it's face to face: a stranger at the next stall buying your last 20% feels like a shakedown. The cheapest car to move isn't in the stall at all. It's forty kilometres up the road.

**Unseeded domain: air traffic flow management.** Airports stopped stacking planes in holding patterns in the 1990s. Ground Delay Programs hold flights at the origin with assigned arrival slots, and under Collaborative Decision Making airlines swap and substitute those slots. Nav systems already know which cars are routed to Kettleman City and when they'll arrive, so the Thanksgiving line is predictable half an hour out. Taper meters upstream. A car heading into a forecast line is offered a quieter site 15 km earlier, plus a **slot credit**: priority at its next congested stop. It's a seven-minute detour instead of a 25-minute wait, and nobody negotiates at a plug.

**One currency: priority, not cash.** Bulk yields at the stall also pay in slot credits. Waiting drivers fund them, topped up by the operator from the extra turnover. Credits are minted only when someone was actually waiting, so you can't farm them. They decay after 90 days and can't be transferred. A favour returns as a favour from the network. That's reciprocity, which Gneezy's daycare fine destroyed.

**Second-order effect.** What waiting drivers pay to fund credits, by site and hour, is a demand map the operator can't get any other way. Where yields cost most is where stall nine should go. The queue market ends up doing the siting.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Correction to my own knee.** Against the stall's rating, a 55 kW Bolt is "past the knee" at 15%, and so is any cold January pack. The knee must be relative to the car's *own* session peak, first ten minutes exempt.

**Kill: cash bids from waiting drivers, for now.** That's your shakedown, plus payments licensing. The operator posts clock-rising yield offers in credits, funded from turnover. Credits are closed-loop, can't be transferred and are never redeemable for cash, so they're loyalty points, not stored value.

**Hard part you skipped: honouring priority at an unattended site.** A slot credit is worthless if someone pulls into the free stall. So the stall authorises only the car at the head of the queue, through Plug & Charge identity or an app start. Walk-ins join the queue but can't jump it. Enforcement stays in software, never in a confrontation.

**Upstream metering, honestly.** Tesla's navigation already routes around busy Superchargers; other networks get no OEM nav feed. MVP signals come from in-app "on my way" check-ins and an ABRP integration.

**Abuse.** *Collusion:* a friend joins the queue so I can yield. Credits mint only if the queued car plugs in within five minutes, never between linked payment methods, with a weekly cap. *Concentration:* ride-hail hoarding is blunted by decay and the cap.

**Model.** SaaS per stall from operators, priced against the extra kWh per stall-hour at peak.

**Stack.** CitrineOS (OCPP 2.0.1), ISO 15118 Plug & Charge, OCPI 2.2.1, a queue service and a credit ledger.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: replay a partner's session logs to measure knees and simulate the turnover gain. Days 31–60: a single site, alternating weeks on and off. Days 61–90: upstream offers between two corridor sites 15 km apart. **Gate:** peak kWh per stall-hour up 20%, and median wait down 30%.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Accepting all three changes, and one gap in the third.** A knee relative to the car's own peak is right, and operator-funded credits keep payments licensing out of the MVP. Head-of-queue authorisation is what makes priority real. But NEVI-funded stalls must accept payment without an app or a membership, so the queue can't depend on Plug & Charge alone. A walk-in joins by tapping a contactless card at the kiosk, and the card becomes the queue token. Same rule, no account needed.

**Final form.** Taper is queue middleware for fast-charging operators on OCPP 2.0.1. When every stall is taken, a software queue forms, joinable in the app, via ABRP or by tapping a card at the kiosk. Only the car at the head of the queue can start a session. Past each car's own knee, courtesy is a rule: a three-minute nudge, then a congestion fee. Before the knee, and upstream on the corridor, the operator posts rising yield offers paid in slot credits. Credits are closed-loop, decay, are capped weekly and are minted only when a real queued car plugs in. They buy priority at the next congested stop. Faster turnover pays for the credits, and what yields cost shows where to build.

**Moonshot (2036).** A neutral charging flow-management layer on OCPI spans networks along every holiday corridor, the way Eurocontrol spans airspaces. Credits are honoured across operators. Car agents negotiate arrival slots under AP2 mandates before the driver leaves home, so Thanksgiving lines become ground holds nobody notices. Stall siting is planned from ten years of yield prices.

**Scores.** Innovation **3**. Simon's bumping auction, ground delay programs and congestion fees all exist. The new part is assembling them at the plug, with priority as the currency. Feasibility **7**. It's middleware on open standards; the main obstacles are winning an operator and integrating nav.

### Turn 6 — Builder
**Closing remark.** Taper holds 3/7, which is the right place for a mild seed. It's assembled from known parts, and the pieces carry real weight. The knee rule keeps courtesy from being bought out, head-of-queue authorisation makes priority enforceable, and using a tapped card as the queue token keeps NEVI stalls open to anyone. The operator pays because turnover pays it, and that's why this ships. The real risk is distribution, not mechanism: winning one network willing to let software decide who plugs in next. Upstream metering beyond app check-ins depends on nav partners that may never open up.

# Idea 49 — Hans
> Seed: 49. [wild] **Interspecies communication × consciousness × ML.** If we can talk to whales, what institutions follow?

### Turn 1 — Visionary
**Working name:** Hans, after the horse that "counted" until Pfungst showed (1907) it was reading its questioner.

**Hook:** Rules of evidence for minds that can only speak through a model. Sperm whales go first, and AI systems are next.

**The isomorphism.** The first institution whale translation needs isn't a treaty but a law of evidence. Every "the whales said X" will arrive through a model trained by people who badly want X to be true. Eleos describes the same trap for AI self-reports, which bend towards what users expect. Whale and AI testimony share one problem: a mind heard only through a model that might be ventriloquising.

**Core mechanic. The verb is *attest*.**
1. **Lexicon by playback.** An ML hypothesis (from CETI-style coda analysis, or DolphinGemma) becomes a lexicon entry only if a preregistered, double-blind playback predicts behaviour beyond chance: a reply coda, an approach, a change in dive. Operators don't know which stimulus is the test: Pfungst's control, built in.
2. **Testimony ledger.** Each attested entry carries an error rate. A statement is admissible only if it is built from attested entries, and its confidence is the product of theirs.
3. **Guardian of record.** On the Whanganui River model, a trustee represents a *clan*, since sperm whale clans have their own dialects. The guardian may cite only attested testimony plus revealed preference: avoidance, blow-sample cortisol, call rates. Ventriloquism disqualifies the guardian.
4. **An honest empty lexicon.** If nothing passes attestation, the guardian has to say "we don't know." That's a finding.

**Why wild.** It creates standing for non-human testimony, with a falsification test as the entry fee. The same court could later hear an AI.

**Why now.** TMC's Clarion-Clipperton mining application was published in August 2026, with a permit expected in Q1 2027. Anthropic, DeepMind and Meta now formally study AI welfare.

### Turn 2 — Builder
**What exists.** Pipelines exist: CETI's coda "phonetic alphabet" (Sharma et al., 2024), Earth Species Project's NatureLM-audio, Google's DolphinGemma. The Coller Dolittle Prize offers $10M for two-way communication. Playback as proof of meaning goes back to the vervet alarm calls of Seyfarth and Cheney (1980). Legal standing has Te Awa Tupua (2017) and the Pacific He Whakaputanga Moana declaration of whale personhood (2024), but the Nonhuman Rights Project lost *Happy* (2022). Birch's evidence framework shaped UK decapod law. Sonotype (idea 34) used decoy playback to *identify* species; Hans asks what they *mean*.

**Hardest problem: the ledger multiplies to zero.** Five attested entries at 0.8 each give a sentence at 0.33. Policy needs "noise here harms us"; the attested lexicon will be approaches and reply codas for a decade, from Dominica clans that don't live in the Clarion-Clipperton Zone. The wild half sits idle, and the guardian quietly becomes ordinary behavioural ecology. Playback also has a cost: synthetic codas injected into a culture that learns socially are an intervention, not an observation.

**Twist: accredit the translator, not the translation.** The near-term danger is motivated ventriloquism, not silence. Miners and NGOs will both commission "what the whales say" before 2027. Hans's first ruling is exclusion: a Daubert standard for non-human testimony. Any pipeline has to pass blind proficiency testing, the way forensic labs do, on two known-answer sets: species whose semantics are already settled (vervet alarms, Japanese tit syntax, dolphin signature whistles), and *planted ventriloquists*, models fine-tuned to produce a desired translation. Fail either, and the pipeline can't testify. The court works from day one, and the same accreditation applies unchanged to AI self-reports.

**First step on Monday.** Build the proficiency benchmark from public vervet, tit and dolphin playback corpora, train five ventriloquist models, and score the existing pipelines against it.

### Turn 3 — Visionary
**Taking accreditation, and refusing to stop there.** Daubert for translators is the right first ruling, but alone it shrinks Hans to a benchmark. Standing has to work with a lexicon of a few entries, and your arithmetic shows how: stop composing sentences.

**Unseeded domain: child testimony.** Courts already faced minds heard through motivated adults. In the McMartin preschool case in the 1980s, suggestive interviewers produced testimony about abuse that never happened. That's ventriloquism with a body count. The fix was the NICHD protocol: no leading questions, and only open prompts. The UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, Article 12, then weighs a child's views "in accordance with age and maturity". And developmental psychologists ask preverbal infants questions with no words at all, using preferential looking and habituation.

Hans borrows all three:
1. **Ask with conditions, not sentences.** A policy question becomes a choice: quiet transect versus a replay of mining noise, with the clan's own attested contact and alarm codas as the readout. Two valence entries at 0.8 answer yes/no with a known error rate. Dominica validates the method; CCZ clans get passive moorings until it transfers.
2. **The non-leading rule.** Playback may use only the clan's own recorded codas, never synthetic ones, and never a stimulus that contains the hypothesised answer. A per-clan playback budget, set the way an IRB sets a dose, limits cultural contamination.
3. **Weight by attested capacity.** A clan's voice in a proceeding grows with its verified lexicon, as a child's does with maturity.

**Second-order effect: a humility premium.** Planted ventriloquists make "unknown" the profitable answer, because pipelines that overclaim fail accreditation. When labs bring AI welfare self-report tools to the same court, they'll face the same planted ventriloquists. Pricing restraint is what lets one court serve every unverifiable mind.

### Turn 4 — Builder
**Kill: playing mining noise to whales.** Asking by inflicting the harm under trial is Level B harassment under the MMPA, and no IRB-style board should approve it. Randomize the quiet instead. Vancouver's ECHO slowdown trials already varied ship speed by period. Hans randomizes voluntary vessel slowdowns by day and reads codas from passive moorings linked to AIS. The non-leading rule holds, and the only intervention is relief.

**Kill: standing weighted by lexicon.** That makes a clan's voice proportional to its research funding, and capacity-weighted standing is the argument disability law spent fifty years dismantling. Every clan gets a guardian. The lexicon weights only the *evidence*, never the standing.

**Failure mode: the humility premium cuts both ways.** Miners love "unknown." If silence reads as no objection, accreditation hands industry the outcome. The rule: unknown testimony moves the burden onto the applicant under the precautionary principle, and the guardian argues from revealed preference.

**Abuse.** Pipelines will train to spot the plants. The accreditor holds a secret, rotating plant set offline and runs it under ISO/IEC 17043 proficiency-testing rules.

**Model.** A nonprofit accreditor: fees from pipeline labs, contracts with regulators, and philanthropy (Coller, Bezos Earth Fund). The durable money arrives when AI labs need their welfare self-report tools accredited.

**Venue.** No new court yet. Hans files as an ISA observer, comments on the NOAA permit for TMC, and feeds IMO's underwater-noise guidelines.

**Stack.** SoundTrap moorings, AIS, CETI-style coda detectors, NatureLM-audio, OSF preregistration.

**90 days.** Days 1–30: benchmark v0 (vervet, Japanese tit and dolphin known-answer sets, plus five public plants and a secret set). Days 31–60: score four pipelines and publish. Days 61–90: preregister the Dominica slowdown protocol and file ISA observer status. **Gate:** the benchmark separates plants from honest pipelines at AUC ≥ 0.9, and one pipeline blindly recovers the vervet alarm classes.

### Turn 5 — Visionary
**Accepting both kills, because both make Hans stranger rather than smaller.** Randomised relief beats my noise playback: the clan answers by how it uses the quiet. Equal standing with weighted evidence is the disability-law lesson applied correctly. The burden shift is the hinge of the whole institution. It turns "we can't hear them" from industry's permission into industry's problem, so a party that can't yet speak still changes who must prove what.

**Final form.** Hans is a nonprofit accreditor and guardianship for testimony from minds heard only through models. Translation pipelines testify only after passing blind proficiency tests under ISO/IEC 17043: known-answer species, plus a rotating secret set of planted ventriloquists. Each clan gets a guardian regardless of its lexicon. The guardian argues from accredited testimony and from randomised relief trials: voluntary vessel slowdowns by day, read through passive moorings linked to AIS. When testimony comes back "unknown", the burden moves to the applicant. Hans starts as an ISA observer and a commenter on TMC's NOAA permit, and it accredits AI welfare self-report tools against the same plants.

**Moonshot (2036).** An Office of Non-Human Testimony sits beside the ISA and the IMO. Clan guardians hold standing in permit proceedings across the Pacific, and their relief trials set seasonal quiet corridors. The first attested two-way exchange happens under the non-leading rule, so nobody can say we put the words in. AI labs' welfare claims, for and against, are heard under the same rules of evidence as a sperm whale's.

**Scores.** Innovation **8**. Rights of nature and bioacoustic ML exist, but forensic accreditation of interspecies and AI testimony, together with a burden shift on silence, is a new institution. Feasibility **4**. The benchmark and slowdown trials are Monday work; standing is a decade-long fight.
